# Science and Technology Flashcards

> One flashcard per examinable item in the repository Science and Technology owners (basic + advanced), plus a concept card per owner file. IDs: SNT-1001 onward by agent block.
> **Verification status:** built from the repository owners with claims checked against Wikipedia and other pages that loaded while building; confirmed items carry a ✅ source tag; unverified details are marked ⚠️. Owner-vs-source conflicts are flagged inside the cards. Time-sensitive facts carry as-of dates from the owner.

---

## SCIENCE AND TECHNOLOGY — Part 1: Space Programme, Satellites, Human Spaceflight, Nuclear Power and Fusion

### GROUP — Space Programme, ISRO, Launch Vehicles and Space Governance

### Flashcard SNT-1001 — Access to space as a system, not a rocket
**Front:** Why is "access to space" a system rather than a vehicle? Name the four variables that decide whether a launcher programme becomes strategic and commercial capability.
**Definition (learn):** Access to space depends on propulsion class, orbit reach, throughput and institutional clarity, not on the rocket alone.
**Breakdown:**
- (i) Propulsion class: solid/earth-storable liquid -> cryogenic -> semi-cryogenic -> reusable.
- (ii) Orbit reach: LEO/SSO vs GTO vs escape trajectories.
- (iii) Throughput: pads, integration bays, stage manufacturing, range slots.
- (iv) Institutional clarity: who researches, who authorises, who sells.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** India solved (i) and (ii) first, invested in (iii), and used Indian Space Policy 2023 to settle (iv) (⚠️ analytical frame, owner 01 Advanced §1).
**Links:** SNT-1002, SNT-1003, SNT-1004, SNT-1005, SNT-1007, SNT-1013, SNT-1018.
**Trap:** A Cabinet approval for a future vehicle or pad is not present capability; judge every "space power" claim against all four variables.
**Mains hook:** Argue that launch capability is an ecosystem problem (pads, manufacturing, regulation, cadence) and that propulsion alone does not make a space power. Conclude with the need for reliability to be re-earned every flight.
**Back:** *"Propulsion, Orbit, Throughput, Institutions — four legs, not one rocket."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1002 — Department of Space (DoS)
**Front:** What is the Department of Space and what role does it play in India's space architecture?
**Definition (learn):** The Union-government umbrella for India's civilian space programme and its institutional architecture.
**Breakdown:**
- Provides the policy umbrella, budgetary direction and governmental backing.
- Sits above ISRO, IN-SPACe and NSIL.
- Keeps space as a sovereign strategic capability domain even after reforms.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 (Basic §2, Advanced §5). Indian Space Policy 2023 formally delineates DoS, ISRO, IN-SPACe and NSIL roles.
**Links:** SNT-1003, SNT-1004, SNT-1005, SNT-1019 (Indian Space Policy 2023).
**Trap:** DoS is the policy and institutional umbrella, not the launch agency; do not attribute launches to DoS directly.
**Mains hook:** Use DoS as the sovereign anchor when arguing that reform separated functions rather than withdrew the state.
**Back:** *"DoS is the roof; ISRO, IN-SPACe and NSIL live under it."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1003 — ISRO
**Front:** What is ISRO's role, and which centres does the owner name?
**Definition (learn):** India's public space agency responsible for launch vehicles, spacecraft, applications and flagship missions.
**Breakdown:**
- Strategic R&D anchor and technology owner for launch-vehicle families and major missions.
- Named centres: VSSC, LPSC/IPRC and SDSC-SHAR.
- Remains the sovereign anchor for rockets, which are dual-use and high-consequence systems.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 (Basic §4; Advanced §5).
**Links:** SNT-1002, SNT-1004 (IN-SPACe), SNT-1005 (NSIL), SNT-1018 (Third Launch Pad).
**Trap:** Reform did not make ISRO a regulator or a commercial seller; it keeps research and mission assurance.
**Mains hook:** Reform widened the ecosystem but ISRO still carries strategic capability and quality assurance.
**Back:** *"ISRO builds; it does not police private launches or sell them."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1004 — IN-SPACe
**Front:** What is IN-SPACe and what does it do for non-government space activity?
**Definition (learn):** A single-window nodal agency in DoS that promotes, authorises and supervises space activities of non-government entities.
**Breakdown:**
- Autonomous agency under DoS (owner: "autonomous single-window nodal agency").
- Authorises, promotes and supervises private participation, including facility access and technology-transfer pathways.
- Interface for private players, not a manufacturer or a commercial seller.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01. Indian Space Policy 2023 (10 Aug 2023) defines its role.
**Links:** SNT-1005 (NSIL), SNT-1006 (trap pair), SNT-1027 (Vikram-I), SNT-1028 (re-entry norms).
**Trap:** IN-SPACe is not the commercial arm; NSIL is. IN-SPACe is also not India's comprehensive space law.
**Mains hook:** Authorisation is necessary because launch carries liability and debris risk; IN-SPACe is the State's supervisory interface.
**Back:** *"IN-SPACe permits, NSIL sells."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1005 — NSIL
**Front:** What is NSIL and what services does it offer?
**Definition (learn):** The public-sector commercial arm offering launch, satellite and technology-related services using Indian space assets.
**Breakdown:**
- Launch services and satellite-building services.
- Satellite-based services and technology transfer / commercial production models.
- Converts mature public capability into market-facing services.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 (Basic §4; Advanced §5). NSIL signed the SSLV technology-transfer route to HAL (see SNT-1024).
**Links:** SNT-1004, SNT-1006, SNT-1024.
**Trap:** NSIL does not regulate private players; regulation and authorisation belong to IN-SPACe.
**Mains hook:** Commercialisation through NSIL allows Indian industry into production without displacing ISRO's public-science role.
**Back:** *"NSIL is the cash counter, not the referee."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1006 — Institutional split: ISRO vs IN-SPACe vs NSIL
**Front:** Match ISRO, IN-SPACe and NSIL with their principal roles. Which one is the "trap" pair candidate?
**Definition (learn):** Three distinct functions under DoS: research and missions (ISRO), authorisation and supervision (IN-SPACe), commercial services (NSIL).
**Breakdown:**
- ISRO -> R&D, launch systems, flagship missions.
- IN-SPACe -> authorise, promote, supervise non-government activity.
- NSIL -> commercial launch, satellite and technology services.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mnemonic R-A-C: ISRO Researches, IN-SPACe Authorises, NSIL Commercialises; DoS sits above all three (✅ owner 01 Basic §6).
**Links:** SNT-1002 to SNT-1005.
**Trap:** The three names are not synonyms; "NSIL authorises private launches" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use separation of function to argue reform is institutional differentiation rather than simple privatisation.
**Back:** *"R-A-C: Researches, Authorises, Commercialises."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1007 — Indian Space Policy 2023
**Front:** What is the Indian Space Policy 2023, and is it a space statute?
**Definition (learn):** An executive policy framework approved and released on 10 Aug 2023 that separates state roles in research, regulation/promotion and commercialisation while opening room for private players.
**Breakdown:**
- Clarifies roles of DoS, ISRO, IN-SPACe and NSIL.
- Opens the value chain to non-government entities.
- Does not withdraw the state from space.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Approved and published 10 Aug 2023 (📰 owner 01 §8; PIB reference in owner grounding).
**Links:** SNT-1004, SNT-1006, SNT-1008 (OST), SNT-1009 (Liability Convention).
**Trap:** It is a policy, not an Act. India has no comprehensive domestic space statute; the owner flags this as the unfinished legal question.
**Mains hook:** Policy-based authorisation leaves open liability apportionment, insurance floors and appeal routes; a domestic space law is the next step. (⚠️ analytical inference)
**Back:** *"Policy, not Act: roles split, state still leads."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1008 — Outer Space Treaty, 1967
**Front:** What does the Outer Space Treaty (1967) require of States regarding non-government space activity?
**Definition (learn):** Foundational treaty under which outer space is free for use by all, not subject to national appropriation, and used for peaceful purposes.
**Breakdown:**
- States bear international responsibility for national activities, including those of non-government entities.
- Non-government activity needs authorisation and continuing supervision by the State (Art. VI, as cited by owner 01 Advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ treaty content from owner 01 (not fetched); year 1967 as stated by owner.
**Links:** SNT-1007, SNT-1009 (Liability Convention), SNT-1010 (Registration Convention).
**Trap:** The OST does not create a domestic licensing regime; India's obligations flow from it, but the authorisation regime is executive policy.
**Mains hook:** India's State authorisation of private launch flows from OST obligations; the missing piece is a statute apportioning liability.
**Back:** *"OST: space is for all, States answer for their nationals."* (⚠️ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1009 — Liability Convention, 1972
**Front:** Under the Liability Convention (1972), who bears liability for damage caused by a space object on Earth's surface or to aircraft in flight?
**Definition (learn):** The launching State is absolutely liable for such damage.
**Breakdown:**
- "Launching State" bears absolute liability for surface damage and aircraft damage.
- This is why private launch needs State authorisation, indemnity and insurance chains.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1972 year and absolute-liability rule per owner 01 (⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** SNT-1007, SNT-1008, SNT-1010.
**Trap:** Absolute liability is for surface damage/aircraft in flight; it is a State-to-victim rule, not an operator-to-State rule as used in the CLND Act (see SNT-1106).
**Mains hook:** The State's absolute liability explains why an authorising regime must control private launch risk.
**Back:** *"Launching State pays for falling junk."* (⚠️ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1010 — Registration Convention, 1975
**Front:** What does the Registration Convention (1975) require for objects launched into outer space?
**Definition (learn):** Launched objects must be entered in a national register and notified to the UN Secretary-General.
**Breakdown:**
- National register entry.
- Notification to the UN Secretary-General.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1975 per owner 01 (⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** SNT-1008, SNT-1009, SNT-1007.
**Trap:** Registration is an information obligation, not a liability rule and not a licence.
**Mains hook:** Registration plus authorisation makes national space activity traceable to a responsible State.
**Back:** *"Register it, then tell the UN."* (⚠️ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1011 — SLV-3 (India's first experimental SLV)
**Front:** What was SLV-3 and where does it sit in India's launch-vehicle lineage?
**Definition (learn):** India's first experimental satellite launch vehicle, the start of the SLV -> ASLV -> PSLV technology ladder.
**Breakdown:**
- Lineage: SLV-3 -> ASLV -> PSLV -> GSLV -> LVM3.
- Now historical, not operational.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 (Basic §6). Launch year not stated in the owner; not added here.
**Links:** SNT-1012 (ASLV), SNT-1013 (PSLV), SNT-1014 (GSLV), SNT-1015 (LVM3).
**Trap:** SLV-3 and ASLV are not part of the operational fleet; ISRO's active operational list is PSLV, GSLV and LVM3.
**Mains hook:** Lineage shows learning from basic orbital access to cryogenic and heavy-lift competence.
**Back:** *"SLV-3: first step, not frontline."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1012 — ASLV (intermediate learning step)
**Front:** What was ASLV's role in the Indian launch-vehicle sequence?
**Definition (learn):** ASLV (expansion not stated in owner; ⚠️): an intermediate technology-learning step before PSLV.
**Breakdown:**
- Between SLV-3 and PSLV in the lineage.
- Historical; not an active operational vehicle.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 (Basic §6). Year not recorded in owner.
**Links:** SNT-1011, SNT-1013.
**Trap:** ASLV is not the same as SSLV; SSLV is a separate small-satellite launcher concept.
**Mains hook:** Failed or partial steps (ASLV-era learning) feed later reliability; use as an example of incremental capability-building.
**Back:** *"ASLV: the bridge step before PSLV."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1013 — PSLV (Polar Satellite Launch Vehicle)
**Front:** What is PSLV, what is its mission role, and what recent results does the owner record?
**Definition (learn):** India's versatile multi-mission workhorse launcher, with multiple variants and a strong record (ISRO's own description).
**Breakdown:**
- Use cases: Earth observation, navigation, mixed multi-payload launches.
- Exam value lies in versatility across orbit profiles, not only the word "polar".
- Second stage uses earth-storable liquid propellant (UDMH/UH25 with N₂O₄; ⚠️ owner class).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PSLV-C61/EOS-09 failed on 18 May 2025 after a third-stage observation; PSLV-C62 (Jan 2026) saw an end-of-PS3 anomaly under expert review (📰 owner 01).
**Links:** SNT-1021 (propulsion classes), SNT-1026 (2025-26 reliability balance), SNT-1014, SNT-1015.
**Trap:** PSLV does not differ from GSLV/LVM3 only by payload size; cryogenic capability and orbit architecture also differ.
**Mains hook:** Reliability is an engineering variable that must be re-earned each flight; it governs insurance and customer confidence.
**Back:** *"PSLV: the workhorse that must prove itself every flight."* (✅ owner 01; ⚠️ anomaly status time-varying)

### Flashcard SNT-1014 — GSLV (cryogenic upper stage)
**Front:** What distinguishes GSLV, and what does it deliver for a communication satellite?
**Definition (learn):** Geosynchronous Satellite Launch Vehicle, with an indigenous cryogenic upper stage for heavier communication missions.
**Breakdown:**
- Key distinction: cryogenic upper stage (LOX + liquid hydrogen).
- Delivers communication satellites to GTO (geosynchronous transfer orbit), not directly to GEO.
- Recent flights: GSLV-F16/NISAR succeeded on 30 Jul 2025; GSLV-F17/EOS-05 reported successful with placement into intended Sub-GTO on 4 Sep 2026.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 (Basic §5, §8); 📰 flight dates from owner.
**Links:** SNT-1020 (orbit vocabulary), SNT-1021 (propulsion), SNT-1022 (cryogenic trap), SNT-1015 (LVM3).
**Trap:** "GSLV places satellites in geostationary orbit" is loose; it injects into GTO and the satellite raises itself.
**Mains hook:** The cryogenic stage is the technology bridge to heavier communication payloads and to LVM3's upper stages.
**Back:** *"GSLV: cryogenic lift to GTO; the satellite finishes the climb."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1015 — LVM3 (heavy lift and human-rated base)
**Front:** What is LVM3, why is it significant for Gaganyaan, and what recent flight does the owner record?
**Definition (learn):** Heavier launch vehicle (earlier designated GSLV Mk III), identified as the base launcher for Gaganyaan after human rating.
**Breakdown:**
- Heavier lift than GSLV; heavy LEO/GTO missions.
- Human-rated derivative (HLVM3) for Gaganyaan.
- Stage propulsion: solid boosters, L110 earth-storable core and a cryogenic upper stage (⚠️ owner class).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** LVM3-M5/CMS-03 succeeded on 2 Nov 2025 (📰 owner 01). Stage-counting trap: 2018 PYQ asked about GSLV Mk III stage count (owner 01 PYQ block).
**Links:** SNT-1014, SNT-1023 (human-rating), SNT-1052 (Gaganyaan overview).
**Trap:** LVM3 is not the same as GSLV; the older name GSLV Mk III is a naming trap.
**Mains hook:** A heavy-lift, human-rated vehicle is the strategic prerequisite for crewed access, not a sign of crewed capability by itself.
**Back:** *"LVM3: heavy lift, then human-rated for Gaganyaan."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1016 — SSLV (Small Satellite Launch Vehicle)
**Front:** What is SSLV for, and how is it different from a renamed PSLV?
**Definition (learn):** A small-satellite launch vehicle designed for low-cost, quick-turnaround launch of smaller payloads.
**Breakdown:**
- On-demand small-payload market; low infrastructure needs.
- Separate launcher concept with its own market logic, not a PSLV variant.
- Developmental flights completed; handed over to NSIL and industry for production and launch (DoS reply, 1 Apr 2026, owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Technology transfer to HAL signed 10 Sep 2025 (SNT-1024). Kulasekarapattinam SSLV complex under construction (SNT-1019).
**Links:** SNT-1019, SNT-1024, SNT-1013.
**Trap:** "SSLV is simply a renamed PSLV variant" is wrong; SSLV has different market and infrastructure logic.
**Mains hook:** SSLV is the test case of industrial handover: the binding risk moves to industrial quality assurance.
**Back:** *"SSLV: small, quick, sold to industry."* (✅ owner 01; ⚠️ post-handover flights time-varying)

### Flashcard SNT-1017 — NGLV (Next Generation Launch Vehicle)
**Front:** What is NGLV and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** A Cabinet-approved future three-stage launcher with a recoverable/reusable first stage, intended for much heavier payloads than the present fleet.
**Breakdown:**
- Approved 18 Sep 2024 with an outlay of ₹8,240 crore.
- Partially reusable configuration, about 30 tonnes to LEO class (stated capability).
- Configuration reply 27 Mar 2025 (PIB reference in owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Approved; under development; nothing has flown (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** SNT-1018 (Third Launch Pad), SNT-1026 (reliability), SNT-1002.
**Trap:** Describing NGLV as operational. It is an approved future vehicle; reusability pays only at high cadence.
**Mains hook:** Reusability lowers marginal cost only if refurbishment is cheap and cadence is high; otherwise it raises cost. (⚠️ analytical)
**Back:** *"NGLV: approved, reusable, not yet flown."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1018 — Third Launch Pad, Sriharikota
**Front:** What is the Third Launch Pad, when was it approved, and what is its cost and timeline?
**Definition (learn):** A launch pad at Sriharikota intended to support NGLV and to act as standby for the Second Launch Pad.
**Breakdown:**
- Cabinet approval 16 Jan 2025.
- Cost ₹3,984.86 crore; realisation target 48 months.
- Infrastructure capacity, not present operational capability.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Approved 16 Jan 2025; not commissioned (✅ owner 01; PIB reference in owner).
**Links:** SNT-1017 (NGLV), SNT-1019 (Kulasekarapattinam), SNT-1003 (SDSC-SHAR).
**Trap:** Approved does not mean commissioned; a new pad alone does not mean commercial dominance.
**Mains hook:** Launch capacity is an ecosystem of pads, integration, logistics and safety; infrastructure is a constraint, not a symptom.
**Back:** *"Third pad: approved for NGLV, 48 months, not yet built."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1019 — Kulasekarapattinam SSLV launch complex
**Front:** What is the Kulasekarapattinam complex and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** A future spaceport and SSLV launch complex supporting small-satellite and polar-orbit ambitions.
**Breakdown:**
- Infrastructure expansion for small-satellite launch and regional industrialisation.
- Construction progressing across 32 of 33 major facilities (ISRO report, 27 Aug 2025; owner 01).
- Launch-pad foundation milestone recorded.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Under development, not operational (✅ owner 01 Advanced §9).
**Links:** SNT-1016 (SSLV), SNT-1018 (Third Launch Pad).
**Trap:** A foundation or construction milestone is not an operational launch site.
**Mains hook:** Regional spaceports can diversify the launch market, but only once they are operational and staffed.
**Back:** *"Kulasekarapattinam: building, not flying."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1020 — Orbit vocabulary: LEO, SSO, GTO, GEO
**Front:** Distinguish LEO, SSO/polar, GTO and GEO/GSO with the typical payload for each.
**Definition (learn):** Orbits are destinations; a launcher's job is defined by the destination.
**Breakdown:**
- LEO: a few hundred km; short period, low energy; remote sensing, crewed missions, small-satellite constellations.
- SSO/polar: near-polar LEO crossing a latitude at the same local solar time; consistent imaging illumination.
- GTO: highly elliptical intermediate orbit; launcher stops here; communication satellites then raise themselves.
- GEO/GSO: circular, about 35,786 km above the equator, period of one sidereal day; appears fixed.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** GEO altitude ~35,786 km (owner 01 table; ⚠️ standard value, not fetched).
**Links:** SNT-1014 (GSLV), SNT-1015 (LVM3), SNT-1033 (NavIC GEO/IGSO).
**Trap:** "GSLV puts satellites in GEO" is loose; it puts them in GTO.
**Mains hook:** Use destination logic when judging a launcher's suitability for a mission rather than rocket size.
**Back:** *"LEO low, SSO sun-synced, GTO stop, GEO fixed."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1021 — Propulsion classes for launch vehicles
**Front:** Name the four propulsion classes in the owner's launch-vehicle vocabulary and where each is used.
**Definition (learn):** Propulsion class determines throttling, storability and specific impulse.
**Breakdown:**
- Solid: composite propellant; cannot be throttled or shut down at will; PSLV/GSLV/LVM3 boosters and first stages, SSLV stages.
- Earth-storable liquid (UDMH/UH25 with N₂O₄): PSLV second stage, LVM3 L110 core.
- Cryogenic (LOX + liquid hydrogen): highest specific impulse; GSLV and LVM3 upper stages.
- Semi-cryogenic (LOX + refined kerosene): under development (SE2000/SC120) as an LVM3 payload-enhancement upgrade.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ stage assignments from owner 01 table (propulsion vocabulary).
**Links:** SNT-1022 (cryogenic vs semi-cryogenic trap), SNT-1013, SNT-1014, SNT-1015.
**Trap:** Semi-cryogenic is not flying; it is an upgrade under development.
**Mains hook:** Propulsion class determines reusability and throttling options, which shapes launch economics.
**Back:** *"Solid, storable, cryo, semi-cryo: burn, store, chill, kerosene."* (⚠️ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1022 — Cryogenic vs semi-cryogenic (trap pair)
**Front:** How do cryogenic and semi-cryogenic propulsion differ, and which is flying in India?
**Definition (learn):** Cryogenic = LOX + liquid hydrogen; semi-cryogenic = LOX + refined kerosene.
**Breakdown:**
- Cryogenic: flying on GSLV and LVM3 upper stages; highest specific impulse.
- Semi-cryogenic: denser and less cryogenically demanding; more thrust per stage volume; in development (SE2000/SC120).
- ISRO semi-cryogenic breakthrough noted in owner advanced grounding (Mar 2025).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 Basic §7. Semi-cryogenic: not yet a flying stage per owner.
**Links:** SNT-1021, SNT-1014, SNT-1015.
**Trap:** "Cryogenic and semi-cryogenic are the same" is wrong; the fuel pair differs (hydrogen vs kerosene).
**Mains hook:** Semi-cryogenic is a cost-and-complexity strategy for future LVM3 payload capacity, not a current capability.
**Back:** *"Cryo = hydrogen, semi = kerosene; only cryo flies in GSLV/LVM3."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1023 — Human-rating (process)
**Front:** What is human-rating of a launch vehicle?
**Definition (learn):** Re-qualification of a launcher and its systems to crew-survival standards, adding margins, redundancy, health monitoring and abort capability.
**Breakdown:**
- A process, not a new rocket.
- Requires validated abort modes at every flight phase.
- A launcher's success record alone does not make it human-rated (owner 03 Advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 Essential definitions; ⚠️ details.
**Links:** SNT-1015 (HLVM3), SNT-1057 (Crew Escape System), SNT-1052 (Gaganyaan).
**Trap:** Consecutive successes do not imply human-rating.
**Mains hook:** Human-rating is a safety culture requirement; schedule caution is structural, not hesitation.
**Back:** *"Human-rated = margins, redundancy, abort."* (✅ owner 01 / ⚠️ details)

### Flashcard SNT-1024 — SSLV technology transfer to HAL
**Front:** What was the SSLV technology-transfer agreement, and why is it the concrete test of reform?
**Definition (learn):** ISRO signed an SSLV technology-transfer agreement with Hindustan Aeronautics Limited (HAL) on 10 Sep 2025.
**Breakdown:**
- Value ₹511 crore; ten-year term.
- First full transfer of an Indian launch vehicle to industry.
- HAL is to build and market SSLVs.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 Sep 2025 (📰 owner 01). Industry-built SSLV yet to fly as of owner source date.
**Links:** SNT-1005 (NSIL), SNT-1016 (SSLV), SNT-1004 (IN-SPACe).
**Trap:** Agreement is not an operational industry vehicle; "privatisation" is the wrong word.
**Mains hook:** Transferring build responsibility shifts the risk to quality assurance, where launcher programmes usually fail (⚠️ analytical).
**Back:** *"SSLV to HAL: build transfer, not privatisation."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1025 — Space-sector FDI (2024 amendment)
**Front:** State the automatic-route FDI limits for the space sector after the 2024 amendment.
**Definition (learn):** Foreign investment rules that open capital while keeping launch-vehicle control tighter than downstream segments.
**Breakdown:**
- Up to 74% automatic: satellite manufacturing/operation, satellite data products, ground/user segment.
- Up to 49% automatic: launch vehicles, spaceports and associated systems.
- Up to 100% automatic: components and sub-systems for satellites/ground/user segment.
- Beyond automatic limits: government approval.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 Basic §6 (amended 2024; no date given beyond 2024).
**Links:** SNT-1016, SNT-1018, SNT-1004.
**Trap:** 49% for launch vehicles is not 74%; keep the three tiers separate.
**Mains hook:** Access to space is treated as more sensitive than use of space; liberalisation stops short of launch control.
**Back:** *"74 satellites, 49 launch, 100 components."* (✅ owner 01)

### Flashcard SNT-1026 — Launch record 2025-26: a balanced reliability reading
**Front:** What do the 2025-26 launch results show about reliability, and how should an answer use them?
**Definition (learn):** A balanced reading: heavy-lift successes alongside PSLV anomalies, so reliability is a live variable.
**Breakdown:**
- Successes: GSLV-F16/NISAR (30 Jul 2025); LVM3-M5/CMS-03 (2 Nov 2025).
- Failure: PSLV-C61/EOS-09 (18 May 2025) after a third-stage observation.
- Anomaly: PSLV-C62 (Jan 2026), end-of-PS3 anomaly under expert review.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 📰 owner 01 §8 and Advanced §9.
**Links:** SNT-1013 (PSLV), SNT-1014 (GSLV), SNT-1015 (LVM3), SNT-1028 (re-entry norms).
**Trap:** Do not present the record as triumphal; do not generalise one anomaly to all flights.
**Mains hook:** Reliability must be re-earned every flight; it directly governs insurance costs and customer confidence.
**Back:** *"Two heavy wins, two PSLV stumbles: re-earn every flight."* (📰 owner 01; ⚠️ review pending)

### Flashcard SNT-1027 — Vikram-I: first private orbital launch from Indian soil
**Front:** What was Vikram-I and what does it prove (and not prove)?
**Definition (learn):** Skyroot's maiden orbital mission on 18 Jul 2026, placing SCOPE and Grahaa into LEO after IN-SPACe clearances and ISRO facility support.
**Breakdown:**
- First private Indian-company orbital launch from Indian soil (owner).
- RAMAN-I liquid upper stage tested at LPSC.
- Clearances from IN-SPACe.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 18 Jul 2026 (📰 owner 01; single-source, not fetched). Status: one successful maiden orbital mission.
**Links:** SNT-1004 (IN-SPACe), SNT-1025 (FDI), SNT-1026.
**Trap:** One successful mission is not a repeat-service record.
**Mains hook:** Private success needs cadence, insurance confidence and regulatory predictability to become market evidence.
**Back:** *"Vikram-I: one flight proves a door, not a market."* (⚠️ single-source, time-sensitive)

### Flashcard SNT-1028 — IN-SPACe planned re-entry casualty norm
**Front:** What casualty-risk threshold do IN-SPACe planned re-entry norms set (Jul 2026)?
**Definition (learn):** The applicant's risk assessment must keep expected casualty risk below 1 in 10,000.
**Breakdown:**
- Applicant bears specified third-party and indemnity obligations.
- Insurance prescribed at authorisation must be complied with.
- Activity-specific norms, not a comprehensive space statute.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jul 2026 (📰 owner 01; ⚠️ single-source).
**Links:** SNT-1004 (IN-SPACe), SNT-1007 (policy), SNT-1009 (Liability Convention).
**Trap:** Such norms are not a space law; they are authorisation rules.
**Mains hook:** Debris and re-entry risk are why authorisation and liability must be linked in a private-launch regime.
**Back:** *"1 in 10,000 casualty ceiling, insured re-entry."* (⚠️ owner 01)

### GROUP — Satellites, NavIC, GAGAN and Applications

### Flashcard SNT-1029 — Layered satellite service architecture
**Front:** What are the layers of India's satellite ecosystem, and why must a constellation, an augmentation system and an application never be treated as the same capability?
**Definition (learn):** India's satellites form a layered architecture: Earth observation, communication, meteorology, independent regional navigation (PNT) and GPS augmentation.
**Breakdown:**
- Remote sensing / EO: Resourcesat, Cartosat (IRS family).
- Communication: INSAT, GSAT.
- Meteorology: INSAT-3D, INSAT-3DR, INSAT-3DS.
- Navigation constellation: NavIC / IRNSS. Augmentation: GAGAN (SBAS).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 (Basic §1; §12 thesis).
**Links:** SNT-1030, SNT-1031, SNT-1032, SNT-1033, SNT-1038, SNT-1047.
**Trap:** Launched count is not service count; a constellation can be nominal while satellites degrade.
**Mains hook:** Value comes from downstream adoption (receivers, standards, departments), not from launches alone.
**Back:** *"EO sees, Comms talks, Met warns, NavIC places, GAGAN corrects."* (✅ owner 02)

### Flashcard SNT-1030 — Remote-sensing / Earth-observation satellites
**Front:** What are India's remote-sensing (EO) satellites used for, and what are the Resourcesat and Cartosat examples?
**Definition (learn):** Satellites that observe Earth with optical, multispectral or radar sensors for land, water, resource and disaster applications.
**Breakdown:**
- Resourcesat-type: agriculture, water-resource monitoring, land-use analysis.
- Cartosat-type: mapping and infrastructure planning.
- Data becomes public value only when converted to products (NRSC/Bhuvan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 (Basic §5; ISRO EO pages in owner grounding).
**Links:** SNT-1029, SNT-1045 (Mission Drishti), SNT-1046 (OptoSAR), .
**Trap:** Remote sensing is not only defence imaging; agriculture, water and disaster uses are central.
**Mains hook:** EO matters for governance outcomes such as agricultural advisories and flood response; limits include cloud cover and revisit time.
**Back:** *"EO: crops, water, maps, disasters."* (✅ owner 02)

### Flashcard SNT-1031 — Communication satellites (INSAT / GSAT)
**Front:** What do INSAT and GSAT do, and what does a transponder do?
**Definition (learn):** Communication satellites carry transponders that receive, amplify and retransmit signals for telecom, broadcasting and related services.
**Breakdown:**
- INSAT/GSAT: telecom, TV broadcasting, DTH.
- Disaster warning and search-and-rescue linked services.
- Transponder = receive, amplify, retransmit.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 (Basic §2, §5).
**Links:** SNT-1029, SNT-1032 (meteorology), SNT-1014 (GSLV to GTO).
**Trap:** Communication and meteorological satellites are not interchangeable.
**Mains hook:** Communication capacity creates public value through networked services, not by being "in orbit".
**Back:** *"INSAT talks: transponders relay, not observe."* (✅ owner 02)

### Flashcard SNT-1032 — Meteorological satellites INSAT-3D / 3DR / 3DS
**Front:** What do the INSAT-3D family satellites do, and when was INSAT-3DS launched?
**Definition (learn):** Satellites dedicated to weather, climate and atmosphere-ocean observation and warning.
**Breakdown:**
- INSAT-3D, INSAT-3DR, INSAT-3DS: imaging, sounding, forecasting, early warnings.
- Users include IMD and INCOIS-linked users.
- INSAT-3DS launched on GSLV-F14 (17 Feb 2024; augments operational 3D and 3DR).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 17 Feb 2024 (📰 owner 02 §8). Launch vehicle GSLV-F14 per PIB note in owner.
**Links:** SNT-1029, SNT-1031, SNT-1014.
**Trap:** Meteorological satellites are not communication satellites, despite both being in GEO.
**Mains hook:** Satellite weather data supports early-warning and disaster governance; satellite utility is sector-specific.
**Back:** *"3D weather: image, sound, warn."* (✅ owner 02)

### Flashcard SNT-1033 — NavIC / IRNSS: constellation and coverage
**Front:** What is NavIC (IRNSS), how many satellites are in its nominal constellation, and how far does its coverage extend?
**Definition (learn):** India's independent regional Position, Navigation and Timing (PNT) system covering India and its surroundings.
**Breakdown:**
- Nominal constellation: 7 satellites — 3 geostationary and 4 inclined geosynchronous (IGSO).
- Coverage: India plus about 1,500 km beyond its land mass (✅ Wikipedia; extension to about 3,000 km planned).
- First satellite IRNSS-1A launched 1 Jul 2013 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 Basic §2; ✅ Wikipedia (NavIC) for composition, coverage and IRNSS-1A date.
**Links:** SNT-1034 (SPS/RS), SNT-1036 (constellation health), SNT-1040 (SBAS vs GBAS), SNT-1042 (NavIC vs GAGAN vs GPS), SNT-1049 (independent navigation countries).
**Trap:** NavIC is not India's name for GPS; it is regional by design, not a global system.
**Mains hook:** Regional PNT is sovereignty in positioning and timing, and its strength is in replenishment and clock health, not raw count.
**Back:** *"Seven in the sky: three GEO, four IGSO, 1,500 km ring."* (✅ owner 02; ✅ Wikipedia)

### Flashcard SNT-1034 — NavIC services: SPS vs RS
**Front:** What are NavIC's two services, and what separates the open service from the restricted one?
**Definition (learn):** SPS (Standard Positioning Service) is the open civilian service; RS (Restricted Service) is an encrypted service for authorised users.
**Breakdown:**
- SPS: open civilian positioning and timing.
- RS: encrypted; available to authorised users such as the military (✅ Wikipedia).
- The distinction is encryption and authorisation, not a simple military-civilian label.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 (ISRO states two services). ✅ Wikipedia for encryption and authorised users.
**Links:** SNT-1033, SNT-1035 (bands).
**Trap:** "RS means NavIC is unavailable for civilians" is wrong; SPS is the civilian open layer.
**Mains hook:** Sovereign PNT includes protected layers; openness and security must be balanced in signal policy.
**Back:** *"SPS shares, RS locks."* (✅ owner 02; ✅ Wikipedia)

### Flashcard SNT-1035 — NavIC signal bands and the civil L1 signal
**Front:** In which bands does NavIC broadcast, and why was the civil L1 signal added?
**Definition (learn):** NavIC broadcasts in L5 and S-band; a civil L1 signal makes NavIC usable in mass-market GNSS chipsets.
**Breakdown:**
- L5 (1176.45 MHz) and S-band (✅ Wikipedia).
- L1 (1575.42 MHz) for interoperability with mass-market receivers (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner says the civil L1 signal was first carried by NVS-01 (launched 29 May 2023).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Conflict: owner 02 says L1 first on NVS-01 (29 May 2023); Wikipedia says L1 used "since the second generation" of satellites. Flag, do not resolve.
**Links:** SNT-1033, SNT-1034, SNT-1036.
**Trap:** L1 is an interoperability choice, not merely a technical upgrade.
**Mains hook:** Signal interoperability decides handset adoption; the policy question is whether receivers are mandated or incentivised.
**Back:** *"L5 and S for NavIC; L1 to meet your phone."* (⚠️ conflict owner 02 vs Wikipedia)

### Flashcard SNT-1036 — NavIC constellation health: launched is not functional
**Front:** What does the official July 2025 disaggregation of NavIC satellites say, and why is "launched" not "operational"?
**Definition (learn):** Launched satellites are not the same as functional PNT satellites; health must be cited as a dated breakdown.
**Breakdown:**
- 23 Jul 2025 breakdown: 4 PNT satellites, 4 one-way message-broadcast satellites, 1 decommissioned, 2 that could not reach intended orbit.
- 12 Feb 2026 Rajya Sabha reply: 11 launched, 8 operational (operational includes message-broadcast-only satellites).
- Wikipedia notes several satellites have lost atomic clocks (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §8 (PIB/parliamentary replies). ✅ Wikipedia (clock-loss note).
**Links:** SNT-1033, SNT-1037 (NVS-02), SNT-1044 (clock dependence).
**Trap:** "Eleven launched, so eleven working" is wrong; "operational" ≠ PNT-capable.
**Mains hook:** A small nominal constellation has no spare capacity, so each clock failure is strategically material.
**Back:** *"4 PNT, 4 messages, 1 dead, 2 lost in orbit."* (✅ owner 02; ✅ Wikipedia)

### Flashcard SNT-1037 — NVS-02: launch success, orbit-raising anomaly
**Front:** What happened to NVS-02, and what does it teach about milestones?
**Definition (learn):** A NavIC satellite injected correctly by GSLV-F15 but unable to complete planned orbit-raising.
**Breakdown:**
- GSLV-F15 injection on 29 Jan 2025 (correct injection).
- Orbit-raising not done: an oxidiser-line pyro valve did not get its drive signal (likely connector-contact disengagement).
- ISRO report 25 Feb 2026 (📰 owner 02).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 29 Jan 2025; 25 Feb 2026 (owner).
**Links:** SNT-1036, SNT-1014 (GSLV), SNT-1026 (reliability).
**Trap:** Launch success does not guarantee a working satellite; launch, injection, orbit-raising and service entry are four milestones.
**Mains hook:** Single-point propulsion failures show why capability claims must state which milestone was reached.
**Back:** *"Launch, inject, raise, serve: four milestones."* (✅ owner 02)

### Flashcard SNT-1038 — GAGAN: SBAS for civil aviation
**Front:** What is GAGAN, who developed it, and what is its architecture?
**Definition (learn):** GPS Aided GEO Augmented Navigation: an Indian Satellite Based Augmentation System for aviation-grade navigation.
**Breakdown:**
- Jointly developed by ISRO and the Airports Authority of India (AAI).
- Chain: Indian Reference Stations -> master control centre -> uplink stations -> GEO satellites -> GPS-like frequency broadcast.
- Payloads on GSAT-8, GSAT-10 and GSAT-15.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 (GAGAN portal; DGCA page in owner grounding).
**Links:** SNT-1039 (DGCA certification), SNT-1040 (SBAS vs GBAS), SNT-1041 (integrity), SNT-1042 (comparison), SNT-1043 (landing milestone).
**Trap:** GAGAN is not a separate navigation constellation and does not replace GPS.
**Mains hook:** SBAS adds integrity for safety-critical aviation; certification, not raw accuracy, governs adoption.
**Back:** *"GAGAN: GPS plus Indian corrections, beamed from GEO."* (✅ owner 02)

### Flashcard SNT-1039 — GAGAN certification milestones (DGCA)
**Front:** Which DGCA certifications did GAGAN receive, and on what dates?
**Definition (learn):** DGCA is the aviation certifying regulator; GAGAN was certified for RNP 0.1 and APV-1 approaches.
**Breakdown:**
- RNP 0.1 certification: 30 Dec 2013.
- APV-1 (approach with vertical guidance) certification: 21 Apr 2015.
- Operational since 2015 per owner.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 (DGCA certification page in grounding; ⚠️ dates not fetched).
**Links:** SNT-1038, SNT-1041, SNT-1043.
**Trap:** Operational start and certification milestones differ; keep the RNP and APV labels distinct.
**Mains hook:** Certification is the gate for safety-critical adoption, which is why GAGAN's landing milestone rests on 2013/2015 approvals.
**Back:** *"2013 RNP, 2015 APV: certified before landed."* (✅ owner 02; ⚠️ date-check)

### Flashcard SNT-1040 — SBAS vs GBAS (trap pair)
**Front:** How do SBAS and GBAS differ in coverage and transmission?
**Definition (learn):** SBAS = wide-area corrections via geostationary satellites; GBAS = local corrections via VHF from one airport.
**Breakdown:**
- SBAS (GAGAN, WAAS, EGNOS, MSAS): wide area, GEO satellites.
- GBAS: local aerodrome, VHF broadcast, very high precision in a small radius (⚠️ standard definition, owner 02).
- Complements, not substitutes.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 Basic §2 (GBAS marked ⚠️).
**Links:** SNT-1038, SNT-1041, SNT-1042.
**Trap:** SBAS and GBAS are not alternative names for one system.
**Mains hook:** Airport-local precision and wide-area integrity serve different aviation needs; regional augmentation complements constellations.
**Back:** *"SBAS spans the sky; GBAS sits on one runway."* (⚠️ owner 02)

### Flashcard SNT-1041 — Integrity and time-to-alert
**Front:** Why is integrity, not accuracy, the decisive aviation requirement?
**Definition (learn):** Integrity is the system's ability to warn users, within a time-to-alert, that a signal must not be used.
**Breakdown:**
- SBAS must warn within a defined interval.
- Accuracy without a timely alert is useless for approach and landing.
- DGCA certification (RNP, APV) matters more than a raw accuracy number.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ analytical definition; owner 02 Advanced §3.
**Links:** SNT-1038, SNT-1039, SNT-1040.
**Trap:** "SBAS is just more accurate GPS" is wrong; its regulatory value is integrity.
**Mains hook:** Safety-critical navigation is judged by alerting, not only by position precision.
**Back:** *"Integrity says stop, accuracy says where."* (⚠️ analytical)

### Flashcard SNT-1042 — NavIC vs GAGAN vs GPS (comparison)
**Front:** Set out NavIC, GAGAN and GPS in terms of ownership, function and the strategic problem each solves.
**Definition (learn):** NavIC = Indian constellation providing PNT; GAGAN = SBAS augmenting GPS; GPS = foreign global GNSS.
**Breakdown:**
- NavIC: sovereign regional constellation; open SPS and encrypted RS.
- GAGAN: GPS correction and integrity for aviation; no independent navigation.
- GPS: US global system; GAGAN relies on it.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 Basic §6.
**Links:** SNT-1033, SNT-1038, SNT-1049.
**Trap:** Augmenting GPS and owning a PNT constellation solve different problems; GAGAN does not make NavIC redundant.
**Mains hook:** Use "sovereignty in PNT" for NavIC and "aviation-grade integrity" for GAGAN; mixing the phrases weakens an answer.
**Back:** *"NavIC owns, GAGAN augments, GPS belongs to someone else."* (✅ owner 02)

### Flashcard SNT-1043 — GAGAN landing milestone (June 2026)
**Front:** What satellite-based landing milestone did GAGAN record in June 2026?
**Definition (learn):** DGCA conducted India's first satellite-based landing-system approach on a commercial jet using GAGAN.
**Breakdown:**
- PIB recorded it on 1 Jul 2026 (📰 owner 02).
- Rests on the 2013/2015 DGCA certifications (SNT-1039).
- Illustrates certification as the gate for safety-critical use.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jun 2026 event; PIB 1 Jul 2026 (single-source, owner).
**Links:** SNT-1038, SNT-1039, SNT-1041.
**Trap:** A milestone on one commercial jet is not fleet-wide mandatory adoption.
**Mains hook:** Space technology serves aviation safety only through regulatory trust.
**Back:** *"GAGAN lands a jet: certified first, flown second."* (⚠️ single-source)

### Flashcard SNT-1044 — Trilateration and clock dependence
**Front:** How does a NavIC receiver compute position, and why are atomic clocks the critical component?
**Definition (learn):** Trilateration: the receiver measures signal travel time from several satellites at known positions.
**Breakdown:**
- Travel time measured against on-board atomic clocks.
- Light travels about 30 cm per nanosecond; ranging errors scale with clock error (⚠️ owner 02 Advanced §3).
- Clock failures, not launch failures, usually degrade a constellation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 Basic §3; ✅ Wikipedia (clock-loss note).
**Links:** SNT-1036, SNT-1033.
**Trap:** Navigation is timing before positioning.
**Mains hook:** Timing sovereignty is a strategic asset for finance, power grids and telecom synchronisation.
**Back:** *"Trilaterate by time: clocks are the weak link."* (✅ owner 02; ⚠️ 30 cm/ns analytical)

### Flashcard SNT-1045 — Mission Drishti (GalaxEye, 3 May 2026)
**Front:** What is Mission Drishti, who built it, and when was it launched?
**Definition (learn):** An Earth-observation satellite built by GalaxEye, a Bengaluru-based private start-up, launched 3 May 2026 on a SpaceX Falcon 9 rideshare from California.
**Breakdown:**
- Weight about 190 kg; described as India's largest privately developed EO satellite.
- Carries an optical multispectral imager (MSI) and a Synthetic Aperture Radar (SAR) payload on one platform.
- Exact 2026 Mains Q15 (GS-III, 15 marks, 250 words) asked about salient features, imaging techniques and why it is "world's first".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §13 (OCR of 2026 GS-III paper; Vajiram & Ravi May 2026).
**Links:** SNT-1046 (OptoSAR), SNT-1030 (EO), SNT-1047 (segments).
**Trap:** Do not describe it as a constellation; it is one satellite.
**Mains hook:** Single-platform optical-SAR fusion removes cross-satellite parallax and temporal gaps; the "world first" claim rests on that synchronisation.
**Back:** *"Drishti: one bird, two eyes, cloud-proof."* (✅ owner 02)

### Flashcard SNT-1046 — OptoSAR vs optical vs SAR (comparison)
**Front:** Compare optical imaging, SAR and OptoSAR fusion, and state what OptoSAR removes.
**Definition (learn):** OptoSAR = optical and SAR payloads capturing the same scene simultaneously on one platform.
**Breakdown:**
- Optical (MSI): fails in cloud and darkness; high interpretability.
- SAR: works through cloud and at night; low ease of interpretation.
- OptoSAR: both, from one pass; removes parallax, temporal-gap and alignment errors.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §13 comparison table.
**Links:** SNT-1045, SNT-1030, SNT-1047.
**Trap:** OptoSAR is one satellite, not two satellites working together.
**Mains hook:** SAR's all-weather capability supports disaster, agriculture and maritime awareness, not only defence.
**Back:** *"Optical sees the day, SAR sees through cloud, OptoSAR sees both at once."* (✅ owner 02; ⚠️ processing-claim)

### Flashcard SNT-1047 — Space, ground and user segments
**Front:** Name the three segments of a satellite value chain and the recurring Indian bottleneck.
**Definition (learn):** Space segment (satellites), ground segment (control, reference and uplink stations) and user segment (chipsets, receivers, standards, applications).
**Breakdown:**
- Space segment: satellites.
- Ground segment: tracking, calibration, data processing.
- User segment: receivers, chipsets, departmental adoption.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 Basic §3 (item 7); Advanced §3.
**Links:** SNT-1029, SNT-1046, SNT-1033.
**Trap:** Satellites are not useful alone; the user segment has been the NavIC bottleneck, not the space segment.
**Mains hook:** Satellite public value depends on receivers, standards, ground infrastructure and departments.
**Back:** *"Space, Ground, User: the user is the bottleneck."* (⚠️ owner 02 analytical)

### Flashcard SNT-1048 — Solar flares, GNSS and the aurora (2022 PYQ)
**Front:** How can solar flares affect GPS/GNSS, power grids and communication, and what is the aurora's role?
**Definition (learn):** Solar flares and coronal activity disturb the ionosphere, degrading GNSS propagation and radio communication.
**Breakdown:**
- Ionospheric disturbance degrades GNSS and radio links.
- Geomagnetically induced effects can stress ground power systems.
- Aurora is an atmospheric light phenomenon, not a navigation service.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 GS-I Prelims Q40 (owner routing; key not held locally; not inferred).
**Links:** SNT-1033, SNT-1044, SNT-1049.
**Trap:** Do not say a navigation satellite performs a banking transaction or controls a grid; PNT supplies position/time only.
**Mains hook:** Space weather is a resilience issue for PNT-dependent infrastructure.
**Back:** *"Flares scramble GNSS, aurora just glows."* (✅ owner 02 PYQ routing; ⚠️ answer not inferred)

### Flashcard SNT-1049 — Countries with independent navigation systems (2023 PYQ)
**Front:** Which satellite navigation systems are independent global or regional systems, and where does NavIC fit?
**Definition (learn):** Independent GNSS are systems with their own constellations: GPS, GLONASS, Galileo, BeiDou (global) and NavIC (regional).
**Breakdown:**
- Global: GPS (US), GLONASS (Russia), Galileo (EU), BeiDou (China).
- Regional: NavIC (India).
- GAGAN is an augmentation layer, not a constellation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 PYQ routing (2023 GS-I Q57; key not held locally). Country list from standard knowledge marked ⚠️.
**Links:** SNT-1033, SNT-1042, SNT-1048.
**Trap:** An SBAS, a launch record and an independent navigation system are three different propositions.
**Mains hook:** Independent PNT is a sovereignty measure; augmentation does not substitute for it.
**Back:** *"Four global, one Indian regional; GAGAN is not a constellation."* (✅ owner 02; ⚠️ country list)

### Flashcard SNT-1050 — Need for IRNSS (2018 GS-I Mains Q4)
**Front:** Why was IRNSS needed, according to the owner's Mains routing for 2018?
**Definition (learn):** The 2018 Mains question asked for the need for IRNSS and its role in navigation (10 marks, 150 words).
**Breakdown:**
- Strategic and civilian navigation needs over India's region.
- Positioning, navigation and timing services.
- Answer frame: context, dimensions, evidence, counterpoint, conclusion.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 Advanced PYQ routing (2018 GS-I Q4).
**Links:** SNT-1033, SNT-1034, SNT-1042.
**Trap:** Do not answer as if NavIC were a global replacement for GPS.
**Mains hook:** Justify IRNSS as sovereignty in PNT with honest adoption and constellation-health caveats.
**Back:** *"IRNSS: our sky for our map."* (✅ owner 02)

### GROUP — Human Spaceflight, Gaganyaan and Planetary Missions

### Flashcard SNT-1051 — Status discipline across four ladders
**Front:** Why must human spaceflight, lunar, solar and international crewed exposure be kept as separate ladders, and what status words must never be mixed?
**Definition (learn):** Four ladders: (a) human spaceflight: test -> uncrewed orbital -> crewed -> station; (b) lunar: orbit -> soft land -> rove -> sample return; (c) solar/deep-space observation at Lagrange points; (d) international crewed cooperation.
**Breakdown:**
- Status vocabulary: completed != operational != approved != under development != concluded mission.
- Human-spaceflight credibility comes from demonstrated survivability, not from launch success.
- Planetary missions depend on deep-space navigation, autonomy and mass budgets.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §1 table and §2 ladder note.
**Links:** SNT-1052, SNT-1062, SNT-1067, SNT-1071, SNT-1073, SNT-1074.
**Trap:** Writing an approved programme as a flown one is the most common status error.
**Mains hook:** Status discipline and mission sequencing are the test of a credible long-horizon space policy (⚠️ analytical).
**Back:** *"Four ladders, five statuses: never climb the wrong ladder."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1052 — Gaganyaan: crew, orbit, duration and recovery
**Front:** What is Gaganyaan's official concept: crew size, orbit, duration and recovery?
**Definition (learn):** India's indigenous human-spaceflight programme to send a crew to low Earth orbit and recover them in Indian waters.
**Breakdown:**
- Crew: three members.
- Orbit: about 400 km (LEO).
- Duration: about three days per ISRO's concept (owner 03).
- Recovery: splashdown in Indian sea waters; Indian Navy supports recovery.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §6 (ISRO Gaganyaan page). ✅ Wikipedia: crew 3, about 400 km, LEO; ⚠️ Wikipedia gives "up to seven days" for duration.
**Links:** SNT-1053 (HLVM3), SNT-1054 (Orbital Module), SNT-1057 (CES), SNT-1059 (air-drop tests), SNT-1074 (status).
**Trap:** The duration conflicts between sources; quote ISRO's official "about three days" and flag the other figure.
**Mains hook:** Crewed flight needs launcher human-rating, abort, life support and recovery; a single test cannot certify all of them.
**Back:** *"Three crew, four hundred km, three days, Indian sea."* (✅ owner 03; ⚠️ duration conflict with Wikipedia)

### Flashcard SNT-1053 — HLVM3: the human-rated launcher
**Front:** Which launcher does ISRO identify for Gaganyaan, and what is the human-rated variant called?
**Definition (learn):** Human Rated LVM3 (HLVM3) is the launcher identified for Gaganyaan.
**Breakdown:**
- Base vehicle: LVM3 (SNT-1015).
- Human-rating adds margins, redundancy, health monitoring and abort provisions.
- Human rating is a process, not a new rocket.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §6.
**Links:** SNT-1015, SNT-1023, SNT-1052.
**Trap:** A strong success record alone does not make a launcher human-rated.
**Mains hook:** Human rating is the first qualification layer before crewed flight.
**Back:** *"HLVM3: LVM3 with crew-grade margins."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1054 — Orbital Module = Crew Module + Service Module
**Front:** What comprises the Gaganyaan Orbital Module?
**Definition (learn):** A combined flight element of a Crew Module and a Service Module.
**Breakdown:**
- Crew Module: habitable, re-entry capable.
- Service Module: propulsion, power and non-habitable orbital support.
- Redundancy and abort capability are built into the stack.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §2 and §6.
**Links:** SNT-1055, SNT-1056, SNT-1052.
**Trap:** The Service Module is not habitable; do not describe crew living there.
**Mains hook:** Separating habitable and support functions is what makes survivability testable.
**Back:** *"Orbital = Crew (lives) + Service (drives)."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1055 — Crew Module (CM)
**Front:** What is the Crew Module, and what does it do?
**Definition (learn):** The habitable, re-entry-capable part of Gaganyaan that carries astronauts in an Earth-like environment.
**Breakdown:**
- Carries the crew and ECLSS-supported habitat.
- Re-entry and parachute-based recovery (see SNT-1059).
- Tested with a crew-module mass simulator in air-drop tests.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §2.
**Links:** SNT-1054, SNT-1056, SNT-1060 (ECLSS), SNT-1059.
**Trap:** The Crew Module is not the same as the Crew Escape System; CES pulls the module away from a failing launcher.
**Mains hook:** Habitability and re-entry are where human-spaceflight engineering diverges from satellite engineering.
**Back:** *"Crew Module: cabin that returns."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1056 — Service Module (SM)
**Front:** What does the Service Module provide to Gaganyaan?
**Definition (learn):** The non-habitable module providing propulsion, power and orbital support to the Crew Module.
**Breakdown:**
- Propulsion and orbital manoeuvring.
- Power and other support functions.
- Part of the Orbital Module (SNT-1054).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §2.
**Links:** SNT-1054, SNT-1055.
**Trap:** Service Module is not a crew habitat.
**Mains hook:** Splitting habitat from propulsion is how a crewed stack gets redundancy.
**Back:** *"Service: push and power, no beds."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1057 — Crew Escape System, Pad Abort and TV-D1
**Front:** What is the Crew Escape System, and what do the Pad Abort Test (5 Jul 2018) and TV-D1 (21 Oct 2023) demonstrate?
**Definition (learn):** A set of solid motors that pulls the Crew Module clear of a failing launch vehicle, usable from the pad through atmospheric ascent.
**Breakdown:**
- Pad Abort Test: 5 Jul 2018 at SDSC-SHAR (✅ Wikipedia).
- TV-D1: Test Vehicle high-altitude abort mission, 21 Oct 2023 (✅ Wikipedia; owner 03 §6).
- TV-D1 is an abort demonstration, not an orbital mission.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia for both dates.
**Links:** SNT-1023 (human-rating), SNT-1054, SNT-1074.
**Trap:** TV-D1 is not India's first uncrewed Gaganyaan mission; G1 is the first uncrewed orbital flight and is later.
**Mains hook:** Abort modes must be validated per flight phase before human-rating.
**Back:** *"Pad 2018 pulls, TV-D1 2023 proves."* (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia)

### Flashcard SNT-1058 — Vyommitra
**Front:** What is Vyommitra?
**Definition (learn):** ISRO's humanoid mission-support robot for uncrewed human-spaceflight precursor missions.
**Breakdown:**
- A robot, not an astronaut.
- Intended for uncrewed test phases.
- Part of the test ladder before crewed flight.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §2.
**Links:** SNT-1052, SNT-1074.
**Trap:** Vyommitra is not an Indian astronaut.
**Mains hook:** Uncrewed robotic payloads validate systems before risking crew.
**Back:** *"Vyommitra: robot on the test bench."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1059 — Integrated Air Drop Tests and IMAT-05
**Front:** What are the Integrated Air Drop Tests and IMAT-05, and what do they qualify?
**Definition (learn):** Crew-module mass simulator drops from a helicopter or aircraft to qualify parachute deceleration and recovery, with no rocket launch.
**Breakdown:**
- IADT-01: 24 Aug 2025.
- IADT-02: 10 Apr 2026.
- IMAT-05 (Integrated Main Parachute Air Drop Test): 7 Jul 2026; qualification step toward G1.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 📰 owner 03 §8 (ISRO pages).
**Links:** SNT-1055, SNT-1057, SNT-1074.
**Trap:** An air-drop test is not a Gaganyaan flight.
**Mains hook:** Recovery qualification precedes crewed risk acceptance; these are survivability rungs.
**Back:** *"Drop it to qualify it: parachutes before people."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1060 — ECLSS (Environmental Control and Life Support System)
**Front:** What is ECLSS, and why does it separate a crewed spacecraft from a satellite?
**Definition (learn):** The onboard system that maintains breathable atmosphere, pressure, temperature, humidity and CO₂ removal.
**Breakdown:**
- Breathable atmosphere and CO₂ removal.
- Pressure, temperature and humidity control.
- Defining difference between satellite and crewed spacecraft (⚠️ owner 03 analytical).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 03 §2.
**Links:** SNT-1055, SNT-1052.
**Trap:** Life support is not the same as propulsion; both are needed in the Orbital Module.
**Mains hook:** Life support and abort systems drive the cost and time of crewed programmes.
**Back:** *"ECLSS: breathe, cool, scrub."* (⚠️ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1061 — Lagrange point L1 and halo orbit
**Front:** What is a Lagrange point, a halo orbit and why is Sun-Earth L1 used for Aditya-L1?
**Definition (learn):** A Lagrange point is a position where gravitational and centripetal effects allow a small body to hold a configuration; a halo orbit is a periodic 3D orbit around one.
**Breakdown:**
- L1 sits between Sun and Earth and gives an uninterrupted Sun view without eclipses.
- A halo orbit is only quasi-stable and needs station-keeping.
- Station-keeping propellant often sets mission duration.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §2; ⚠️ Lagrange definition (standard).
**Links:** SNT-1062 (Aditya-L1), SNT-1051.
**Trap:** Aditya-L1 is not "on the Sun"; it sits in a halo orbit around Sun-Earth L1.
**Mains hook:** L1 observatories are propellant-budget-limited rather than instrument-limited.
**Back:** *"L1: Sun in view, no eclipse, fuel on a timer."* (✅ owner 03; ⚠️ standard definition)

### Flashcard SNT-1062 — Aditya-L1: solar observatory
**Front:** What is Aditya-L1, where is it, and when did it enter its halo orbit?
**Definition (learn):** ISRO's solar observatory placed in a halo orbit around Sun-Earth L1.
**Breakdown:**
- Continuous solar observation and space-weather science.
- Halo-orbit insertion successful on 6 Jan 2024 (ISRO).
- Solar observatory, not a planetary lander.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 6 Jan 2024 (✅ owner 03; ISRO pages in grounding).
**Links:** SNT-1061, SNT-1048 (space weather), SNT-1051.
**Trap:** Aditya-L1 is not a planetary mission or a lander.
**Mains hook:** Solar observation broadens India's space science beyond landing narratives.
**Back:** *"Aditya-L1: halo-sitting sun watcher, Jan 2024."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1063 — Chandrayaan-1: lunar water and hydroxyl
**Front:** What was Chandrayaan-1's major lunar science outcome?
**Definition (learn):** India's first lunar mission, with science results including detection of hydroxyl/water molecules on the lunar surface and evidence related to subsurface water-ice in permanently shadowed regions.
**Breakdown:**
- Status: completed.
- Water and hydroxyl detection on the Moon.
- Water-ice evidence in permanently shadowed regions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §6 (ISRO Chandrayaan-1 science page). Launch year not recorded in owner; not added.
**Links:** SNT-1064 (Chandrayaan-2), SNT-1065 (Chandrayaan-3).
**Trap:** Chandrayaan-1 is an orbital science mission, not a landing mission.
**Mains hook:** Orbital discovery preceded landing ambitions; science and technology feed each other.
**Back:** *"Chandrayaan-1: water on the Moon, from orbit."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1064 — Chandrayaan-2: orbiter success, lander failure
**Front:** How should Chandrayaan-2 be classified, and why is "total failure" wrong?
**Definition (learn):** Orbiter placed in lunar orbit and science operations continue per ISRO; the lander did not complete soft landing.
**Breakdown:**
- Orbiter: healthy, payloads operational per ISRO (owner 03 Basic §6).
- Lander: soft-landing not achieved.
- Classify as partial success, not total failure.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §6, §7. Owner warns to re-verify orbiter's current status before quoting "currently operating".
**Links:** SNT-1065 (Chandrayaan-3), SNT-1063, SNT-1066 (soft landing).
**Trap:** Chandrayaan-2 and -3 are not the same by chronology alone; -3 is the successful landing.
**Mains hook:** Partial successes teach landing-guidance lessons that enabled later success.
**Back:** *"Two-Two: orbit won, landing lost."* (✅ owner 03; ⚠️ current orbiter status to re-verify)

### Flashcard SNT-1065 — Chandrayaan-3: soft landing, 23 Aug 2023
**Front:** What did Chandrayaan-3 achieve and where?
**Definition (learn):** A controlled soft landing near the lunar south polar region with in-situ surface science, including sulphur detection.
**Breakdown:**
- Soft landing: 23 Aug 2023.
- Surface science and sulphur detection on the surface (owner 03 §6).
- Did not demonstrate sample return, human lunar flight or a lunar base.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 23 Aug 2023 (✅ owner 03; date from standard record, ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** SNT-1064, SNT-1066, SNT-1067.
**Trap:** Chandrayaan-3's soft landing does not mean sample return.
**Mains hook:** Landing and surface operations are the prerequisite for sample return and future lunar sequencing.
**Back:** *"Chandrayaan-3: landed, looked, sulphur found."* (✅ owner 03; ⚠️ date-check)

### Flashcard SNT-1066 — Soft landing vs orbit insertion vs sample return
**Front:** Distinguish soft landing, orbital insertion and sample return with the capability each requires.
**Definition (learn):** Soft landing = controlled non-destructive touchdown; orbit insertion = entering orbit; sample return = retrieving material to Earth.
**Breakdown:**
- Soft landing: defining condition is a controlled, survivable descent.
- Orbit insertion: orbiter only; no touchdown.
- Sample return: needs ascent from another body plus rendezvous/docking or direct return with containment; India has not yet demonstrated these together (owner 03 Basic §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §2.
**Links:** SNT-1064, SNT-1065, SNT-1067.
**Trap:** A landing is not a sample return; an orbiter is not a lander.
**Mains hook:** Sequence landing -> sample return -> human-rating to show the capability ladder.
**Back:** *"Orbit, touch, bring back: three rungs."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1067 — Chandrayaan-4: lunar sample return (approved)
**Front:** What is Chandrayaan-4 and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** A lunar sample-return technology mission approved on 18 Sep 2024.
**Breakdown:**
- Cabinet wording: completion within 36 months.
- Status: approved, under development.
- Requires ascent and rendezvous capability not yet demonstrated.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 18 Sep 2024 (✅ owner 03 Cabinet approval).
**Links:** SNT-1066, SNT-1065, SNT-1051.
**Trap:** Approved is not launched; Chandrayaan-4 is not a flown mission.
**Mains hook:** Sample return is the next lunar rung after soft landing (⚠️ analytical).
**Back:** *"Chandrayaan-4: approved return ticket, not yet flown."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1068 — Venus Orbiter Mission (Shukrayaan)
**Front:** What is the Venus Orbiter Mission, and what is its launch opportunity?
**Definition (learn):** India's next comparative-planetology mission to Venus, also called Shukrayaan.
**Breakdown:**
- Approved 18 Sep 2024.
- Stated launch opportunity: March 2028.
- Approved future mission, not an operating mission.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 18 Sep 2024; Mar 2028 (✅ owner 03). Shukrayaan name: ⚠️ owner-listed.
**Links:** SNT-1069 (Mangalyaan), SNT-1051.
**Trap:** Mars legacy does not guarantee Venus success; each destination has different challenges.
**Mains hook:** Venus is the next comparative-planetology step after Mars (⚠️ analytical).
**Back:** *"Shukrayaan: Venus next, March 2028 window."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1069 — Mangalyaan (Mars Orbiter Mission): concluded
**Front:** What is Mangalyaan's status and why is it cited as a historic mission?
**Definition (learn):** India's Mars Orbiter Mission, a low-cost historic Mars orbiter, now treated as a concluded mission.
**Breakdown:**
- Outlived original design life.
- Recent official references treat it as concluded.
- Not operational today.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §6. Launch year not in owner; not added.
**Links:** SNT-1068, SNT-1051.
**Trap:** Do not describe Mangalyaan as operational.
**Mains hook:** Low-cost Mars mission is a benchmark for frugal planetary science (⚠️ analytical).
**Back:** *"Mangalyaan: historic, concluded, not operating."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1070 — Bharatiya Antariksh Station (BAS-01)
**Front:** What is the Bharatiya Antariksh Station, when was BAS-01 approved and targeted, and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** India's planned space station, approved within an expanded Gaganyaan scope.
**Breakdown:**
- Approved 18 Sep 2024.
- BAS-01 (first module) target: December 2028.
- Shift from one-off crewed demonstration to continuity of presence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §1, §8.
**Links:** SNT-1052, SNT-1051, SNT-1078 (station aspiration).
**Trap:** An announced station is not an operational facility.
**Mains hook:** A station is a harder logistics, life-support and resupply problem than a one-off flight.
**Back:** *"BAS-01: December 2028 target, not yet a station."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1071 — Axiom-4 (Shubhanshu Shukla)
**Front:** What was Axiom-4, who flew, and what is its significance?
**Definition (learn):** An international ISS mission on a SpaceX Dragon with Group Captain Shubhanshu Shukla as mission pilot.
**Breakdown:**
- Launched 25 Jun 2025; returned 15 Jul 2025; 18-day mission.
- Seven Indian experiments aboard the ISS.
- International mission, not an Indian human-spaceflight mission.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §6, §8.
**Links:** SNT-1072 (Gaganyatri), SNT-1073 (trap), SNT-1079 (microgravity).
**Trap:** Axiom-4 is not Gaganyaan and not an indigenous crewed flight.
**Mains hook:** International exposure complements indigenous capability but does not substitute for it.
**Back:** *"Axiom-4: Indian eyes on the ISS, not an Indian ship."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1072 — Gaganyatri and the Astronaut Training Facility
**Front:** Who are Gaganyatris and where is astronaut training done?
**Definition (learn):** Gaganyatri is the term for Indian astronaut-designate crew associated with Gaganyaan.
**Breakdown:**
- Astronaut Training Facility, Bengaluru (ISRO): academics, simulators, physical fitness, aero-medical work, survival and procedures.
- HSFC (Human Space Flight Centre) leads the Gaganyaan programme.
- Shubhanshu Shukla's ISRO return was branded as the Gaganyatri mission (owner 03 grounding).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §4.
**Links:** SNT-1071, SNT-1052.
**Trap:** Training is not the same as flight; Gaganyatri status does not equal crewed orbital flight.
**Mains hook:** Training and recovery are institutional parts of a human-spaceflight ecosystem.
**Back:** *"Gaganyatri trains at Bengaluru, flies later."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1073 — Axiom-4 vs Gaganyaan (trap pair)
**Front:** Compare Axiom-4 and Gaganyaan on sponsor, vehicle, destination and status.
**Definition (learn):** Axiom-4 is an international ISS mission; Gaganyaan is indigenous.
**Breakdown:**
- Axiom-4: SpaceX Dragon, to the ISS, flown and concluded.
- Gaganyaan: ISRO's HLVM3, LEO ~400 km, under development.
- Gaganyaan crewed flight targeted 2027-28 per owner.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §6, §7.
**Links:** SNT-1071, SNT-1052, SNT-1074.
**Trap:** "Axiom-4 made Gaganyaan unnecessary" and "an Indian astronaut has gone to space under Gaganyaan" are both wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use the pair to show the difference between experience and indigenous capability.
**Back:** *"Dragon rented, HLVM3 owned."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1074 — Gaganyaan test sequence and G1 status
**Front:** Which Gaganyaan tests are complete, what is G1, and what is the crewed-flight target?
**Definition (learn):** The uncrewed test sequence is complete up to IMAT-05; G1 is the first uncrewed orbital flight and had not flown as of the owner's latest verified statement.
**Breakdown:**
- Sequence: Pad Abort Test (2018) -> TV-D1 (2023) -> IADT-01 (2025) -> IADT-02 (2026) -> IMAT-05 (7 Jul 2026).
- G1: uncrewed orbital flight; not yet flown (owner, Aug 2026).
- Crewed flight: officially targeted 2027-28.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §6, §8 (status as of 2 Aug 2026). ⚠️ Wikipedia lists G1 as scheduled for October 2026; time-varying, re-verify before use.
**Links:** SNT-1057, SNT-1059, SNT-1052, SNT-1073.
**Trap:** Test success is not an orbital flight; target dates are not achievements.
**Mains hook:** A slipped schedule in a crewed programme is safety governance working, not failure.
**Back:** *"Tests passed, G1 pending, crew 2027-28 target."* (✅ owner 03; ⚠️ G1 date conflict)

### Flashcard SNT-1075 — Radioisotope thermoelectric generator (RTG)
**Front:** What is an RTG and how does it differ from a fission reactor? (2024 Prelims Q31)
**Definition (learn):** An RTG converts heat from radioactive decay into electricity through thermocouples.
**Breakdown:**
- Not a fission reactor.
- Useful where solar power is weak or intermittent (deep-space probes).
- Trade-off: low electrical output and radiological containment.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-I Q31 (official Set-A key held locally; answer not recorded here).
**Links:** SNT-1061, SNT-1076.
**Trap:** RTG is not a reactor and does not offer unlimited power.
**Mains hook:** Power sources for deep-space missions trade mass, duration and containment.
**Back:** *"RTG: decay heat to current, no chain reaction."* (✅ owner 03 PYQ routing; ⚠️ answer not inferred)

### Flashcard SNT-1076 — Asteroid threat and DART (2024 GS-III Q16)
**Front:** How should an answer treat asteroid threat, detection and deflection, with DART as evidence?
**Definition (learn):** Planetary defence is chiefly an early-warning problem: detection -> orbit and characterisation -> decision -> deflection -> post-action tracking.
**Breakdown:**
- DART: NASA kinetic impact on Dimorphos (2022) changed its orbit.
- Other ideas: gravity tractor, civil defence/evacuation; nuclear disruption is not a routine deployed defence.
- Limit: a controlled test, not a universal shield.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-III Q16 (15 marks, 250 words); DART 2022 (✅ owner 03 Advanced).
**Links:** SNT-1075, SNT-1077.
**Trap:** Do not present DART as a guaranteed planetary shield.
**Mains hook:** Invest in detection, characterisation and international coordination; treat deflection as one method.
**Back:** *"Spot it early, nudge it, watch it."* (✅ owner 03)

### Flashcard SNT-1077 — LISA concept (2020 Prelims Q43)
**Front:** What is the LISA concept and how does it detect gravitational waves?
**Definition (learn):** A planned gravitational-wave observatory of three spacecraft in a large formation measuring tiny distance changes by laser interferometry.
**Breakdown:**
- Three spacecraft formation.
- Laser interferometry for tiny distance changes.
- A space observatory design, not a conventional imaging satellite.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 GS-I Q43 (key not held locally).
**Links:** SNT-1076, SNT-1078.
**Trap:** LISA is not an Earth-imaging satellite.
**Mains hook:** Physics-led space missions broaden the science case beyond landing and orbit.
**Back:** *"LISA: three ships, one laser ruler."* (✅ owner 03 PYQ routing)

### Flashcard SNT-1078 — Space station aspiration and JWST (Mains routes)
**Front:** Using the 2019 station question and the 2022 JWST question, how should a station and the James Webb Space Telescope be answered?
**Definition (learn):** A station extends one-off crewed flight into sustained presence; JWST is an international infrared observatory at Sun-Earth L2.
**Breakdown:**
- Station: microgravity research, docking/resupply, life support, long-duration operation.
- JWST: infrared observations of early-universe objects, star/planet formation and exoplanet atmospheres.
- JWST is not an Indian launch vehicle.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 GS-III Q6 (station plan); 2022 GS-III Q15 (JWST) per owner routing.
**Links:** SNT-1070, SNT-1061, SNT-1065.
**Trap:** A target module is not a functioning station.
**Mains hook:** Value of station depends on safety, budget, research use and launch/recovery maturity.
**Back:** *"Station = sustained presence; JWST = infrared eye at L2."* (✅ owner 03 routing)

### Flashcard SNT-1079 — Microgravity research: Axiom-4, SpaDeX, Gaganyaan (2025 Prelims Q83)
**Front:** How do Axiom-4, SpaDeX and Gaganyaan differ in relation to microgravity research?
**Definition (learn):** Microgravity research uses orbital environments for biology, materials and physics in low gravity.
**Breakdown:**
- Axiom-4: international ISS mission with Indian experiments.
- SpaDeX: in-space docking technology concerns.
- Gaganyaan: indigenous crewed programme, not flown yet.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-I Q83 (official key held locally; answer not recorded).
**Links:** SNT-1071, SNT-1073, SNT-1052.
**Trap:** Do not collapse astronaut exposure, docking capability and an unflown crewed programme into one accomplishment.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish microgravity access, docking technology and indigenous crewed capability.
**Back:** *"Axiom: ride. SpaDeX: dock. Gaganyaan: yet to fly."* (✅ owner 03 routing; ⚠️ answer not inferred)

### GROUP — Nuclear Power and the Three-Stage Programme

### Flashcard SNT-1080 — Fission power and the three-stage resource logic
**Front:** Why is India's three-stage nuclear programme a resource-constrained fuel-cycle strategy rather than a timetable?
**Definition (learn):** Converts abundant fertile thorium-232 into fissile uranium-233 via a plutonium-fuelled fast-reactor bridge, with PHWRs first.
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: PHWRs on natural uranium; plutonium produced in spent fuel.
- Stage 2: fast breeder reactors multiply fissile material and irradiate thorium blankets.
- Stage 3: thorium–U-233 systems close the cycle.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 04 §1, §6 (BARC programme page in grounding).
**Links:** SNT-1084, SNT-1086, SNT-1087, SNT-1088, SNT-1111.
**Trap:** The three stages are sequentially linked, not three parallel labels.
**Mains hook:** The difficulty is closed-fuel-cycle engineering, not physics; capacity has been constrained by fuel, siting, liability and finance.
**Back:** *"Stage 1 burns, Stage 2 breeds, Stage 3 thorium-burns."* (✅ owner 04)

### Flashcard SNT-1081 — Nuclear fission and criticality
**Front:** What is nuclear fission, and what is criticality (k = 1)?
**Definition (learn):** Fission splits heavy nuclei releasing neutrons and heat; criticality is a state where the effective multiplication factor k equals exactly 1.
**Breakdown:**
- k > 1: reaction growing; k < 1: dying out; k = 1: sustained.
- Heat -> coolant -> steam -> turbine -> electricity.
- Criticality is the start of a controlled chain reaction, not grid synchronisation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 04 §3 (standard k-definition; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** SNT-1082, SNT-1096 (PFBR first criticality), SNT-1097 (RAPP-7 criticality).
**Trap:** Criticality is not power operation or commercial operation.
**Mains hook:** Use criticality as a milestone, not as proof of a fleet.
**Back:** *"k equals one: the chain holds, not the grid."* (✅ owner 04 / ⚠️ k definition)

### Flashcard SNT-1082 — Moderator vs coolant
**Front:** What is a moderator, what is a coolant, and which reactors have none of the first?
**Definition (learn):** Moderator slows neutrons so fission can be sustained in thermal reactors; coolant removes heat from the core.
**Breakdown:**
- Thermal reactors (PHWR, BWR, PWR) have moderators.
- Fast breeder reactors have no moderator; sodium is the coolant.
- Heavy water lets India burn natural uranium; light water absorbs more neutrons and needs enriched fuel.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 04 §2, §6.
**Links:** SNT-1089, SNT-1092, SNT-1093.
**Trap:** "Every reactor has a moderator" is wrong; FBRs deliberately have none.
**Mains hook:** Moderator choice drives fuel choice and therefore India's natural-uranium strategy.
**Back:** *"Moderator slows, coolant carries; fast reactors skip the slowing."* (✅ owner 04)

### Flashcard SNT-1083 — Control rods vs containment
**Front:** What regulates the chain reaction, and what does containment do?
**Definition (learn):** Control rods (boron or cadmium absorbers) regulate the chain reaction; containment is a passive barrier against radioactive release.
**Breakdown:**
- Control rods: operator's regulation tool.
- Containment: release barrier, not a control device.
- Moderator: slows neutrons, present only in thermal reactors.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 04 §3.
**Links:** SNT-1082, SNT-1081.
**Trap:** Containment does not control the reaction.
**Mains hook:** Safety architecture uses layers: control, cooling, containment; keep them separate in answers.
**Back:** *"Rods control, containment contains."* (✅ owner 04)

### Flashcard SNT-1084 — Fissile vs fertile
**Front:** Define fissile and fertile materials and give examples including U-233 and thorium.
**Definition (learn):** Fissile nuclides sustain a chain reaction with thermal neutrons; fertile nuclides convert into fissile ones on neutron capture.
**Breakdown:**
- Fissile: U-235, Pu-239, U-233.
- Fertile: U-238 (-> Pu-239), Th-232 (-> U-233).
- Thorium must first be irradiated in a reactor with a fissile driver.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 04 §1 table.
**Links:** SNT-1085, SNT-1088, SNT-1087.
