### Flashcard SNT-3121 — ICAR's genome-edited rice varieties (4 Apr 2025)
**Front:** Name the two genome-edited rice varieties ICAR announced on 4 Apr 2025, and their origin.
**Definition (learn):** DRR Rice 100 (Kamla), derived from Samba Mahsuri, has more grains per panicle and about 20 days earlier maturity. Pusa DST Rice 1, derived from MTU 1010, performs better in saline and alkaline soils. Both were produced with CRISPR-Cas without adding foreign DNA, under the SDN-1/SDN-2 route.
**Breakdown:**
- DRR Rice 100 (Kamla): from Samba Mahsuri; more grains per panicle; ~20 days earlier
- Pusa DST Rice 1: from MTU 1010; saline/alkaline tolerance
- Route: SDN-1/SDN-2 without foreign DNA
- Seed availability and area under cultivation: unverified
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 04 Apr 2025 (✅ owner 14 basic §8 and advanced §10; ICAR announcement URL cited in owner).
**Links:** SNT-3112, SNT-3120
**Trap:** Announced varieties are not yet a measured cultivation area.
**Mains hook:** The 2022 exemption produced varieties, not just papers. Qualified verdict: check seed access and farmer uptake.
**Back:** *"Kamla from Samba Mahsuri, Pusa DST from MTU 1010: edited, not transgenic."*

### Flashcard SNT-3122 — GEAC minutes of 2026 and the BGII Roundup Ready Flex proposal
**Front:** What do the GEAC minutes of 20 Mar 2026 and 14 Jul 2026 show about the herbicide-tolerant cotton proposal?
**Definition (learn):** Published minutes of the 159th (20 Mar 2026) and 161st (14 Jul 2026) GEAC meetings show continued scrutiny of BGII Roundup Ready Flex, a herbicide-tolerant cotton proposal. The applicant was asked for additional India-specific data and protocols, and the matter was referred back to the expert committee.
**Breakdown:**
- Trait: herbicide tolerance (HT)
- Status: under regulatory review, not approved
- Required: India-specific data and protocols
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 20 Mar 2026; 14 Jul 2026; 159th and 161st meetings (✅ owner 14 advanced §6 and §8; GEAC minute links cited in owner).
**Links:** SNT-3106, SNT-3105, SNT-3113
**Trap:** Reading "under review" as approval.
**Mains hook:** HT proposals are where the ecological and labour debate concentrates. Qualified verdict: review must produce India-specific evidence.
**Back:** *"Roundup Ready Flex: still under review, asked for Indian data."*

### Flashcard SNT-3123 — Off-target editing as a characterisation problem
**Front:** Why is off-target editing a characterisation problem rather than an unbounded hazard?
**Definition (learn):** Off-target editing cuts at sequences similar to the target. It is managed through guide design, high-fidelity Cas variants and whole-genome sequencing of candidate lines.
**Breakdown:**
- Off-target: similar sequences cut
- Controls: guide design, high-fidelity Cas, whole-genome sequencing
- Bounded by testing, not eliminated by it
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Characterisation (⚠️ owner 14 advanced §4).
**Links:** SNT-3108, SNT-3110, SNT-3096
**Trap:** Precision means no ecological concern. It does not.
**Mains hook:** Testing standards make gene editing governable. Qualified verdict: testing is necessary, not sufficient.
**Back:** *"Look-alike cuts: test the genome, do not assume the target was the only one."*

### Flashcard SNT-3124 — Pronuclear transfer and mitochondrial replacement: 2020 and 2021 PYQs
**Front:** What do the 2020 Q37 and 2021 Q66 PYQs test about mitochondrial disease and reproductive technology?
**Definition (learn):** Pronuclear transfer moves the intended parents' pronuclei into a donor zygote with healthy mitochondria to reduce the risk of specified mitochondrial disease. It does not repair all nuclear-gene disorders and raises consent, germline and regulation questions.
**Breakdown:**
- 2020 GS-I Q37: pronuclear transfer, mitochondrial disease prevention
- 2021 GS-I Q66: mitochondrial disease heredity and replacement therapy
- Germline and consent issues apply
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 Q37, 2021 Q66 (✅ owner 14 advanced routed evidence; keys unavailable).
**Links:** SNT-3072, SNT-3125
**Trap:** Mitochondrial replacement is not a repair of all nuclear genes.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"Pronuclear swap fixes mitochondria only, not every gene."*

### Flashcard SNT-3125 — RNA interference and Cas9 in the 2019 Prelims
**Front:** What do the 2019 Q96 (RNA interference) and Q99 (Cas9) PYQs test?
**Definition (learn):** RNA interference uses small RNA molecules to guide sequence-specific suppression of target messenger RNA, reducing gene expression. Cas9 is a protein that cuts DNA at guide-specified sites in gene editing.
**Breakdown:**
- RNAi: acts on messenger RNA, not DNA
- Cas9: nuclease that cuts DNA
- Different mechanisms, different applications
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 GS-I Q96 and Q99 (✅ owner 14 advanced routed evidence; keys unavailable).
**Links:** SNT-3066, SNT-3108, SNT-3124
**Trap:** Treating RNAi as CRISPR cutting.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"RNAi silences messages; Cas9 cuts DNA."*

### Flashcard SNT-3126 — Artificial chromosome and microsatellite PYQs
**Front:** What do the 2019 Q93 (artificial chromosome) and 2023 Q70 (microsatellite) PYQs test?
**Definition (learn):** An artificial chromosome is an engineered DNA carrier with chromosome-like elements. Microsatellites are short repeated DNA sequences used as genetic markers for relatedness and variation.
**Breakdown:**
- Artificial chromosome: engineered carrier
- Microsatellite: short repeats used as markers
- A marker shows variation, not the cause of a trait
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 GS-I Q93; 2023 GS-I Q70 (✅ owner 14 advanced routed evidence; keys unavailable).
**Links:** SNT-3124, SNT-3073
**Trap:** A microsatellite marker does not establish a trait's cause.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"Artificial chromosome carries DNA; microsatellite marks variation."*

### Flashcard SNT-3127 — 2019 GS-III PYQ on biotechnology and farmer living standards
**Front:** What does the 2019 GS-III Q16 ask, and what qualification should the answer carry?
**Definition (learn):** The 2019 GS-III Q16 (15 marks, 250 words) asks how biotechnology applications can improve farmer living standards.
**Breakdown:**
- Tissue culture, diagnostics, biofertilisers, biopesticides, climate-resilient traits
- Qualification: locally validated performance, price, extension, seed choice, biosafety
- Structure: context, dimensions, evidence, counterpoint, conclusion
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 GS-III Q16; 15 marks; 250 words (✅ owner 14 advanced PYQ integration).
**Links:** SNT-3070, SNT-3104, SNT-3083
**Trap:** Equating "biotech" with farmer benefit without field evidence.
**Mains hook:** Farmer benefit depends on local validation. Qualified verdict: inclusion of small farmers and seed access.
**Back:** *"Biotech helps farmers only when validated, priced, extended and safe."*

### Flashcard SNT-3128 — 2018 Prelims PYQs on GM mustard and CRISPR classification
**Front:** What do the 2018 Q63 and Q64 Prelims questions test?
**Definition (learn):** Q63 tests GM mustard, its genes and properties. Q64 tests technology terms, including CRISPR-Cas9, Belle II and blockchain, for context identification.
**Breakdown:**
- Q63: GM mustard developed in India, genes and properties
- Q64: CRISPR-Cas9 classification among technology terms
- Keys unavailable locally
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 GS-I Q63 and Q64 (✅ owner 14 advanced routed evidence).
**Links:** SNT-3113, SNT-3108
**Trap:** Mixing CRISPR with blockchain terms in a classification question.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"Q63 mustard facts; Q64 CRISPR among tech terms."*

### Flashcard SNT-3129 — 2020 embryo editing and stem-cell transfer PYQ
**Front:** What does the 2020 Q44 PYQ on embryo modification and stem-cell transfer test?
**Definition (learn):** The question tests the distinction between somatic therapy, germline alteration, stem-cell research and reproductive cloning. Ethical acceptability and legal permission must never be inferred from laboratory feasibility.
**Breakdown:**
- Somatic vs germline
- Stem-cell research vs reproductive cloning
- Feasibility is not permission
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 GS-I Q44 (✅ owner 14 advanced routed evidence; key unavailable).
**Links:** SNT-3072, SNT-3071, SNT-3124
**Trap:** A laboratory technique is not automatically legal in humans.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"Lab can, law may not: somatic is not germline."*

### Flashcard SNT-3130 — 2021 Bollgard I and II PYQ
**Front:** What does the 2021 Q67 Prelims PYQ on Bollgard I and II test?
**Definition (learn):** It tests Bt cry-gene insect resistance in Bollgard I and Bollgard II, and the fact that gene stacking delays but does not remove the need for refuges and monitoring.
**Breakdown:**
- Bollgard II: two cry genes stacked
- Refuge and pest monitoring still needed
- Insect resistance, not herbicide tolerance
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 GS-I Q67 (✅ owner 14 advanced routed evidence; key unavailable).
**Links:** SNT-3104, SNT-3106
**Trap:** Bollgard II does not remove resistance risk.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"Bollgard II stacks; refuges still matter."*

### GROUP — Vaccines, Monoclonal Antibodies and Biopharma (basic, owner 15)

### Flashcard SNT-3131 — Vaccine, monoclonal antibody and antiviral: three different interventions
**Front:** Distinguish vaccines, monoclonal antibodies and antiviral drugs by their mode of action.
**Definition (learn):** A vaccine stimulates the body's own immunity (active immunisation) and builds memory. A monoclonal antibody is a lab-made antibody that binds a target directly (passive). An antiviral is a small molecule that inhibits a step of viral replication, involving no immunity.
**Breakdown:**
- Vaccine: active, durable, memory
- mAb: passive, immediate, temporary, no memory
- Antiviral: pharmacological, no immune training
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Three-category frame (✅ owner 15 basic §1, §3).
**Links:** SNT-3141, SNT-3139, SNT-3140
**Trap:** "Monoclonal antibody and vaccine are the same thing."
**Mains hook:** Separate active and passive immunity in every biologics answer. Qualified verdict: antibodies do not replace population immunisation.
**Back:** *"Vaccine trains, antibody arrives, antiviral blocks."*

### Flashcard SNT-3132 — Live-attenuated vaccines and their contraindication
**Front:** What is a live-attenuated vaccine, give examples, and when is it contraindicated?
**Definition (learn):** A live-attenuated vaccine uses a weakened live pathogen that triggers a strong, often lifelong immune response. It is contraindicated in immunocompromised individuals and generally in pregnancy.
**Breakdown:**
- Examples: oral polio, MMR, BCG
- Strong immune response
- Contraindicated: immunocompromised; generally pregnancy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Examples and contraindication (✅ owner 15 basic §1 and §7).
**Links:** SNT-3133, SNT-3141
**Trap:** "All vaccines can be given to everyone." Live vaccines are a standard discriminator.
**Mains hook:** Platform choice affects who can be vaccinated. Qualified verdict: safety screening is part of delivery.
**Back:** *"Weakened live: strong and lasting, but not for the immunocompromised."*

### Flashcard SNT-3133 — Inactivated vaccines
**Front:** What is an inactivated vaccine, give examples, and why do they need boosters?
**Definition (learn):** An inactivated vaccine uses a killed pathogen. It cannot replicate and usually needs boosters.
**Breakdown:**
- Examples: injectable polio, Covaxin
- No replication risk
- Boosters needed
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Examples (✅ owner 15 basic §1).
**Links:** SNT-3132, SNT-3134
**Trap:** Killed does not mean ineffective; it means no replication.
**Mains hook:** Platform trade-offs include stability and boosters. Qualified verdict: choose by context.
**Back:** *"Killed, cannot replicate, needs boosters."*

### Flashcard SNT-3134 — Subunit vaccines and adjuvants
**Front:** What is a subunit vaccine, and what is an adjuvant?
**Definition (learn):** A subunit vaccine presents a specific antigen or protein part. An adjuvant is a substance added to enhance and shape the immune response. Subunit vaccines often need one.
**Breakdown:**
- Examples: hepatitis B, Corbevax
- Adjuvant: enhances response
- Adjuvants are a technological bottleneck
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Examples and adjuvant definition (✅ owner 15 basic §1, §2).
**Links:** SNT-3133, SNT-3143
**Trap:** Confusing an adjuvant with an antigen.
**Mains hook:** Adjuvant supply shapes platform cost. Qualified verdict: subunit safety, with adjuvant-related design cost.
**Back:** *"Antigen piece plus adjuvant booster for the immune signal."*

### Flashcard SNT-3135 — Toxoid vaccines: DPT and the toxin
**Front:** What is a toxoid vaccine, and what does it protect against?
**Definition (learn):** A toxoid vaccine uses a chemically inactivated bacterial toxin. Immunity is against the toxin, not the bacterium. Tetanus and diphtheria are the classic examples.
**Breakdown:**
- Target: toxin
- Examples: tetanus, diphtheria
- Explains why DPT protection is toxin-directed
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Toxoid definition (✅ owner 15 basic §1).
**Links:** SNT-3134
**Trap:** A toxoid is not a whole bacterium vaccine.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"Toxoid: immunity against the toxin, not the germ."*

### Flashcard SNT-3136 — Virus-like particles and Cervavac
**Front:** What is a virus-like particle vaccine, and which Indian HPV vaccine is an example?
**Definition (learn):** A VLP vaccine uses an empty protein shell that mimics the virus and contains no genetic material, so it produces strong antibodies without infectivity. Cervavac is India's HPV VLP vaccine.
**Breakdown:**
- No genetic material
- No infectivity
- Example: Cervavac (HPV)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** VLP and Cervavac (✅ owner 15 basic §1, advanced §3).
**Links:** SNT-3133, SNT-3137
**Trap:** VLP contains no genetic material, unlike mRNA or DNA platforms.
**Mains hook:** VLPs give safety with strong antibody response. Qualified verdict: cost and cold chain still matter.
**Back:** *"Empty shell, no genes, strong antibodies: Cervavac."*

### Flashcard SNT-3137 — Viral-vector vaccines and Covishield
**Front:** What is a viral-vector vaccine, and what does "engineered" mean?
**Definition (learn):** A viral-vector vaccine uses an engineered, usually replication-deficient virus that carries the antigen code. The body makes antigen and mounts a response. Covishield (ChAdOx1) is an example.
**Breakdown:**
- Vector: engineered, usually replication-deficient
- Antigen code delivered
- Example: Covishield (ChAdOx1)
- Pre-existing immunity to the vector can reduce effectiveness
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Vector and Covishield (✅ owner 15 basic §1).
**Links:** SNT-3136, SNT-3138, SNT-3153
**Trap:** "A viral vector is a harmless virus." It is engineered for safety, not naturally harmless.
**Mains hook:** Vector platforms are reusable for new antigens. Qualified verdict: pre-existing immunity limits reuse.
**Back:** *"Engineered carrier, antigen code inside, Covishield."*

### Flashcard SNT-3138 — mRNA and DNA vaccines: ZyCoV-D and needle-free delivery
**Front:** How do mRNA and DNA vaccines differ in their cellular route, and what is ZyCoV-D?
**Definition (learn):** mRNA is delivered in lipid nanoparticles and acts in the cytoplasm. DNA must reach the nucleus to be transcribed, which is why DNA vaccines often need needle-free jet delivery. ZyCoV-D is India's DNA vaccine, described in the owner as the world's first approved DNA vaccine for humans.
**Breakdown:**
- mRNA: cytoplasm, lipid nanoparticle
- DNA: nucleus, often jet injection
- ZyCoV-D: needle-free jet delivery
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ZyCoV-D and "world's first approved DNA vaccine for humans" (✅ owner 15 basic §1 as owner-stated; ⚠️ not separately verified).
**Links:** SNT-3137, SNT-3153
**Trap:** "mRNA and DNA vaccines work the same way."
**Mains hook:** Route determines delivery design. Qualified verdict: cold chain and delivery shape reach.
**Back:** *"mRNA works in the cytoplasm; DNA must reach the nucleus."*

### Flashcard SNT-3139 — Monoclonal antibodies: hybridoma and humanisation suffixes
**Front:** How are monoclonal antibodies made, and what do -ximab, -zumab and -umab mean?
**Definition (learn):** A monoclonal antibody is directed against a single epitope. It was historically made by hybridoma technology (fusing a B cell and a myeloma cell) and now mainly by recombinant mammalian cell culture. Suffixes show the degree of humanisation: -ximab (chimeric), -zumab (humanised), -umab (fully human).
**Breakdown:**
- Single epitope specificity
- Hybridoma: historical method
- Recombinant mammalian culture: current method
- Suffixes: -ximab, -zumab, -umab
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Method and suffixes (✅ owner 15 basic §2).
**Links:** SNT-3131, SNT-3140
**Trap:** Chimeric, humanised and fully human are not interchangeable labels.
**Mains hook:** Humanisation reduces immunogenicity. Qualified verdict: affordability still limits access.
**Back:** *"Ximab is part-mouse, zumab mostly human, umab fully human."*

### Flashcard SNT-3140 — Antivirals as a third category
**Front:** Why must antivirals not be folded into the vaccine-or-antibody binary?
**Definition (learn):** Antivirals are small molecules that inhibit a viral replication step, such as entry, polymerase or protease. They treat infection without active or passive immunity.
**Breakdown:**
- Target: viral replication steps
- No immune training, no antibody supply
- Third therapeutic category
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Category (✅ owner 15 basic §1, §3).
**Links:** SNT-3131, SNT-3139
**Trap:** Calling an antiviral "a vaccine" or "an antibody."
**Mains hook:** Three categories, three roles. Qualified verdict: each role needs its own evidence.
**Back:** *"Antiviral blocks the virus; no immunity involved."*

### Flashcard SNT-3141 — Active immunisation, memory and passive therapy
**Front:** Compare active and passive immunisation on onset, duration and memory.
**Definition (learn):** Active immunisation is generated by the person's own immune response, slower to develop but durable because memory cells form. Passive immunisation is supplied from outside: immediate but temporary, with no memory.
**Breakdown:**
- Active: slow onset, durable, memory
- Passive: immediate, temporary, no memory
- Vaccines are active; mAbs are passive
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Definitions (✅ owner 15 basic §2 and §3).
**Links:** SNT-3131, SNT-3132
**Trap:** A monoclonal antibody gives long-term memory.
**Mains hook:** Use onset and memory in any policy choice. Qualified verdict: outbreak tools may need both.
**Back:** *"Active builds memory; passive delivers now and fades."*

### Flashcard SNT-3142 — Biologics, biosimilars and generics
**Front:** Why is a biosimilar not a generic?
**Definition (learn):** A generic is a chemically identical copy of a small-molecule drug, approved on bioequivalence. A biosimilar is highly similar but never identical to a biologic, and needs comparative analytical, non-clinical and usually clinical data.
**Breakdown:**
- Biologics: large molecules made in living cells
- Generic: identical copy of a small molecule
- Biosimilar: highly similar, not identical
- Costs more to develop
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Definitions (✅ owner 15 basic §2 and advanced §3).
**Links:** SNT-3139, SNT-3143, SNT-3155
**Trap:** "A biosimilar is the biologic equivalent of a generic."
**Mains hook:** The generics model does not transfer to biologics. Qualified verdict: approval needs more evidence.
**Back:** *"Generic is identical; biosimilar is highly similar, never identical."*

### Flashcard SNT-3143 — Cold chain and platform equity
**Front:** How does cold-chain need affect the equity of vaccine platforms?
**Definition (learn):** Platform thermostability determines cold-chain cost and reach. mRNA vaccines historically needed ultra-cold chains, while protein-subunit and inactivated vaccines suit standard 2-8 °C infrastructure, which matters in low-income settings.
**Breakdown:**
- Ultra-cold: mRNA historically
- 2-8 °C: subunit, inactivated
- Reach depends on logistics, not just science
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Cold-chain comparison (✅ owner 15 basic §2; ⚠️ 2-8 °C standard owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-3152, SNT-3134
**Trap:** The best science does not always give the widest reach.
**Mains hook:** Platform strategy is public-health policy. Qualified verdict: pick platforms the supply chain can carry.
**Back:** *"Ultra-cold limits reach; 2-8 degrees reaches more people."*

### Flashcard SNT-3144 — CDSCO and the DCGI
**Front:** What does CDSCO regulate, who heads it as licensing authority, and under which Act?
**Definition (learn):** CDSCO is India's national drug and biological products regulator. The Drugs Controller General of India (DCGI) is the Central Licensing Authority, under the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940 and its Rules, read with NDCTR 2019.
**Breakdown:**
- Regulates vaccines and biologicals
- DCGI: Central Licensing Authority
- Act: Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** CDSCO, DCGI, 1940 Act (✅ owner 15 basic §2 and §4).
**Links:** SNT-3145, SNT-3146, SNT-3150
**Trap:** ICMR approves vaccines for market. It does not; CDSCO does.
**Mains hook:** Regulation is the approval gate. Qualified verdict: approval must cite the official list.
**Back:** *"CDSCO and the DCGI approve; the 1940 Act and NDCTR 2019 govern."*

### Flashcard SNT-3145 — NDCTR 2019
**Front:** What is NDCTR 2019, and what does it govern?
**Definition (learn):** The New Drugs and Clinical Trials Rules, 2019 set the framework for clinical-trial permissions and new-drug evaluation used by CDSCO.
**Breakdown:**
- Clinical trial permissions
- New-drug evaluation
- Linked to the 1940 Act
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** NDCTR 2019 (✅ owner 15 basic §2, §4).
**Links:** SNT-3144, SNT-3146
**Trap:** Confusing NDCTR with the Drugs and Cosmetics Act itself.
**Mains hook:** Trials, approval and surveillance form one chain. Qualified verdict: evidence must precede approval.
**Back:** *"NDCTR 2019: how trials are permitted and new drugs judged."*

### Flashcard SNT-3146 — ICMR generates evidence, it does not approve
**Front:** What is ICMR's role in vaccines and biomedical research?
**Definition (learn):** ICMR is India's apex biomedical research body, generating research, trial networks, surveillance and public-health evidence. It is not the statutory drug-approval authority.
**Breakdown:**
- Research, trials, surveillance
- Evidence generation
- Not the approver; CDSCO is
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ICMR role (✅ owner 15 basic §2, §6).
**Links:** SNT-3144, SNT-3145, SNT-3075
**Trap:** ICMR and CDSCO are interchangeable.
**Mains hook:** Evidence generation and approval are separate functions. Qualified verdict: independent trials support trust.
**Back:** *"ICMR studies; CDSCO signs."*

### Flashcard SNT-3147 — Mission COVID Suraksha (29 Nov 2020)
**Front:** What was Mission COVID Suraksha, when was it launched, and what did it aim to do?
**Definition (learn):** A DBT-led mission launched on 29 Nov 2020 to accelerate Indian COVID-19 vaccine development from preclinical work to deployment support.
**Breakdown:**
- Launch: 29 Nov 2020 (PIB)
- Lead: DBT
- End-to-end support
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 29 Nov 2020 (✅ owner 15 basic §2, §8; PIB PDF cited in owner).
**Links:** SNT-3148, SNT-3159
**Trap:** A mission announcement is not a vaccine approval.
**Mains hook:** Mission-mode state support builds capability. Qualified verdict: capacity needs follow-on manufacturing.
**Back:** *"Twenty-nine November 2020: DBT's vaccine accelerator."*

### Flashcard SNT-3148 — BIRAC's Mission Implementation Unit under Mission COVID Suraksha
**Front:** What role did BIRAC play in Mission COVID Suraksha, and what is the 1 June 2021 date?
**Definition (learn):** DBT identified BIRAC to implement the mission through a Mission Implementation Unit. BIRAC's call page shows an announcement on 01 Jun 2021 for enhancing capacity for COVID-19 vaccine development.
**Breakdown:**
- Implementer: BIRAC via Mission Implementation Unit
- Call page: 01 Jun 2021
- Implementation, not regulation
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** MIU and 01 Jun 2021 (✅ owner 15 basic §6, §8; BIRAC call page cited in owner).
**Links:** SNT-3147, SNT-3075
**Trap:** BIRAC approves vaccines. It does not.
**Mains hook:** Implementation support follows declarations. Qualified verdict: follow-on support matters.
**Back:** *"BIRAC implements the mission; CDSCO approves the vaccine."*

### Flashcard SNT-3149 — Phases I to IV and AEFI surveillance
**Front:** Name the four clinical phases and the post-rollout surveillance system.
**Definition (learn):** Phase I tests safety in small numbers. Phase II tests dose and immunogenicity. Phase III tests efficacy in large populations. Phase IV is post-marketing surveillance and pharmacovigilance. AEFI (adverse events following immunisation) surveillance continues after rollout.
**Breakdown:**
- Phase I: safety, small n
- Phase II: dose and immunogenicity
- Phase III: efficacy, large population
- Phase IV: post-marketing; AEFI
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Phases and AEFI (✅ owner 15 basic §3 item 7).
**Links:** SNT-3144, SNT-3151
**Trap:** Efficacy from Phase III is not field effectiveness.
**Mains hook:** Surveillance after approval is part of the chain. Qualified verdict: evidence continues after launch.
**Back:** *"One safety, two dose, three efficacy, four surveillance."*

### Flashcard SNT-3150 — NexCAR19: India's first indigenous CAR-T therapy (4 Apr 2024)
**Front:** What is NexCAR19, when was it launched, and what kind of therapy is it?
**Definition (learn):** NexCAR19 is India's first indigenously developed CAR-T-cell therapy, developed through an IIT Bombay, Tata Memorial Centre and ImmunoACT collaboration. It is a cell or gene therapy, not a vaccine or monoclonal antibody.
**Breakdown:**
- Launch: 4 Apr 2024 (PIB)
- Type: cell/gene therapy
- Collaboration: IIT Bombay, Tata Memorial Centre, ImmunoACT
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 4 Apr 2024 and collaborators (✅ owner 15 basic §8 and PIB link cited in owner).
**Links:** SNT-3151, SNT-3152, SNT-3157
**Trap:** Launch date is not a current access or price claim. No 2026 approval scope located.
**Mains hook:** Indigenous complex therapy shows movement beyond manufacturing others' biologics. Qualified verdict: access is a separate question.
**Back:** *"NexCAR19: Indian CAR-T, launched April 2024, not a vaccine."*

### Flashcard SNT-3151 — Efficacy versus effectiveness
**Front:** Distinguish vaccine efficacy from effectiveness.
**Definition (learn):** Efficacy is performance under trial conditions. Effectiveness is performance in field use, depending on coverage, storage, variants and population health.
**Breakdown:**
- Efficacy: trial
- Effectiveness: field
- Conflation is a common analytical error
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Definitions (✅ owner 15 basic §7).
**Links:** SNT-3149, SNT-3143
**Trap:** A trial efficacy figure is the real-world effectiveness.
**Mains hook:** Specify which measure you quote. Qualified verdict: effectiveness needs surveillance.
**Back:** *"Efficacy is the trial; effectiveness is the street."*

### GROUP — Vaccines, Monoclonal Antibodies and Biopharma (advanced, owner 15)

### Flashcard SNT-3152 — Platform choice as an equity decision
**Front:** Why is platform choice a public-health equity decision?
**Definition (learn):** A scientifically superior platform that needs ultra-cold logistics may protect fewer people in low-infrastructure settings than a sturdier subunit or inactivated product. Platform strategy is public-health policy.
**Breakdown:**
- Thermostability sets reach
- Cold chain sets cost
- Sturdier platforms may protect more people
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Equity argument (⚠️ owner 15 advanced §3).
**Links:** SNT-3143, SNT-3153
**Trap:** Best science equals widest protection.
**Mains hook:** Use platform reach as the tie-breaker. Qualified verdict: choose platforms the chain can carry.
**Back:** *"Reach follows the cold chain, not the lab bench."*

### Flashcard SNT-3153 — Platform reusability and antigen-agnostic manufacturing
**Front:** Why are mRNA and vector platforms a strategic asset?
**Definition (learn):** Their manufacturing process is largely antigen-agnostic: a new pathogen needs a new insert, not a new factory. This underlies "100 Days Mission"-style pandemic preparedness thinking.
**Breakdown:**
- New pathogen: new insert
- Sovereign platform capability
- Relevant to pandemic readiness
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Reusability (⚠️ owner 15 advanced §3; "100 Days Mission" as owner-stated framing, not fetched).
**Links:** SNT-3137, SNT-3138
**Trap:** Platform reusability does not mean instant licensed products.
**Mains hook:** Platforms give speed only with manufacturing readiness. Qualified verdict: capacity is still needed.
**Back:** *"New pathogen, new insert, same factory."*

### Flashcard SNT-3154 — Speed versus evidence in outbreak response
**Front:** What is the speed-versus-evidence debate in vaccine regulation?
**Definition (learn):** Outbreaks create pressure for fast approval, but biologics need rigorous evidence and quality control. Emergency pathways change speed and route, not the need for evidence.
**Breakdown:**
- Pressure: outbreak urgency
- Requirement: evidence and quality control
- Emergency route: speed, not zero evidence
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Debate (⚠️ owner 15 advanced §5; owner basic §7 trap).
**Links:** SNT-3144, SNT-3145, SNT-3149
**Trap:** "Emergency pathways mean no evidence requirement."
**Mains hook:** Balance speed and rigour. Qualified verdict: transparent evidence supports trust.
**Back:** *"Fast lane, same evidence."*

### Flashcard SNT-3155 — Biologics regulation and public versus private capacity
**Front:** Why are biologics regulation and India's ecosystem not one undifferentiated field?
**Definition (learn):** Vaccines, recombinant products and antibodies all fall within biological-product oversight but not under identical scientific considerations. India depends on both public research and private manufacturing.
**Breakdown:**
- Different scientific considerations per product type
- Public research (ICMR, DBT) and private manufacturing
- Neither alone is enough
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Framing (✅ owner 15 advanced §5).
**Links:** SNT-3142, SNT-3146, SNT-3152
**Trap:** Treating all biologics as one regulatory class.
**Mains hook:** Map product type to scientific test. Qualified verdict: ecosystem depends on both sectors.
**Back:** *"One biologics umbrella, many scientific rules."*

### Flashcard SNT-3156 — "Vaccine hub of the world" as a qualitative phrase
**Front:** Why should "vaccine hub of the world" be used qualitatively?
**Definition (learn):** The phrase is useful only as a qualitative description unless an official percentage or output figure is verified. Manufacturing scale does not automatically equal innovation depth.
**Breakdown:**
- Qualitative use
- Scale is not innovation
- Platform R&D and regulatory science must also deepen
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Qualifier (✅ owner 15 advanced §10).
**Links:** SNT-3152, SNT-3155
**Trap:** Quoting a share of world vaccine supply without a source.
**Mains hook:** Measure capability by platform and quality, not dose count. Qualified verdict: depth needs R&D.
**Back:** *"Hub is a word; verify the figure before you say it."*

### Flashcard SNT-3157 — NexCAR19 as analytical evidence
**Front:** How should NexCAR19 be used in a Mains answer?
**Definition (learn):** Analyse NexCAR19 as cell or gene therapy and translational biomanufacturing, not as a vaccine or mAb. It shows origination of a complex therapy, while access and cost remain separate questions.
**Breakdown:**
- Shows movement from manufacturing others' biologics to originating a complex therapy
- Autologous therapies face cost and infrastructure barriers
- Launch is not access
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 4 Apr 2024 launch; approval scope unverified (✅ owner 15 advanced §13).
**Links:** SNT-3150, SNT-3155
**Trap:** Citing NexCAR19 as proof of affordable access.
**Mains hook:** Capability claim plus access qualifier. Qualified verdict: platform-level capability is solid, product-level access is not.
**Back:** *"Capability shown, access still to prove."*

### Flashcard SNT-3158 — Verify product approvals on CDSCO before claiming them
**Front:** Why should product-level approval claims be verified on CDSCO's own records?
**Definition (learn):** At the verification date (2 Aug 2026), CDSCO's approval index was reachable but its tables were not machine-readable. No product-level approval date is asserted in the owner, including for NexCAR19 and any Indian mRNA vaccine.
**Breakdown:**
- Cite cdsco.gov.in for each product
- Argue capability at platform level where evidence is solid
- Do not extend a launch date into an access claim
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Verification discipline (✅ owner 15 basic §8; owner 15 advanced §13).
**Links:** SNT-3150, SNT-3144
**Trap:** Secondary reporting taken as an approval.
**Mains hook:** Discipline on evidence signals rigour. Qualified verdict: check the regulator's list.
**Back:** *"Check CDSCO's list, not the headline."*

### Flashcard SNT-3159 — Mission COVID Suraksha as mission-mode capability
**Front:** What does Mission COVID Suraksha show about India's vaccine policy style?
**Definition (learn):** It shows mission-mode vaccine capability built beyond routine grant funding, with follow-on manufacturing and translational support needed after declaration.
**Breakdown:**
- Mission-mode, not routine grants
- Follow-on manufacturing and translation needed
- Declarations are not outcomes
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 29 Nov 2020 and 01 Jun 2021 (✅ owner 15 advanced §13).
**Links:** SNT-3147, SNT-3148, SNT-3076
**Trap:** A mission's name is evidence of product output.
**Mains hook:** Mission design plus delivery capacity. Qualified verdict: sustain beyond the emergency.
**Back:** *"Mission first, then factories, then doses."*

### Flashcard SNT-3160 — 2020 Prelims PYQ on pneumococcal conjugate vaccines
**Front:** What does the 2020 Q45 PYQ on pneumococcal conjugate vaccines test?
**Definition (learn):** A PCV couples a bacterial polysaccharide to a protein carrier, producing a stronger T-cell-dependent response and memory in young children than plain polysaccharide. It targets pneumococcal disease, not every pneumonia cause.
**Breakdown:**
- Polysaccharide plus protein carrier
- T-cell-dependent response
- Targets pneumococcal disease only
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 GS-I Q45 (✅ owner 15 advanced PYQ integration; key unavailable).
**Links:** SNT-3134, SNT-3135
**Trap:** PCV covers all pneumonia.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"Carrier-linked sugar: stronger memory, pneumococcus only."*

### Flashcard SNT-3161 — 2021 Prelims PYQ on recombinant vector vaccines
**Front:** What does the 2021 Q65 PYQ on recombinant vector vaccines test?
**Definition (learn):** Genetic engineering places antigen instructions in a controlled vector. The recipient's cells make antigen and generate active immunity. A vector platform is neither a live wild pathogen nor a monoclonal antibody.
**Breakdown:**
- Vector: controlled carrier
- Recipient cells make antigen
- Active immunity
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 GS-I Q65 (✅ owner 15 advanced PYQ integration; key unavailable).
**Links:** SNT-3137, SNT-3131
**Trap:** Calling a vector vaccine a monoclonal antibody.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"Vector carries the code; the body makes the antigen."*

### Flashcard SNT-3162 — 2022 Prelims PYQ on COVID-19 vaccine platforms
**Front:** What does the 2022 Q39 PYQ test about COVID-19 platforms?
**Definition (learn):** It tests mRNA, viral-vector and inactivated-pathogen platforms, and how each presents or produces antigen by a different route. Product approvals must be cited separately.
**Breakdown:**
- mRNA: cytoplasm route
- Viral vector: engineered carrier
- Inactivated: killed whole pathogen
- Product claims need a regulator source
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 GS-I Q39 (✅ owner 15 advanced PYQ integration; key unavailable).
**Links:** SNT-3137, SNT-3138, SNT-3133
**Trap:** Mixing platform routes with individual product approval.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"mRNA, vector, inactivated: three routes to one antigen."*

### Flashcard SNT-3163 — 2025 Prelims PYQ on monoclonal antibodies (answer not recorded)
**Front:** What does the 2025 Q48 Prelims PYQ on monoclonal antibodies test?
**Definition (learn):** It tests the monoclonal antibody concept, namely passive, targeted biological therapy. An official Set-A key is held locally, but no answer is recorded or inferred here.
**Breakdown:**
- Test point: mAb concept
- Passive, targeted
- Not a vaccine
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-I Q48 (✅ owner 15 basic PYQ integration; key available locally, answer not recorded).
**Links:** SNT-3139, SNT-3131
**Trap:** Treating a mAb as active immunisation.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable beyond concept recall.
**Back:** *"Q48: mAb is passive and targeted, not a vaccine."*

### Flashcard SNT-3164 — 2022 GS-III Q16 on vaccine development principles
**Front:** What does the 2022 GS-III Q16 ask about vaccine development and Indian COVID-19 approaches?
**Definition (learn):** The 2022 GS-III Q16 (15 marks, 250 words) asks for vaccine development principles and Indian COVID-19 vaccine approaches. It tests the platform logic and the approval chain.
**Breakdown:**
- Principles: antigen, platform, immunogenicity, safety
- Indian approaches: multiple platforms
- Qualification: efficacy vs effectiveness, cold chain, access
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 GS-III Q16; 15 marks; 250 words (✅ owner 15 advanced PYQ integration).
**Links:** SNT-3131, SNT-3149, SNT-3151
**Trap:** Listing products without the platform principle.
**Mains hook:** Principles first, then platforms, then access. Qualified verdict: efficacy is not effectiveness.
**Back:** *"Principle, platform, approval, access."*

## SCIENCE AND TECHNOLOGY — Part 4: Nanotechnology, IPR, EVs, Drones, Materials and Physics Fundamentals

> Scope: owner files 16–21 (basic and advanced) of `upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Science-and-Technology/`. Tags: `(✅ owner NN)` = grounded in the repository owner (which itself cites official pages); `⚠️` = standard knowledge or owner analysis not independently confirmed in this pass. Web fetches of Wikipedia and other pages failed during this build (socket errors), so no card carries a `(✅ <fetched source>)` tag. Time-varying facts carry their as-of date. Current date for this build: 2026-11-01.

### GROUP — Nanotechnology and Applications

### Flashcard SNT-4001 — Nanotechnology: size band and the two core ideas
**Front:** What size band defines nanotechnology, and why do materials behave differently at that scale?
**Definition (learn):** Science, engineering and technology conducted at about 1–100 nm. Matter can behave differently there because quantum effects and a high surface-area-to-volume ratio alter its properties (✅ owner 16 §1–2).
**Breakdown:**
- Size band: about 1–100 nm (1 nm = 10⁻⁹ m, ⚠️ SI standard).
- Lever 1: quantum effects change electron and light behaviour.
- Lever 2: surface-area effect raises reactivity, adsorption and catalytic potential.
- Chain to memorise: size → property change → application.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** INST Mohali frames nanotechnology on the 1–100 nm band (✅ owner 16 §6). The owner cites DST and INST pages verified 16 Jul 2026.
**Links:** SNT-4002, SNT-4003, SNT-4009, SNT-4024.
**Trap:** "Nanotechnology is only miniaturisation." The exam point is the functional property change, not the reduction in size alone (✅ owner 16 §7).
**Mains hook:** Open with size → property → application, then qualify that laboratory effects still need scale-up and safety evidence.
**Back:** *"One to a hundred nanometres: size changes the rules, through quantum and surface."*

### Flashcard SNT-4002 — Surface-area effect and reactivity
**Front:** What is the surface-area effect, and why does it matter for catalysis, adsorption and reactivity?
**Definition (learn):** As particle size falls, the exposed surface area relative to volume rises sharply, so a larger share of atoms sits at the surface and drives reactivity, catalysis and adsorption (✅ owner 16 §2–3).
**Breakdown:**
- Fraction of surface atoms rises steeply at the nanoscale.
- Consequences: greater catalytic action, adsorption and mechanical performance.
- Used in catalysts, filtration adsorbents and sensors.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives the mechanism as items 1–2 of its nanoscale mechanism (✅ owner 16 §3).
**Links:** SNT-4001, SNT-4019, SNT-4016.
**Trap:** "Higher reactivity always means a better product." Reactivity is also tied to toxicity and environmental-fate questions (✅ owner 16 §5, nanotoxicology limitation).
**Mains hook:** Use surface-area logic to explain why nano-catalysts and adsorbents work, then state the exposure risk that comes with the same property.
**Back:** *"More surface, more action, more scrutiny."*

### Flashcard SNT-4003 — Quantum confinement and quantum dots
**Front:** What is quantum confinement, and why does a quantum dot emit a different colour when its size changes?
**Definition (learn):** Quantum confinement changes the electronic and optical behaviour of a nanocrystal once its size is small enough. A quantum dot's emission depends strongly on size; smaller dots emit shorter (bluer) wavelengths (✅ owner 16 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Larger dot → longer (redder) emission; smaller dot → shorter (bluer) emission.
- The classic demonstration of quantum confinement.
- Uses in imaging, displays, sensing and diagnostics (✅ owner 16 §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Size-dependent optical emission is the defining property (✅ owner 16 §6).
**Links:** SNT-4001, SNT-4025.
**Trap:** "Bigger dot, bluer light" is reversed. Also, quantum dots are not only a physics-lab topic; their optics matter for imaging and displays.
**Mains hook:** Use quantum dots as the cleanest example of size controlling function, then note the toxicity question for heavy-metal cores (⚠️ standard).
**Back:** *"Small dot, blue light; big dot, red light."*

### Flashcard SNT-4004 — Graphene
**Front:** What is graphene, and how is it structurally different from a carbon nanotube?
**Definition (learn):** Graphene is a single-atom-thick sheet of carbon atoms arranged in a hexagonal lattice (✅ owner 16 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Two-dimensional sheet; one nanoscale dimension makes it a nanoplate/nanosheet in the nano-object taxonomy (⚠️ owner 16 advanced §2).
- Relevance: strength and conductivity; used in electronics and composites research.
- Member of the carbon-allotrope set with CNT, fullerene and diamond/graphite (⚠️ owner 16 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sheet versus tube is the core distinction (✅ owner 16 §6).
**Links:** SNT-4005, SNT-4006, SNT-4009, SNT-4024.
**Trap:** Graphene and CNTs are both carbon nanomaterials but are not the same form. One is a flat sheet; the other is a cylindrical tube (✅ owner 16 §7).
**Mains hook:** Use the sheet-versus-tube distinction to show why no single carbon nanomaterial replaces all others.
**Back:** *"Sheet flat, tube rolled."*

### Flashcard SNT-4005 — Carbon nanotube (CNT)
**Front:** What is a carbon nanotube, what are its main uses, and what does a PYQ on CNTs test?
**Definition (learn):** A cylindrical carbon nanostructure with high strength and useful electrical and thermal properties (✅ owner 16 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Strength and conductivity used in composites and conductive materials.
- Sensor and biomedical research applications (✅ owner 16 historical PYQ routing).
- Two nano-dimensions define a tube within the nano-object taxonomy (⚠️ owner 16 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Prelims GS-I 2020 Q41 asked CNT uses in medicine, biosensors and industry. The official key is not held locally, so no answer is recorded here (✅ owner 16 §9).
**Links:** SNT-4004, SNT-4006, SNT-4023.
**Trap:** A CNT used in a product does not establish clinical safety. Use needs material-specific exposure and lifecycle assessment (✅ owner 16 advanced routed evidence).
**Mains hook:** Present CNTs as strong, conductive building blocks, then qualify with toxicology and cost.
**Back:** *"Rolled sheet, strong tube."*

### Flashcard SNT-4006 — Fullerene (C₆₀) and the carbon-allotrope set
**Front:** What is fullerene (C₆₀), and which carbon forms make up the standard allotrope set?
**Definition (learn):** A closed, hollow carbon cage, often called a "buckyball" (⚠️ owner 16 §2, standard knowledge).
**Breakdown:**
- Standard allotrope set cited for Prelims: diamond, graphite, graphene, carbon nanotubes and fullerene (⚠️ owner 16 §2).
- Fullerene is neither a sheet nor a tube; it is a cage.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** C₆₀ is the common fullerene (⚠️ standard knowledge, 60 carbon atoms).
**Links:** SNT-4004, SNT-4005.
**Trap:** Including fullerene under "tubes" or "sheets" is the usual error. It is a cage structure.
**Mains hook:** Rarely a Mains item; use only as a Prelims classification anchor.
**Back:** *"Sixty-carbon football."*

### Flashcard SNT-4007 — Dendrimer
**Front:** What is a dendrimer, and what is it used for?
**Definition (learn):** A precisely branched, tree-like polymer with internal cavities and a controllable surface (⚠️ owner 16 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Internal cavities can hold drug molecules (encapsulation).
- Controllable surface allows targeting.
- Studied for drug encapsulation and targeted delivery (⚠️ owner 16 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level analytical note; no Indian programme figure is attached (⚠️).
**Links:** SNT-4025, SNT-4008.
**Trap:** A dendrimer is a branched polymer, not a liquid droplet dispersion. Do not confuse it with a nanoemulsion.
**Mains hook:** Cite as a delivery-platform example in health answers, with the caveat that clinical translation is still required.
**Back:** *"Branch, cavity, target."*

### Flashcard SNT-4008 — Nanoemulsion
**Front:** What is a nanoemulsion, and where is it used?
**Definition (learn):** A kinetically stable dispersion of nanoscale droplets of one liquid in another (⚠️ owner 16 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Droplets are liquid, not solid particles.
- Basis of many agricultural formulations, food and cosmetic products (⚠️ owner 16 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level definition; no statistic attached.
**Links:** SNT-4007, SNT-4017.
**Trap:** A nanoemulsion is a liquid-in-liquid dispersion. It is not a solid nanoparticle and not a nano-fertiliser by default.
**Mains hook:** Use as a formulation-science example for agrochemical delivery, then note residue and dose questions.
**Back:** *"Droplets, not solids."*

### Flashcard SNT-4009 — Nano-object taxonomy: nanoparticle, nanotube, nanoplate, nanomaterial, nanotechnology
**Front:** How do nanoparticle, nano-object, nanomaterial and nanotechnology differ in dimensions and meaning?
**Definition (learn):** A nanoparticle has all three external dimensions at the nanoscale. A nanofibre or nanotube has two; a nanoplate or nanosheet has one. Together these are nano-objects. A nanomaterial is any material with nanoscale internal or surface structure. Nanotechnology is the deliberate design and application of matter at that scale (⚠️ owner 16 §2; owner 16 advanced §2).
**Breakdown:**
- 3 nanoscale dimensions → nanoparticle.
- 2 nanoscale dimensions → nanotube or nanofibre.
- 1 nanoscale dimension → nanoplate or nanosheet.
- Nanomaterial is the broader class; nanotechnology is the engineering practice.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dimension counts are the classification test (⚠️ owner 16 §2).
**Links:** SNT-4001, SNT-4004, SNT-4005.
**Trap:** Treating nanoparticle, nanomaterial and nanotechnology as synonyms is a precision error (⚠️ owner 16 §2).
**Mains hook:** Use the taxonomy to open a definitional paragraph before moving to applications.
**Back:** *"Three small: particle. Two small: tube. One small: sheet."*

### Flashcard SNT-4010 — Nanocomposite
**Front:** What is a nanocomposite?
**Definition (learn):** A composite material in which one phase has nanoscale dimensions that modify the bulk properties of the whole (✅ owner 16 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- A bulk matrix plus a nanoscale phase.
- Purpose: improve performance such as strength or conductivity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner definition only; no figure attached.
**Links:** SNT-4005, SNT-4011.
**Trap:** A nanocomposite is not automatically stronger. Performance depends on how well the nanoscale phase is dispersed (⚠️ standard materials knowledge).
**Mains hook:** Present nanocomposites as a route from nano-properties to structural and electronic applications, with dispersion and cost as limits.
**Back:** *"Nano phase inside a bulk matrix."*

### Flashcard SNT-4011 — Top-down and bottom-up fabrication
**Front:** What is the difference between top-down and bottom-up nanofabrication, with examples?
**Definition (learn):** Top-down carves structures from bulk material. Bottom-up assembles structures from atoms or molecules (⚠️ owner 16 §3, item 5).
**Breakdown:**
- Top-down: lithography, milling, etching. Precise but wasteful.
- Bottom-up: self-assembly, chemical vapour deposition, sol-gel. Scalable but harder to control.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level classification without numerical yield data.
**Links:** SNT-4009, SNT-4010.
**Trap:** Top-down is not always cheap, and bottom-up is not always precise. The owner's trade-off is precision versus scalability.
**Mains hook:** Use the trade-off to explain why scale-up is a manufacturing problem, not only a laboratory discovery.
**Back:** *"Carve it or build it: precise versus scalable."*

### Flashcard SNT-4012 — Nanotoxicology and exposure safety
**Front:** What is nanotoxicology, and why is nanoparticle safety not settled?
**Definition (learn):** The study of possible biological and environmental effects of nanomaterials. It is an active, material-specific field, not a settled verdict (✅ owner 16 advanced §2, §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Factors that matter: size, shape, coating, dose, exposure route, persistence and bioaccumulation (✅ owner 16 advanced Evidence units).
- Exposure routes: inhalation, ingestion, skin contact, occupational exposure and disposal (⚠️ owner 16 advanced §6).
- Regulation: existing chemical rules may not capture nano-specific behaviour (⚠️ owner 16 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Prelims GS-I 2022 Q98 asked about natural occurrence of nanoparticles and cosmetics safety (route only; key not held locally).
**Links:** SNT-4023, SNT-4017, SNT-4027, SNT-4028.
**Trap:** "Less material means risk-free." Lower dose does not remove toxicity or environmental-fate questions (✅ owner 16 §7).
**Mains hook:** Argue for material-specific, use-specific risk assessment and labelling, rather than blanket safety or blanket ban.
**Back:** *"Size, shape, coat, dose, route: test each one."*

### Flashcard SNT-4013 — DST National Programme on Nano Science and Technology (NPNST)
**Front:** What is the NPNST, what replaced the earlier Nano Mission label, and what does the 2024 advanced-materials call tell us?
**Definition (learn):** The Union-level R&D support architecture for nano science and applications, run by DST. It was earlier called the Nano Mission (✅ owner 16 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Supports research funding, institutional capacity and proposal-driven thematic work.
- 2024 advanced-materials call focused on low-dimensional materials, sensing and diagnostics, and energy conversion (✅ owner 16 advanced §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Call window opened 1 Sep 2024 and closed 30 Sep 2024 (✅ owner 16 §8, DST call page verified by owner on 16 Jul 2026).
**Links:** SNT-4014, SNT-4015, SNT-4020.
**Trap:** A live programme page or an open call proves institutional continuity, not funding volume or deployment at scale (✅ owner 16 §8).
**Mains hook:** Present NPNST as thematic R&D governance, then ask for outcome metrics: projects funded, prototypes and commercial uptake.
**Back:** *"Nano Mission became NPNST: a call, not proof of cash."*

### Flashcard SNT-4014 — INST Mohali
**Front:** What is INST, and where is it located?
**Definition (learn):** The Institute of Nano Science and Technology, Mohali, an autonomous DST institute established under the Nano Mission umbrella for nanoscience research (✅ owner 16 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Works under DST's nano-science programme architecture.
- Links nano research to agriculture, defence, healthcare, energy, environment and water (✅ owner 16 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Location Mohali, Punjab (⚠️ standard knowledge).
**Links:** SNT-4013, SNT-4015.
**Trap:** INST is a DST institute, not a CSIR network laboratory.
**Mains hook:** Cite INST as the national-level nano-science research node, then compare with CSIR's applied network.
**Back:** *"Mohali: nano-science under DST."*

### Flashcard SNT-4015 — CSIR network laboratories and applied nano capability
**Front:** What role do CSIR laboratories play in applied nanotechnology?
**Definition (learn):** CSIR's network laboratories contribute translational capability in advanced materials, glass and ceramics, electronics and allied sectors (✅ owner 16 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Translational work in sensors, ceramics, electronics and process development (✅ owner 16 advanced §4).
- Complements DST's research architecture.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner cites the CSIR labs-network page verified 16 Jul 2026.
**Links:** SNT-4013, SNT-4014, SNT-4020.
**Trap:** Do not treat CSIR as the funding ministry for nano. Its role is lab-based applied research.
**Mains hook:** Use CSIR to illustrate the research-to-product gap that needs process development and standards.
**Back:** *"CSIR labs: materials turned into products."*

### Flashcard SNT-4016 — Nano urea and nano DAP
**Front:** What are nano urea and nano DAP, and what claim must be qualified about them?
**Definition (learn):** Nano-fertilisers marketed as products. The owner states they are notified under the Fertiliser (Control) Order (✅ owner 16 §5, claim as recorded by the owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Purpose: improve nutrient-use efficiency and reduce bulk handling.
- Claim status: promising input-efficiency technology whose field performance is still under evaluation.
- Agronomic evidence on yield equivalence is contested in the literature (⚠️ owner 16 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** FCO notification date and details were not independently verified in this build (⚠️).
**Links:** SNT-4017, SNT-4021, SNT-4002.
**Trap:** "Nano urea gives the same or better yield, so it replaces bulk urea." The yield-equivalence evidence is contested.
**Mains hook:** Frame nano-fertilisers as input-efficiency infrastructure, not a yield miracle, and insist on field data, extension and farmer-level evidence.
**Back:** *"Efficient input, unproven yield: show the field data."*

### Flashcard SNT-4017 — Nano-enabled agricultural inputs: agrochemicals, sensors, controlled release, seed coating
**Front:** Which nano-enabled tools are used in Indian agriculture beyond fertilisers?
**Definition (learn):** Nano-formulated agrochemicals with lower dose per hectare, nano-sensors for soil moisture and nutrient status, controlled-release systems, seed coating and nano-priming, and nano-enabled post-harvest coatings (✅ owner 16 §5 and §9, specifics ⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Lower active-ingredient dose per hectare (nano-formulated pesticides).
- Sensing: soil moisture and nutrient sensors feeding precision agriculture.
- Release: controlled or targeted release synchronised with crop demand.
- Post-harvest: coatings and active packaging to reduce spoilage.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level list; no adoption figures verified.
**Links:** SNT-4016, SNT-4012, SNT-4021.
**Trap:** A lower dose does not remove residue, soil-microbiome or toxicity questions (✅ owner 16 §9 balance).
**Mains hook:** Treat each tool as an input-efficiency or information instrument and link it to farmer income through cost, credit and extension.
**Back:** *"Less dose, sensed dose, slow release."*

### Flashcard SNT-4018 — Nanomaterials in energy
**Front:** Which energy technologies use nanomaterials?
**Definition (learn):** Nanomaterials are relevant to batteries, fuel cells, supercapacitors and higher-performance solar cells (✅ owner 16 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Batteries and electrodes: storage materials and electrode interfaces.
- Fuel cells and supercapacitors: catalysis and surface-area effects.
- Solar cells: higher-performance interfaces.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level applications only; no performance figure attached.
**Links:** SNT-4002, SNT-4080 (EV battery basics, Part 4 EV group).
**Trap:** A laboratory electrode gain is not a commercial battery. Scale-up and cost decide deployment.
**Mains hook:** Link nano-storage to the energy transition, with cost and manufacturing as the binding constraints.
**Back:** *"Store it, convert it, make it last."*

### Flashcard SNT-4019 — Nanomaterials for water and environment
**Front:** How can nanomaterials support water treatment and environmental monitoring?
**Definition (learn):** Nanomaterials support adsorption, membrane filtration, contaminant detection and remediation (✅ owner 16 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Adsorption: high surface area captures contaminants.
- Membranes: nanoscale pores for filtration.
- Detection: sensors for contaminants.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level applications; no Indian plant or figure attached.
**Links:** SNT-4002, SNT-4012.
**Trap:** Contaminant-removal capacity in the lab does not establish safe disposal of spent nanomaterials (⚠️ owner 16 advanced §6 environmental issue).
**Mains hook:** Present water nano-tools as promising, with waste-stream and lifecycle handling as conditions.
**Back:** *"Adsorb, filter, sense."*

### Flashcard SNT-4020 — Nanoelectronics and the MeitY link
**Front:** Why is nanoelectronics a strategic bridge, and what event anchored it in 2025?
**Definition (learn):** Nanoelectronics links nanotechnology with the semiconductor and device ecosystem, which raises strategic relevance beyond academic science (✅ owner 16 advanced §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Sensors, flexible devices and advanced materials connect nano with chips.
- The 27 Mar 2025 Nano Electronics Roadshow and Conference on Semiconductor Ecosystem in India (MeitY/PIB) highlighted indigenous IP and startups (✅ owner 16 §8, PIB release as cited by owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Event date 27 Mar 2025.
**Links:** SNT-4013, SNT-4015.
**Trap:** An event held is not a measure of production capacity or market size.
**Mains hook:** Use nanoelectronics to connect nano policy with semiconductor strategy, then note the need for metrology and standards.
**Back:** *"Nano meets silicon: a strategic bridge."*

### Flashcard SNT-4021 — 2025 GS-III PYQ: nanotechnology in agriculture
**Front:** How has nanotechnology advanced agriculture, and how can it uplift farmers' socio-economic status? (2025 GS-III Q15, 250 words, 15 marks)
**Definition (learn):** A discussion of input-efficiency and precision-agriculture advances, followed by a socio-economic pathway and a balanced qualification (✅ owner 16 §9 routed PYQ).
**Breakdown:**
- Advances: nano-fertilisers, nano-formulated pesticides, nano-sensors, seed coatings, nano-enabled water treatment, post-harvest coatings.
- Chain: lower input cost per hectare → efficiency → less subsidy and import pressure → lower residue → lower spoilage → higher net farm income.
- Balance: contested yield evidence, unsettled nanotoxicology, incomplete standards, need for extension and credit.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Paper GS-III, 2025, Q15 (✅ owner 16 PYQ integration).
**Links:** SNT-4016, SNT-4017, SNT-4012.
**Trap:** Writing nanotechnology as a yield miracle. The strong answer frames it as input-efficiency and precision infrastructure.
**Mains hook:** Conclude that benefits reach smallholders only with credit, extension, affordability and independent testing.
**Back:** *"Input, sense, release, store, then credit and advice."*

### Flashcard SNT-4022 — 2018 Prelims PYQ: 3D printing applications
**Front:** Does additive manufacturing (3D printing) count as nanotechnology, and what does the 2018 Prelims item test?
**Definition (learn):** Additive manufacturing builds an object layer by layer from a digital model. It supports customised medical devices, prototypes and industrial parts. It is not intrinsically nanotechnology (✅ owner 16 advanced routed PYQ; key not held locally).
**Breakdown:**
- Layer-by-layer build from a digital model.
- Applications across sectors; material and certification constraints decide use.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Prelims GS-I 2018 Q84 (✅ owner 16 historical PYQ table).
**Links:** SNT-4011, SNT-4009.
**Trap:** "3D printing is nanotechnology" is wrong. Layer resolution and nano-scale structure are different concepts.
**Mains hook:** Rarely a Mains item; use as a classification check.
**Back:** *"Layer by layer, not atom by atom."*

### Flashcard SNT-4023 — 2022 Prelims PYQ: natural nanoparticles and cosmetics
**Front:** Do nanoparticles occurring in nature, or used in cosmetics, prove that nanoparticles are safe?
**Definition (learn):** Nanoparticles can occur naturally and can be intentionally used in products such as cosmetics. Natural occurrence or consumer use does not prove universal harmlessness (✅ owner 16 advanced routed PYQ, key not held locally).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural occurrence is a fact about origin, not a safety verdict.
- Safety depends on size, coating, dose, exposure route and persistence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Prelims GS-I 2022 Q98 (✅ owner 16 historical PYQ table).
**Links:** SNT-4012, SNT-4005.
**Trap:** "Natural therefore safe." Reject this in any statement-level question.
**Mains hook:** Use to argue for material-specific regulation and labelling.
**Back:** *"Natural is not proof of safe."*

### Flashcard SNT-4024 — Trap set: miniaturisation, graphene versus CNT, and "nano is risk-free"
**Front:** Which three nanotechnology statements are classic UPSC traps?
**Definition (learn):** Three recurring errors are: (a) nanotechnology is just smaller science; (b) graphene and CNTs are the same form; (c) nano-products are automatically risk-free because less material is used (✅ owner 16 §7).
**Breakdown:**
- (a) Correct test: functional property change at 1–100 nm.
- (b) Correct test: sheet versus tube.
- (c) Correct test: toxicology and environmental fate remain live questions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap list (✅ owner 16 §7).
**Links:** SNT-4001, SNT-4004, SNT-4012.
**Trap:** Each trap appears as a plausible-sounding statement in Prelims. Eliminate by using the size band, dimension count and safety-evidence test.
**Mains hook:** Use the three corrections as the spine of a balanced nanotechnology answer.
**Back:** *"Size changes function; sheet is not tube; less is not zero risk."*

### Flashcard SNT-4025 — Nanomedicine and imaging applications
**Front:** How is nanotechnology used in medicine and diagnostics?
**Definition (learn):** Nanocarriers, imaging materials, biosensors and targeted-delivery research are major areas of interest in health (✅ owner 16 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Targeted drug delivery with carriers such as dendrimers.
- Imaging with quantum dots and nano-contrast materials (⚠️ standard).
- Biosensors for diagnostics.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Prelims 2020 Q41 (CNT in medicine) is a related route (✅ owner 16 §9).
**Links:** SNT-4003, SNT-4007, SNT-4012.
**Trap:** A research application is not a marketed therapy. Clinical translation, access and affordability decide public benefit (⚠️ owner 16 advanced ethics).
**Mains hook:** Argue for nano-diagnostics with access and safety monitoring.
**Back:** *"Carry, see, sense: nano in health, with safety checks."*

### Flashcard SNT-4026 — Nanotechnology in textiles, coatings and sensors
**Front:** Which everyday applications of nano-coatings and sensors does the owner list?
**Definition (learn):** Nano-enabled coatings improve stain resistance, antimicrobial behaviour and durability in textiles and surfaces. Nanoelectronic sensors support flexible devices (✅ owner 16 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Textiles: stain resistance, antimicrobial finish, durability.
- Electronics: sensors and flexible devices.
- Both depend on durability and washing or wear data (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level applications; no figure attached.
**Links:** SNT-4020, SNT-4012.
**Trap:** A coating claim is not a safety claim for wearers or washing waste.
**Mains hook:** Use textiles as a consumer example of a nano-product whose lifecycle matters.
**Back:** *"Stain-proof, germ-proof, wash-tested."*

### Flashcard SNT-4027 — Advanced: scale-up, reproducibility and metrology
**Front:** Why does a laboratory nanomaterial often fail to scale into an industrial product?
**Definition (learn):** Performance depends sensitively on particle size, morphology and surface chemistry, so reproducibility and standards become harder at scale (⚠️ owner 16 advanced §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Constraint 1: reproducibility across batches.
- Constraint 2: cost, process integration and scale-up barriers.
- Constraint 3: metrology, testing and certification capacity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level analysis without numbers.
**Links:** SNT-4011, SNT-4009, SNT-4028.
**Trap:** "Works in the lab, so it is ready." Lab success is not mass production.
**Mains hook:** Conclude that India's nano challenge is partly a standards and manufacturing-quality challenge.
**Back:** *"Lab works; batch, metrology and cost decide."*

### Flashcard SNT-4028 — Advanced: nano-specific governance and regulatory gap
**Front:** Why may existing product regulation fail to capture nano-specific risks?
**Definition (learn):** Existing chemical and product regulation may not automatically capture behaviour that depends on nanoscale properties (⚠️ owner 16 advanced §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Nano-specific behaviour depends on size, shape and coating.
- Waste streams and lifecycle handling need explicit rules.
- Access and long-term monitoring matter for nano-medicines (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level analysis; no specific Indian rule cited as verified here.
**Links:** SNT-4012, SNT-4027, SNT-4016.
**Trap:** "Nano is covered by general chemical law." Not necessarily, so test the gap.
**Mains hook:** Argue for nano-specific labelling, testing protocols and environmental monitoring.
**Back:** *"Same chemical, new size: new rules to test."*

### GROUP — Intellectual Property Rights and Patents

### Flashcard SNT-4029 — IPR architecture: differentiated instruments, not one "right"
**Front:** What are the main IP instruments, and what does each protect?
**Definition (learn):** IPR is not one category. Patents protect new inventions; trademarks protect brand identifiers; GIs protect place-linked goods; copyright protects original expression; designs protect appearance; trade secrets protect confidential information (✅ owner 17 §1–2).
**Breakdown:**
- Patent: invention (product or process).
- Trademark: brand-distinguishing sign.
- GI: origin-linked collective indication.
- Copyright: original literary, artistic and musical works.
- Design: visual features of shape, pattern or ornament.
- Trade secret: confidential business information.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Terms differ by instrument (see SNT-4030, SNT-4044, SNT-4045, SNT-4046).
**Links:** SNT-4030, SNT-4044, SNT-4045, SNT-4046, SNT-4063.
**Trap:** Treating "IPR" as one legal label. Each instrument has a different holder, term and logic.
**Mains hook:** Argue that India's regime is differentiated, with public-interest filters inside patent law.
**Back:** *"Invention, brand, place, expression, look, secret."*

### Flashcard SNT-4030 — Patent: definition, criteria and term
**Front:** What must an invention satisfy to be patentable in India, and how long does a patent last?
**Definition (learn):** An exclusive right granted for an invention that is new, involves an inventive step and is capable of industrial application (✅ owner 17 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Novelty.
- Inventive step (non-obvious to a person skilled in the art).
- Industrial applicability.
- Term: 20 years from the date of filing, under the Patents Act, 1970 (✅ owner 17 §1, §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Patent term is time-bound; annual renewal fees apply and Form 27 working statements are required (✅ owner 17 §3).
**Links:** SNT-4031, SNT-4032, SNT-4033, SNT-4029.
**Trap:** A patent cannot be renewed indefinitely as a trademark can. Its term is fixed at 20 years from filing.
**Mains hook:** Use the time-bound monopoly to frame the innovation-incentive versus public-access trade-off.
**Back:** *"New, non-obvious, usable: twenty years from filing."*

### Flashcard SNT-4031 — Inventive step
**Front:** What is an "inventive step," and why is it a patentability test?
**Definition (learn):** A technical advance or economic significance that makes the invention non-obvious to a person skilled in the art (✅ owner 17 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Tests whether the invention is more than an obvious step.
- Distinct from novelty, which asks whether anything identical existed before.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level definition.
**Links:** SNT-4030.
**Trap:** Confusing inventive step with novelty. An invention can be new but obvious.
**Mains hook:** Use inventive step to argue for high patent quality rather than patent quantity.
**Back:** *"Not just new: not obvious."*

### Flashcard SNT-4032 — Section 3(d), limb one: new use and new property are not inventions
**Front:** What does Section 3(d) say about a new use or new property of a known substance?
**Definition (learn):** Mere discovery of a new property or new use of a known substance, or mere use of a known process, machine or apparatus, is not an invention at all (✅ owner 17 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- New use of a known substance: not an invention.
- New property of a known substance: not an invention.
- Mere use of a known process, machine or apparatus: not an invention.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Patents Act, 1970, s.3(d) (✅ owner 17 §2).
**Links:** SNT-4033 (limb two), SNT-4036, SNT-4069.
**Trap:** Enhanced efficacy rescues a new form, not a new use. Collapsing the two limbs is the standard error (✅ owner 17 advanced §2).
**Mains hook:** Use s.3(d) limb one to argue against evergreening through new uses.
**Back:** *"New use is not invention."*

### Flashcard SNT-4033 — Section 3(d), limb two: new forms of known substances and Novartis (2013)
**Front:** When is a new form (salt, polymorph, isomer, metabolite) of a known substance patentable under s.3(d)?
**Definition (learn):** A new form of a known substance is patentable only if it differs significantly in properties with regard to efficacy. Efficacy was read as therapeutic efficacy in *Novartis v. Union of India* (2013) (✅ owner 17 §2, §7).
**Breakdown:**
- New forms include salts, esters, polymorphs, isomers, metabolites and particle size (✅ owner 17 §2).
- Test: significant difference in efficacy, read as therapeutic efficacy.
- Rescue applies to new forms only.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *Novartis v. Union of India* (2013), as recorded in owner 17.
**Links:** SNT-4032, SNT-4037, SNT-4069.
**Trap:** "Section 3(d) bans all pharma patents." It targets weak evergreening claims lacking enhanced therapeutic efficacy (✅ owner 17 §7).
**Mains hook:** Argue that s.3(d) balances innovation and access without banning pharmaceutical patents.
**Back:** *"New form needs real efficacy gain."*

### Flashcard SNT-4034 — Section 3(j): plants, animals and essentially biological processes
**Front:** What does s.3(j) of the Patents Act exclude from patentability?
**Definition (learn):** Plants and animals in whole or in part (other than micro-organisms), including seeds, varieties and species, and essentially biological processes for their production or propagation (⚠️ owner 17 §2, standard statutory text as recorded by owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Excluded: plants, animals, seeds, varieties, species, essentially biological processes.
- Not excluded: micro-organisms, which remain patentable (✅ owner 17 advanced §10).
- Plant varieties are protected under the PPV&FR Act, 2001 (SNT-4049).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner attaches the 2024 life-materials PYQ to this section.
**Links:** SNT-4049, SNT-4065, SNT-4070.
**Trap:** Micro-organisms are not excluded by s.3(j); plants and animals are.
**Mains hook:** Explain why India chose a sui generis plant-variety route rather than patents for life forms.
**Back:** *"Plants and animals out; micro-organisms in."*

### Flashcard SNT-4035 — Section 3(b): public order, morality and serious prejudice
**Front:** What does s.3(b) exclude?
**Definition (learn):** Inventions whose primary or intended use would be contrary to public order or morality, or would cause serious prejudice to human, animal or plant life or health, or to the environment (⚠️ owner 17 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Public order or morality.
- Serious prejudice to life, health or environment.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level statement; section text as summarised by owner.
**Links:** SNT-4030, SNT-4036.
**Trap:** s.3(b) is a use-based exclusion; it asks what the invention is for, not only whether it is new.
**Mains hook:** Use s.3(b) to show that patent law already has public-interest exclusions beyond novelty.
**Back:** *"Use decides: order, morals, life, environment."*

### Flashcard SNT-4036 — Section 84: compulsory licence after three years
**Front:** On what grounds and when can a compulsory licence be sought under s.84?
**Definition (learn):** Statutory authorisation to use a patent without the holder's voluntary licence. An application may be made after three years from grant on any of three grounds (✅ owner 17 §2, §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Timing: after three years from grant.
- Ground 1: reasonable requirements of the public not satisfied.
- Ground 2: invention not available at a reasonably affordable price.
- Ground 3: invention not worked in India.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Patents Act s.84 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** SNT-4037, SNT-4038, SNT-4040.
**Trap:** Compulsory licensing does not abolish patent rights. It is a conditional public-interest exception (✅ owner 17 §7).
**Mains hook:** Present s.84 as a calibrated legal flexibility, not an anti-innovation policy.
**Back:** *"Three years, three grounds: public need, price, local working."*

### Flashcard SNT-4037 — Natco v. Bayer (2012): India's compulsory-licence case
**Front:** Which case is the Indian landmark for a s.84 compulsory licence, and what drug was involved?
**Definition (learn):** Natco Pharma v. Bayer (2012) concerned sorafenib, marketed as Nexavar. The owner records it as India's only granted s.84 licence to date (✅ owner 17 §2, §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Drug: sorafenib (Nexavar).
- Year: 2012.
- Ground: affordability and public access under s.84.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner's statement "only granted to date" is time-varying; treat as an as-of-build claim, ⚠️ to recheck before quoting.
**Links:** SNT-4036, SNT-4033.
**Trap:** Do not say compulsory licences are common in India on the basis of this one case.
**Mains hook:** Use Natco to illustrate how s.84 works in practice and why it is rarely used.
**Back:** *"Natco, Nexavar, 2012: the one licence."*

### Flashcard SNT-4038 — Section 92: emergency and public non-commercial licences
**Front:** How does s.92 differ from s.84?
**Definition (learn):** The government may notify compulsory licences in circumstances of national emergency, extreme urgency or public non-commercial use (✅ owner 17 §2, §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Trigger: emergency, extreme urgency or public non-commercial use.
- Initiated by government notification rather than a private application under s.84.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level statement.
**Links:** SNT-4036, SNT-4039.
**Trap:** s.84 is application-based after three years; s.92 is government-notified and can be used in emergencies.
**Mains hook:** Compare s.84 and s.92 to show a tiered public-interest toolkit.
**Back:** *"Emergency or public non-commercial: government notifies."*

### Flashcard SNT-4039 — Section 107A: Bolar exemption and parallel import
**Front:** What do s.107A's two exceptions allow?
**Definition (learn):** Making, using or selling a patented invention solely for uses reasonably related to developing and submitting regulatory information, so generics are ready at expiry (the Bolar exemption). It also permits importation from a person authorised to produce and sell the product (✅ owner 17 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Bolar: regulatory-approval testing before expiry.
- Parallel import: import from an authorised producer.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level statutory summary.
**Links:** SNT-4036, SNT-4033.
**Trap:** The Bolar exemption is not permission to sell the generic before patent expiry.
**Mains hook:** Use s.107A to explain how generic competition is made possible without ignoring patent rights.
**Back:** *"Test before expiry, import from the authorised."*

### Flashcard SNT-4040 — Opposition: pre-grant (s.25(1)) and post-grant (s.25(2))
**Front:** Who can oppose a patent before grant, and who can oppose after grant, and within what time?
**Definition (learn):** Under s.25(1), any person may oppose before grant. Under s.25(2), an interested person may oppose within one year of grant (✅ owner 17 §2, §7).
**Breakdown:**
- Pre-grant: any person.
- Post-grant: interested person, within one year of grant.
- Function: public-interest filter on patent quality.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Two-stage opposition is distinctive to Indian patent law (✅ owner 17 §2).
**Links:** SNT-4036, SNT-4069.
**Trap:** Pre-grant standing is wider than post-grant standing. Do not swap them.
**Mains hook:** Argue that a broad opposition system protects health access, and note that foreign rights-holders have criticised it (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Back:** *"Before grant, anyone; after grant, interested, within a year."*

### Flashcard SNT-4041 — Product patent versus process patent: 1970 to 2005
**Front:** What was the difference between process-only and product patents in Indian pharmaceuticals, and when did product patents return?
**Definition (learn):** A process patent covers a route of manufacture. A product patent covers the substance itself. India granted only process patents for pharma, food and chemicals from 1970, and restored product patents from 1 January 2005 under TRIPS (✅ owner 17 §2, advanced §2).
**Breakdown:**
- 1970–2004: process patents only; generics industry built on this.
- From 1 Jan 2005: product patents restored.
- Balancing devices retained: s.3(d), compulsory licensing, oppositions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Patents Act 1970; product patents from 1 Jan 2005 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** SNT-4033, SNT-4042, SNT-4036.
**Trap:** Saying product patents were always available in India. They were absent from 1970 to 2004 for these sectors.
**Mains hook:** Explain how the 1970 process-patent regime built domestic generics and how the 2005 shift changed access.
**Back:** *"Seventy process, oh-five product."*
