**Front:** What does VLC offer, and what limits its range? (2020 Prelims GS-I Q39)
**Definition (learn):** Visible light communication uses light for data transmission, with range and performance limited by line of sight, absorption and interference (⚠️ owner 21 advanced routed evidence).
**Breakdown:**
- Uses visible light.
- Limits: line of sight, absorption.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 Q39.
**Links:** SNT-4254, SNT-4225.
**Trap:** VLC is not infinite-range wireless.
**Mains hook:** Rarely central.
**Back:** *"Light carries data; walls block it."*

### Flashcard SNT-4254 — 2022 Prelims PYQ: short-range wireless technologies
**Front:** How are short-range wireless technologies classified? (2022 Prelims GS-I Q36)
**Definition (learn):** Short-range wireless includes technologies such as Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, NFC and Zigbee, which operate over short distances on radio principles (⚠️ standard classification).
**Breakdown:**
- Radio-based.
- Short range.
- Examples: Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, NFC.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 Q36.
**Links:** SNT-4253, SNT-4225.
**Trap:** Confusing short-range with cellular networks.
**Mains hook:** Rarely central.
**Back:** *"Short-range radio, household networks."*

### Flashcard SNT-4255 — 2018 Prelims PYQ: Einstein's general relativity predictions
**Front:** What key predictions did general relativity make? (2018 Prelims GS-I Q62)
**Definition (learn):** General relativity predicts gravitational effects of mass and spacetime curvature, including light bending near mass and gravitational waves (⚠️ standard; owner route).
**Breakdown:**
- Light bending.
- Gravitational waves.
- Time dilation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 Q62.
**Links:** SNT-4256, SNT-4240.
**Trap:** Special relativity and general relativity are not the same.
**Mains hook:** Rarely central.
**Back:** *"Mass bends space; light bends too."*

### Flashcard SNT-4256 — 2019 Prelims PYQ: black-hole merger and gravitational waves
**Front:** What did the 2019 Prelims Q97 test on black-hole mergers and gravitational waves?
**Definition (learn):** Observation of gravitational waves from merging black holes. Gravitational waves were first detected in 2015 (✅ owner 21 advanced routed PYQ; standard date ⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Merging black holes.
- Gravitational waves, first detected 2015.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 Q97; 2015 first detection.
**Links:** SNT-4255, SNT-4240.
**Trap:** Observing a wave is not seeing a black hole directly.
**Mains hook:** Rarely central.
**Back:** *"Merging holes ripple space."*

### Flashcard SNT-4257 — 2018 GS-III PYQ: Bose–Einstein statistics
**Front:** How did Bose–Einstein statistics contribute to a revolution in physics? (2018 GS-III Q5, 10 marks, 150 words)
**Definition (learn):** Indistinguishable integer-spin particles (bosons) can share a quantum state, unlike fermions. The framework helped explain collective quantum phenomena and later technologies such as lasers and Bose–Einstein condensates (✅ owner 21 PYQ routed Core).
**Breakdown:**
- Bosons share states.
- Fermions obey Pauli exclusion.
- Impact: collective quantum effects.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 GS-III Q5.
**Links:** SNT-4241, SNT-4236.
**Trap:** Using it as a generic label for every quantum particle.
**Mains hook:** Qualify that a statistical framework is not itself a consumer technology.
**Back:** *"Bosons share, fermions exclude."*

### Flashcard SNT-4258 — 2021 GS-III PYQ: blue LED and everyday life
**Front:** How has the blue LED affected everyday life? (2021 GS-III Q16, 15 marks, 250 words)
**Definition (learn):** Efficient blue emission enabled white LED lighting through blue light plus phosphor, and completed red-green-blue combinations (✅ owner 21 PYQ routed Core).
**Breakdown:**
- Semiconductor junction converts electricity to light.
- Blue enables white light.
- Impact: lower electricity demand, wider illumination.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 GS-III Q16.
**Links:** SNT-4233, SNT-4249.
**Trap:** An LED principle is not a universal efficiency number; driver and lifetime matter.
**Mains hook:** Weigh energy savings against disposal and product quality.
**Back:** *"Blue made white; white made efficient."*

### Flashcard SNT-4259 — 2024 GS-III PYQ: asteroids and planetary defence
**Front:** What are asteroids, how real is extinction risk, and what strategies exist? (2024 GS-III Q, direct PYQ)
**Definition (learn):** Asteroids are rocky bodies. The extinction-risk question is one of calibration and must not be answered with false precision; strategies include detection, orbit determination, warning and conditional deflection (✅ owner 21 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Detection and orbit determination.
- Warning.
- Deflection: kinetic impactor (as demonstrated by DART), gravity tractor or nuclear as conditional options (⚠️ owner route).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-III.
**Links:** SNT-4251, SNT-4260.
**Trap:** Claiming precise prediction.
**Mains hook:** Argue for detection and response systems, not certainty.
**Back:** *"Find it, track it, nudge it."*

### Flashcard SNT-4260 — 2026 Prelims PYQ: black-box recorders and underwater detection
**Front:** What does the 2026 Prelims GS-I Q44 test on recorders and detection?
**Definition (learn):** Aircraft black-box recorders, underwater detection and crash-survivable memory (✅ owner 21 PYQ integration; provisional key, no answer recorded).
**Breakdown:**
- Recorders: crash-survivable memory.
- Underwater detection: separate function.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2026 Q44; provisional key.
**Links:** SNT-4251, SNT-4259.
**Trap:** A recorder is not a locator for every underwater search.
**Mains hook:** Rarely central.
**Back:** *"Recorder remembers; locator searches."*

### Flashcard SNT-4261 — Nobel Prize in Physics 2024: Hopfield and Hinton
**Front:** What did the 2024 Nobel Physics prize recognise?
**Definition (learn):** Foundational discoveries enabling machine learning with artificial neural networks, awarded to John Hopfield and Geoffrey Hinton, announced 8 Oct 2024 (✅ owner 21 §8, Nobel press release as cited).
**Breakdown:**
- Physics-to-AI bridge.
- Date: 8 Oct 2024.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 8 Oct 2024.
**Links:** SNT-4240, SNT-4263.
**Trap:** Confusing this with the 2023 attosecond prize.
**Mains hook:** Use to show physics underpinning AI.
**Back:** *"Physics prize for neural networks, 2024."*

### Flashcard SNT-4262 — Nobel Prize in Physics 2023: attosecond light pulses
**Front:** What did the 2023 Nobel Physics prize recognise?
**Definition (learn):** Attosecond light pulses for studying electron dynamics in matter, announced 3 Oct 2023 (✅ owner 21 §8; laureate names ⚠️ standard).
**Breakdown:**
- Attosecond pulses.
- Electron dynamics.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 3 Oct 2023.
**Links:** SNT-4226, SNT-4241.
**Trap:** Confusing attosecond with picosecond or femtosecond scale.
**Mains hook:** Use to show wave physics driving diagnostics.
**Back:** *"Attoseconds reveal electrons."*

### Flashcard SNT-4263 — ISM Semicon 2.0: compute, memory, power, networking, RF, sensors
**Front:** Which chip-design blocks does ISM's Semicon 2.0 page highlight?
**Definition (learn):** Compute, memory, power, networking, RF and sensors (✅ owner 21 §8, ISM page as at 16 Jul 2026).
**Breakdown:**
- Compute.
- Memory.
- Power.
- Networking.
- RF.
- Sensors.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Page state as at 16 Jul 2026.
**Links:** SNT-4267, SNT-4234, SNT-4264.
**Trap:** Treating the page list as the full scheme.
**Mains hook:** Map semiconductor physics to application blocks.
**Back:** *"Compute, memory, power, network, RF, sense."*

### Flashcard SNT-4264 — SEMICON India 2026: theme and date
**Front:** What theme and dates did ISM list for SEMICON India 2026?
**Definition (learn):** The theme "Trusted and Resilient Semiconductor Ecosystems", scheduled for 17 to 19 Sep 2026 (✅ owner 21 §8, ISM page as cited).
**Breakdown:**
- Theme: trusted and resilient ecosystems.
- Dates: 17 to 19 Sep 2026.
- As of 1 Nov 2026, the event date has passed; outcomes are not verified in this build.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 17–19 Sep 2026.
**Links:** SNT-4263, SNT-4267.
**Trap:** Describing a past event as upcoming.
**Mains hook:** Link transistor-level science to ecosystem resilience.
**Back:** *"Trusted, resilient; September 2026, now past."*

### Flashcard SNT-4265 — CSIR-NPL: national standards and metrology
**Front:** What does CSIR-NPL do?
**Definition (learn):** National Physical Laboratory: national standards, measurement and metrology (✅ owner 21 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Standards.
- Measurement.
- Metrology.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level.
**Links:** SNT-4270, SNT-4272.
**Trap:** NPL is not a fabrication plant.
**Mains hook:** Argue that metrology is the bottleneck in precision manufacturing.
**Back:** *"NPL sets the measure."*

### Flashcard SNT-4266 — CSIR-CEERI: electronics and device research
**Front:** What does CSIR-CEERI do?
**Definition (learn):** Central Electronics Engineering Research Institute: electronics and semiconductor-device research (✅ owner 21 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Device research.
- Electronics ecosystem.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level.
**Links:** SNT-4270, SNT-4267.
**Trap:** CEERI is not the India Semiconductor Mission.
**Mains hook:** Link device research to chip capability.
**Back:** *"CEERI builds the device science."*

### Flashcard SNT-4267 — India Semiconductor Mission
**Front:** What does the India Semiconductor Mission do?
**Definition (learn):** The policy architecture linking semiconductor fundamentals to manufacturing and chip-design capability (✅ owner 21 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Manufacturing.
- Design.
- Policy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level.
**Links:** SNT-4263, SNT-4264.
**Trap:** A fab announcement is not the whole ecosystem.
**Mains hook:** Argue that capability is a stack.
**Back:** *"ISM turns physics into policy."*

### Flashcard SNT-4268 — DST National Quantum Mission
**Front:** How does the National Quantum Mission link to physics?
**Definition (learn):** A frontier physics-to-technology bridge for quantum devices, sensing and measurement science (✅ owner 21 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Quantum devices.
- Sensing.
- Measurement.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level.
**Links:** SNT-4270, SNT-4259.
**Trap:** Quantum devices are not yet mass consumer products.
**Mains hook:** Link quantum physics to strategic technology.
**Back:** *"Quantum physics, national mission."*

### Flashcard SNT-4269 — DAE and BARC: applied nuclear and radiation physics
**Front:** What role do DAE and BARC play in applied radiation physics?
**Definition (learn):** Policy-level continuation of nuclear and radiation-related physics in applied form (✅ owner 21 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Nuclear physics: applied.
- Radiation: applied.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level.
**Links:** SNT-4237, SNT-4239.
**Trap:** Radiation use is not only nuclear power.
**Mains hook:** Separate nuclear power from radioisotope uses.
**Back:** *"Atomic energy: power and radiation uses."*

### Flashcard SNT-4270 — Semiconductor capability stack: materials, design, fab, packaging, metrology
**Front:** Why is semiconductor capability a stack rather than a fab announcement?
**Definition (learn):** Capability spans materials, design, fabrication, packaging, testing, metrology and talent (✅ owner 21 advanced §4, ⚠️ analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- Materials.
- Design.
- Fabrication.
- Packaging.
- Testing and metrology.
- Talent.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level.
**Links:** SNT-4265, SNT-4266, SNT-4267.
**Trap:** Equating a fab with a full ecosystem.
**Mains hook:** Argue for policy across the full stack.
**Back:** *"Materials, design, fab, pack, test, measure, teach."*

### Flashcard SNT-4271 — Advanced: CMOS and the MOSFET's low static power
**Front:** Why did CMOS logic allow microprocessors to scale?
**Definition (learn):** MOSFET gates are field-controlled and draw almost no gate current; CMOS pairs keep static power low (⚠️ owner 21 advanced §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Field control.
- Low gate current.
- Low static power.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level.
**Links:** SNT-4234, SNT-4270.
**Trap:** Ignoring dynamic power when scaling.
**Mains hook:** Use to explain why chip scaling mattered.
**Back:** *"Field-gated, low standby, scalable."*

### Flashcard SNT-4272 — Advanced: why lab devices fail at scale
**Front:** Why can a device that works in the lab fail at scale?
**Definition (learn):** Thermal management, materials purity or measurement standards may be weak (⚠️ owner 21 advanced §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Thermal management.
- Materials purity.
- Measurement standards.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level.
**Links:** SNT-4265, SNT-4270.
**Trap:** Lab success is production readiness.
**Mains hook:** Argue for metrology and clean-room investment.
**Back:** *"Heat, purity, measure: the scale-up tests."*

### Flashcard SNT-4273 — Advanced: wave propagation and communication limits
**Front:** Why does the wave type decide communication and detection use?
**Definition (learn):** Sound needs a medium; electromagnetic waves travel in vacuum. Radar and VLC use different spectrum parts. Performance depends on line of sight, absorption, weather and interference (✅ owner 21 advanced core architecture, ⚠️ analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- Sound: medium.
- EM: vacuum allowed.
- Limits: line of sight, weather, interference.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-level.
**Links:** SNT-4215, SNT-4253, SNT-4252.
**Trap:** A technique that works in one environment may fail in another.
**Mains hook:** Use to explain why communication technology varies by environment.
**Back:** *"Sound needs matter; light crosses vacuum; obstacles block."*

## SCIENCE AND TECHNOLOGY — Part 5: Chemistry, Biology, Institutions, Computing and Discoveries

> Source key: `(✅ owner NN)` = grounded in the repository owner file NN (basic or advanced, Science-and-Technology); `(✅ Wiki: Department of Science and Technology article)` = confirmed on a fetched Wikipedia page; `⚠️` = owner-stated analytical inference, memory-only, or not web-verified. Nobel Prize pages (nobelprize.org) returned 403 and Wikipedia Nobel pages returned 404 during build; laureate names not stated in the owners are therefore ⚠️. Today's as-of date for time-varying facts is 2026-11-01; owner current-anchor dates are as stated in the owners.

---

### GROUP — 22 General Science: Chemistry Fundamentals (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard SNT-5001 — Chemistry fundamentals: the chain from atom to policy
**Front:** What is the logical chain that links basic chemistry to GS-III policy questions?
**Definition (learn):** Chemistry explains properties through structure: atom → element → periodic trend → bonding → compound → reaction, and from there to acids/bases, redox, polymers, ores and metals, and biomolecules (✅ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Structure gives property: electron arrangement sets bonding, which sets reactivity
- Property gives application: fertiliser, water treatment, corrosion control, plastics, metal extraction
- Application gives policy: waste, energy, safety, industrial regulation
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 sets the chain; the advanced owner adds "bonding → compound properties → process chemistry → industrial system → policy issue" (✅ owner 22, advanced).
**Links:** SNT-5002, SNT-5011, SNT-5015, SNT-5025, SNT-5057
**Trap:** UPSC chemistry asks "what does this property imply?" far more often than "write the full reaction." A correct answer names the property and the consequence.
**Mains hook:** Chemistry becomes GS-III material when a molecular property explains a national problem, for example why urea leaching drives nitrate pollution.
**Back:** *"Structure sets property; property sets process; process sets policy."*

### Flashcard SNT-5002 — Atomic number versus mass number
**Front:** Which quantity identifies an element, and how does it differ from mass number?
**Definition (learn):** Atomic number (Z) equals the number of protons and identifies the element; mass number (A) equals protons plus neutrons (⚠️ owner 22, standard chemistry).
**Breakdown:**
- Z identifies the element and fixes its place in the periodic table
- A changes with neutron count; the same element can have several A values
- The modern periodic table is ordered by atomic number, not atomic mass (✅ owner 22)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Atomic number, not atomic mass, is the basis of the modern periodic table (✅ owner 22, Must-Know Facts).
**Links:** SNT-5003, SNT-5004
**Trap:** Atomic mass is not the ordering principle; some earlier tables ordered by mass, which is why the modern table uses Z.
**Mains hook:** Not usually a Mains question; use it as the base for isotope applications such as dating and radiotherapy.
**Back:** *"Z names the element, A counts the nucleons."*

### Flashcard SNT-5003 — Isotopes, isobars and isotones
**Front:** Define isotopes, isobars and isotones with one example each.
**Definition (learn):** Isotopes share atomic number but differ in mass number; isobars share mass number but differ in atomic number; isotones share neutron number (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Isotopes: C-12 and C-14; U-235 and U-238 (✅ owner 22 examples)
- Isobars: same A, different Z
- Isotones: same neutron count, different element
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Examples as stated in owner 22 (⚠️ owner-stated table row).
**Links:** SNT-5002, SNT-5040 (C-14 dating), SNT-5038 (Co-60)
**Trap:** Isotopes are chemically almost identical (same electron count) but differ in nuclear stability, so a radioisotope can be useful while its chemistry remains the same.
**Mains hook:** Radioisotope use in medicine and dating depends on half-life and nuclear stability, not on chemical differences.
**Back:** *"Same Z, isotopes; same A, isobars; same N, isotones."*

### Flashcard SNT-5004 — Periodic trends across a period and down a group
**Front:** What happens to atomic size, electronegativity and metallic character across a period and down a group?
**Definition (learn):** Across a period (left to right) atomic size decreases, ionisation energy and electronegativity increase, and metallic character decreases; down a group the reverse broadly holds (⚠️ owner 22; the owner marks the trend table as analytical).
**Breakdown:**
- Across a period: size ↓, ionisation energy ↑, electronegativity ↑, metallic character ↓
- Down a group: atomic size ↑, metallic character generally ↑
- Noble gases sit at the end of periods with filled shells
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Across a period, metallic character generally decreases; down a group, atomic size generally increases (✅ owner 22, Visual foundation).
**Links:** SNT-5005, SNT-5006
**Trap:** Electronegativity and metallic character move in opposite directions across a period; do not reverse both from the same rule.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item; supports reasoning on reactivity and materials choice.
**Back:** *"Across: small, grip, less metal. Down: bigger, more metal."*

### Flashcard SNT-5005 — Fluorine and noble gases: extremes of the periodic table
**Front:** Which element is the most electronegative, and why are noble gases chemically inert?
**Definition (learn):** Fluorine is the most electronegative element; noble gases are largely inert because their electron shells are filled (⚠️ owner 22 Chemistry table).
**Breakdown:**
- Fluorine: strongest pull on shared electrons; highly reactive halogen
- Halogens: highly reactive non-metals used in disinfection and salts
- Noble gases: filled shells, least reactive; used for inert atmospheres and lighting
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Halogens highly reactive non-metals; noble gases chemically least reactive (✅ owner 22, Visual foundation).
**Links:** SNT-5004, SNT-5031 (chlorination)
**Trap:** "Most reactive" in the halogen group is fluorine, but "most electronegative" is also fluorine; do not confuse reactivity of a metal (alkali metals) with that of a non-metal (halogens).
**Mains hook:** Halogen chemistry is the basis of chlorine disinfection, a safety-cost trade-off in water supply.
**Back:** *"Fluorine grabs most; noble gases hold out."*

### Flashcard SNT-5006 — Ionic bond
**Front:** How is an ionic bond formed, and why do ionic compounds conduct when molten or dissolved?
**Definition (learn):** An ionic bond forms by transfer of electrons from one atom to another, producing oppositely charged ions held by electrostatic attraction (✅ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Metal loses electron(s) → cation; non-metal gains → anion
- Attraction between ions forms the lattice
- Molten or aqueous state frees ions to carry current
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ionic compounds often conduct in molten or aqueous state because ions are free to move (✅ owner 22, Must-Know Facts).
**Links:** SNT-5007, SNT-5008
**Trap:** Pure solid ionic salts do not conduct well because ions are fixed in the lattice; the conducting state is molten or dissolved.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item; supports electrolysis and battery reasoning.
**Back:** *"Transfer makes ions; ions carry current only when free."*

### Flashcard SNT-5007 — Covalent bond
**Front:** How is a covalent bond formed, and why do pure covalent compounds generally not conduct?
**Definition (learn):** A covalent bond forms when atoms share electron pairs (✅ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Shared pair between two atoms; no net ion transfer
- Molecular compounds generally lack free charged carriers in pure form
- Diamond and graphite show covalent networks with very different properties (SNT-5020)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Covalent compounds generally do not conduct electricity well in pure form (✅ owner 22, Must-Know Facts).
**Links:** SNT-5006, SNT-5020
**Trap:** Graphite is covalently bonded yet conducts through delocalised electrons; "covalent means non-conducting" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Share pairs; no free ions, so little current."*

### Flashcard SNT-5008 — Metallic bonding
**Front:** What is metallic bonding, and which properties does it explain?
**Definition (learn):** Metallic bonding is a lattice of positive metal ions in a sea of delocalised electrons (⚠️ owner 22, standard chemistry).
**Breakdown:**
- Delocalised electrons carry current and heat
- Layers of ions can slide, giving malleability and ductility
- Lustre from electron interaction with light
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Explains conductivity, malleability and lustre (⚠️ owner 22 Chemistry table).
**Links:** SNT-5027 (alloys), SNT-5006
**Trap:** Metals conduct through electrons, whereas ionic compounds conduct through moving ions; do not give the same explanation for both.
**Mains hook:** Metallic bonding is the physical basis for alloy design in defence and railway engineering.
**Back:** *"Ions in an electron sea: conduct, bend, shine."*

### Flashcard SNT-5009 — Hydrogen bonding
**Front:** Why does water have a high boiling point, and why does ice float?
**Definition (learn):** Hydrogen bonding is a weak individual attraction between a hydrogen atom bonded to an electronegative atom and another electronegative atom; collectively strong (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Water's high boiling point and specific heat come from hydrogen bonds
- Ice floats because the hydrogen-bonded lattice is less dense than liquid water
- The same bonding helps stabilise DNA's double helix and protein folding
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Hydrogen bonding explains water's high boiling point and specific heat, ice floating, and DNA and protein structure (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5010, SNT-5046 (water polarity), SNT-5043 (biomolecules)
**Trap:** A hydrogen bond is weaker than a covalent bond; "H-bond is a type of covalent bond" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item; supports the water-chemistry explanation in freshwater answers.
**Back:** *"Weak singly, decisive together: water, ice, DNA."*

### Flashcard SNT-5010 — Van der Waals forces and graphite
**Front:** What are van der Waals forces, and how do they explain graphite's slipperiness?
**Definition (learn):** Van der Waals forces are weak intermolecular attractions between molecules or layers (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Graphite has carbon sheets bonded strongly within layers
- Layers are held only by van der Waals forces
- Layers slide easily, giving the lubricating feel
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Van der Waals forces explain graphite's layered slipperiness (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5020, SNT-5021
**Trap:** Graphite's softness is between layers; within a layer the bonds are very strong.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Strong sheets, weak stacking: graphite slides."*

### Flashcard SNT-5011 — Acids and bases (definitions)
**Front:** Define an acid and a base in aqueous and broader senses.
**Definition (learn):** An acid produces hydrogen ions in aqueous medium or donates protons; a base produces hydroxide ions in aqueous medium or accepts protons (✅ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Aqueous (Arrhenius) view: acid → H⁺, base → OH⁻
- Broader proton view: acid donates H⁺, base accepts H⁺
- Neutralisation: acid + base → salt + water (✅ owner 22 advanced)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 gives the definitions in exam-ready form (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5012, SNT-5013, SNT-5014, SNT-5054, SNT-5055
**Trap:** Strength refers to ionisation; concentration refers to amount per volume. See SNT-5054.
**Mains hook:** Acid-base control underpins soil pH, water treatment and effluent neutralisation.
**Back:** *"Acids give H⁺; bases give OH⁻ or take H⁺."*

### Flashcard SNT-5012 — pH scale and the temperature trap
**Front:** What is pH, what does a one-unit change mean, and when is neutral exactly 7?
**Definition (learn):** pH = −log[H⁺]; it is logarithmic, so one unit equals a tenfold change in hydrogen-ion activity (✅ owner 22, ✅ owner 22 advanced).
**Breakdown:**
- Below 7 acidic, above 7 basic
- Pure water is neutral at 7 only at 25 °C
- The neutral point shifts with temperature because water's self-ionisation changes
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Neutral pH is exactly 7 only at 25 °C (✅ owner 22, Must-Know Facts). Advanced owner uses activity; concentration is a dilute-solution approximation.
**Links:** SNT-5011, SNT-5013
**Trap:** "Neutral pH is always 7" is wrong; the 25 °C qualifier is the examiner's point.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item; cite pH in soil and water-quality arguments.
**Back:** *"Log of H⁺; ten times per unit; seven only at 25 °C."*

### Flashcard SNT-5013 — Buffers and blood chemistry
**Front:** What is a buffer, and what is the standard biological example?
**Definition (learn):** A buffer resists change in pH when small amounts of acid or base are added (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Blood's bicarbonate buffer keeps pH within a narrow range
- Soil buffering capacity resists acidification
- Buffers are weak acid-base systems, not strong ones
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Blood's bicarbonate buffer; soil buffering capacity (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5012, SNT-5014
**Trap:** A buffer does not keep pH exactly constant; it limits the size of the change.
**Mains hook:** Soil buffering explains why acidic inputs damage some soils more than others.
**Back:** *"Buffers blunt pH swings; blood keeps bicarbonate."*

### Flashcard SNT-5014 — Salts: acidic, basic or neutral
**Front:** Why are some salts acidic or basic in water?
**Definition (learn):** The aqueous behaviour of a salt depends on the parent acid and parent base; not all salts are neutral (✅ owner 22, Limitation 1).
**Breakdown:**
- Strong acid + strong base → neutral salt (e.g., NaCl) (⚠️ standard chemistry)
- Strong acid + weak base → acidic salt
- Weak acid + strong base → basic salt
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** "All salts are not neutral" (✅ owner 22, pitfall 1).
**Links:** SNT-5011, SNT-5013
**Trap:** Salt means "neutral" is a common Prelims error; the parent acid and base decide.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Parents decide the salt's pH."*

### Flashcard SNT-5015 — Oxidation and reduction (OIL RIG)
**Front:** Define oxidation and reduction in the modern electron-transfer sense.
**Definition (learn):** Oxidation is loss of electrons or increase in oxidation number; reduction is gain of electrons or decrease in oxidation number. Oxidation and reduction always occur together (✅ owner 22; advanced owner adds that oxidation and reduction always occur together).
**Breakdown:**
- Older usage: oxidation as addition of oxygen; reduction as removal of oxygen or addition of hydrogen
- Modern usage: electron transfer; use this in modern questions
- A redox reaction always has an oxidant and a reductant
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Oxidation and reduction always occur together (✅ owner 22, Must-Know Facts).
**Links:** SNT-5016, SNT-5017, SNT-5057
**Trap:** "Oxidation always means gain of oxygen" is wrong; electron-loss language is safer.
**Mains hook:** Redox underpins batteries, electrolysis and green hydrogen, a chain used in SNT-5057.
**Back:** *"OIL RIG: Oxidation Is Loss, Reduction Is Gain (electrons)."*

### Flashcard SNT-5016 — Galvanic (voltaic) cell versus electrolytic cell
**Front:** How does a galvanic cell differ from an electrolytic cell?
**Definition (learn):** A galvanic cell converts chemical energy to electrical energy; an electrolytic cell uses electrical energy to drive non-spontaneous reactions (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Galvanic: batteries and fuel cells
- Electrolytic: electroplating, electrorefining, aluminium extraction, chlor-alkali
- Electrolysis can produce hydrogen, the basis of green hydrogen (✅ owner 22 advanced)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Electrolysis drives hydrogen production (✅ owner 22 advanced).
**Links:** SNT-5015, SNT-5052
**Trap:** Batteries are not always galvanic in use; a rechargeable battery charged from outside acts as an electrolytic process during charging.
**Mains hook:** The same redox logic links batteries, plating and green hydrogen (advanced owner, Section 4).
**Back:** *"Galvanic makes power; electrolytic takes power to make matter."*

### Flashcard SNT-5017 — Corrosion: electrochemical and dry routes
**Front:** How does rusting of iron occur, and what is dry corrosion?
**Definition (learn):** Aqueous corrosion of iron is electrochemical: anodic and cathodic sites on the same metal surface with an electrolyte film. Dry or chemical corrosion is direct attack by gases such as oxygen at high temperature (✅ owner 22 advanced; ⚠️ owner 22 Chemistry table).
**Breakdown:**
- Moist air provides electrolyte; iron oxidises at anodic sites
- Dry corrosion is non-electrochemical
- Prevention uses barrier coating, galvanising, alloying, cathodic protection
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Iron corrosion in moist air is electrochemical (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5015, SNT-5018, SNT-5027
**Trap:** "All corrosion is electrochemical rusting" is wrong (owner 22 trap list).
**Mains hook:** Corrosion control matters for railways, power plants and defence hardware.
**Back:** *"Wet iron rusts electrochemically; hot gas corrodes dryly."*

### Flashcard SNT-5018 — Corrosion prevention and cathodic protection
**Front:** What are the standard methods of corrosion prevention, and how does a sacrificial anode work?
**Definition (learn):** Corrosion prevention uses barrier coating, galvanising (zinc), alloying (stainless steel) and cathodic protection with a sacrificial anode (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Galvanising: zinc coating sacrifices itself
- Stainless steel: chromium and nickel alloying
- Cathodic protection: a more reactive metal corrodes instead of the protected structure
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Galvanising, alloying and cathodic protection are the named methods (✅ owner 22 advanced).
**Links:** SNT-5017, SNT-5027
**Trap:** The sacrificial anode is consumed; it must be replaced.
**Mains hook:** Infrastructure asset-life planning relies on these choices.
**Back:** *"Coat, zinc, alloy, or sacrifice."*

### Flashcard SNT-5019 — Catalyst: speeds the approach, does not shift equilibrium
**Front:** What does a catalyst do, and what examples of industrial catalysts are standard?
**Definition (learn):** A catalyst speeds a reaction by lowering activation energy and is not consumed; it does not shift the equilibrium position (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Enzymes are biological catalysts
- Iron in Haber-Bosch ammonia synthesis
- Vanadium pentoxide in the contact process for sulphuric acid
- Platinum-group metals in catalytic converters
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Haber-Bosch uses iron; contact process uses vanadium pentoxide; catalytic converters use platinum-group metals (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5001, SNT-5055
**Trap:** "A catalyst raises the yield at equilibrium" is wrong; it changes the rate of approach, not the equilibrium position.
**Mains hook:** Catalysis links fertiliser security and emission control; a catalyst raises efficiency, not the thermodynamic limit.
**Back:** *"Faster to equilibrium; equilibrium unchanged."*

### Flashcard SNT-5020 — Diamond versus graphite: same element, different structure
**Front:** Why do diamond and graphite differ although both are pure carbon?
**Definition (learn):** They are allotropes of carbon; the difference is in structure (allotropy), not element (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Diamond: tetrahedral, hardest natural substance, non-conducting
- Graphite: layered, soft, conducts electricity, used as a lubricant
- Same atoms, different bonding arrangement
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Diamond tetrahedral, hardest natural substance; graphite layered and conducts (⚠️ owner 22 table).
**Links:** SNT-5010, SNT-5021, SNT-5007
**Trap:** "Diamond and graphite contain different elements" is wrong (owner 22 trap list).
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Same carbon, different geometry: hard diamond, sliding graphite."*

### Flashcard SNT-5021 — Fullerenes, carbon nanotubes and graphene
**Front:** Define C₆₀ fullerene, carbon nanotube and graphene, and state one property of each.
**Definition (learn):** Fullerene (C₆₀) is a cage of carbon atoms; a carbon nanotube is a rolled graphene sheet; graphene is a single layer of carbon atoms in a honeycomb lattice (⚠️ owner 22 lists these; the honeycomb and rolled-sheet descriptions are ⚠️ standard chemistry).
**Breakdown:**
- C₆₀: cage (buckyball)
- Nanotube: high strength and conductivity
- Graphene: single layer with exceptional strength and conductivity
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated properties are "exceptional strength and conductivity" for graphene and nanotubes (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5020, SNT-5016, SNT-5035
**Trap:** Graphene is one layer; graphite is many stacked layers.
**Mains hook:** Nanomaterials link to semiconductors and batteries; the material is useful only when its form is matched to the application.
**Back:** *"Cage, tube, sheet: C₆₀, nanotube, graphene."*

### Flashcard SNT-5022 — Addition versus condensation polymers
**Front:** What is the difference between addition and condensation polymers, with examples?
**Definition (learn):** An addition polymer forms without elimination of a small molecule; a condensation polymer forms with elimination of a small molecule such as water or HCl (✅ owner 22 advanced).
**Breakdown:**
- Addition: polythene, PVC, polystyrene, Teflon
- Condensation: nylon, polyester (Terylene), Bakelite
- Class difference, not a difference in use alone
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Addition and condensation are not the same class (✅ owner 22, Must-Know Facts).
**Links:** SNT-5023, SNT-5024
**Trap:** "All plastics are addition polymers" is wrong; nylon and Bakelite are condensation polymers.
**Mains hook:** Condensation polymers release by-products during formation; this affects process design and waste.
**Back:** *"Add: no loss. Condense: a little water or HCl leaves."*

### Flashcard SNT-5023 — Thermoplastic versus thermoset
**Front:** How do thermoplastics and thermosets differ on heating?
**Definition (learn):** Thermoplastics soften on heating and can be reshaped and recycled; thermosets set irreversibly and cannot be remelted (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Thermoplastic: softens on heating, recyclable
- Thermoset: sets irreversibly, cannot be recycled by remelting
- Bakelite is a thermoset example
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 Must-Know Facts and advanced owner (structural difference) (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5022, SNT-5024
**Trap:** "All plastics are thermoplastics" is wrong (owner 22 trap list).
**Mains hook:** Recyclability is a material-design question linked to plastics treaty debates (SNT-5053).
**Back:** *"Thermo-plastic bends back; thermoset sets for good."*

### Flashcard SNT-5024 — Biodegradable and compostable: a claim about conditions
**Front:** Why is "biodegradable" a claim about conditions, not a licence to litter?
**Definition (learn):** Biodegradable and compostable plastics degrade under specified conditions; the claim depends on the environment and time frame (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Degradation pathway, additives and disposal conditions matter
- Industrial composting differs from home or marine conditions
- Biodegradable does not automatically mean harmless
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated principle (✅ owner 22 advanced trap).
**Links:** SNT-5022, SNT-5023, SNT-5053
**Trap:** "Biodegradable automatically means harmless" is wrong (advanced trap).
**Mains hook:** Plastic governance should regulate labels and disposal, not only material names.
**Back:** *"Degrades under conditions, not everywhere."*

### Flashcard SNT-5025 — Soaps versus synthetic detergents
**Front:** How do soaps and synthetic detergents differ in hard water?
**Definition (learn):** Both are surfactants with a hydrophilic head and hydrophobic tail forming micelles; soaps are sodium or potassium salts of long-chain fatty acids and form scum in hard water (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Soap: scum in hard water (calcium and magnesium salts)
- Synthetic detergent: works in hard and even acidic water
- Branched-chain detergents resist biodegradation, causing water-pollution history
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Branched-chain detergents were a historic pollution problem (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5024, SNT-5046 (water chemistry)
**Trap:** Both are surfactants; the difference is in head group and hard-water behaviour, not in their cleaning mechanism.
**Mains hook:** Detergent chemistry is a case of product design affecting water pollution.
**Back:** *"Soap scums in hard water; detergents keep working."*

### Flashcard SNT-5026 — Fertilisers: urea, DAP and MOP
**Front:** What nutrient does each of urea, DAP and MOP supply?
**Definition (learn):** Urea is the main nitrogen carrier; DAP supplies nitrogen and phosphorus; MOP (muriate of potash) supplies potassium (⚠️ owner 22; the owner lists potash fertilisers as potassium suppliers, ✅ owner 22 Section 5).
**Breakdown:**
- Urea: nitrogen
- DAP: nitrogen and phosphorus
- MOP: potassium
- Excess nitrogen relative to P and K causes imbalance, nitrate leaching and eutrophication
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nano urea is the input-efficiency response (⚠️ owner 22, Topic 16 link).
**Links:** SNT-5019, SNT-5058
**Trap:** Urea, DAP and potash are chemically different nutrient carriers; a "fertiliser" label does not identify the nutrient.
**Mains hook:** Nutrient imbalance and subsidy design are linked; policy should address balanced nutrient use, not just urea volume.
**Back:** *"Urea: N. DAP: N and P. MOP: K."*

### Flashcard SNT-5027 — Alloys and their constituents
**Front:** What is the composition of steel, stainless steel, brass, bronze, solder and duralumin?
**Definition (learn):** An alloy is a metal mixed with other elements to improve strength, corrosion resistance or workability (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Steel: iron + carbon
- Stainless steel: iron + carbon + chromium + nickel
- Brass: copper + zinc
- Bronze: copper + tin
- Solder: lead + tin
- Duralumin: aluminium + copper + magnesium + manganese
- Amalgam: mercury alloy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Compositions as listed in owner 22 (⚠️ owner-stated table).
**Links:** SNT-5008, SNT-5018
**Trap:** Stainless steel is not just "steel with more carbon"; its chromium and nickel give corrosion resistance.
**Mains hook:** Alloy choice links corrosion control to defence and infrastructure.
**Back:** *"Steel: Fe+C. Brass: Cu+Zn. Bronze: Cu+Sn."*

### Flashcard SNT-5028 — Stratospheric versus tropospheric ozone
**Front:** Why is ozone protective in the stratosphere but harmful at ground level?
**Definition (learn):** Stratospheric ozone (O₃) absorbs UV-B and shields life; ground-level (tropospheric) ozone is a secondary pollutant formed photochemically from NOx and VOCs (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Stratosphere: UV-B absorption
- Troposphere: photochemical smog pollutant
- Same molecule, opposite policy meaning
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** UV-B absorption and photochemical formation from NOx and VOCs (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5029, SNT-5030
**Trap:** "Ozone is always beneficial" is wrong (owner 22 trap list).
**Mains hook:** Air-quality policy must separate ozone layer protection from urban ozone control.
**Back:** *"Up high, ozone shields; down low, ozone harms."*

### Flashcard SNT-5029 — CFCs, halons, Montreal Protocol and Kigali Amendment
**Front:** How do CFCs and halons destroy stratospheric ozone, and which instruments govern them?
**Definition (learn):** CFCs and halons release chlorine or bromine radicals that destroy ozone catalytically; the Montreal Protocol governs ozone-depleting substances, and the Kigali Amendment extends the framework to HFCs (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Catalytic cycle: one chlorine radical destroys many ozone molecules
- Montreal Protocol: ozone-depleting substances
- Kigali Amendment: hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs), a greenhouse-gas concern
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Montreal Protocol and Kigali Amendment named in owner 22; the Kigali year is not stated in the owner, so no year is given (⚠️).
**Links:** SNT-5028, SNT-5030
**Trap:** The Kigali Amendment is about HFCs and climate, not about CFC-driven ozone depletion as such.
**Mains hook:** The Montreal model shows that a chemical substitution treaty can work; its lesson is relevant to climate-linked refrigerant policy.
**Back:** *"Chlorine eats ozone; Montreal bans; Kigali chases HFCs."*

### Flashcard SNT-5030 — Greenhouse gases and global warming potential
**Front:** Which gases are the principal greenhouse gases, and how are they compared?
**Definition (learn):** The principal greenhouse gases are CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, water vapour and fluorinated gases; they differ in global warming potential (GWP) and atmospheric lifetime (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- CO₂: long-lived reference gas
- CH₄ and N₂O: potent, different lifetimes
- Fluorinated gases: very high GWP
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-listed gases and GWP concept (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5028, SNT-5029
**Trap:** A gas with a small mass in the air can still have a large warming effect; compare GWP, not concentration alone.
**Mains hook:** Use GWP and lifetime to justify targeted mitigation rather than one-gas policies.
**Back:** *"CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, water, fluorinated: compare by GWP."*

### Flashcard SNT-5031 — Chlorination for water disinfection
**Front:** What are the advantages and drawbacks of chlorination for drinking water?
**Definition (learn):** Chlorination disinfects water with chlorine compounds and leaves a residual that protects water in distribution (⚠️ owner 22, 2024 freshwater evaluation).
**Breakdown:**
- Advantage: cheap, residual protection
- Drawback: disinfection by-products
- Evaluate with energy, cost, capacity, brine/sludge and operator skill
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated trade-offs (⚠️ owner 22 Chemistry table).
**Links:** SNT-5032, SNT-5033, SNT-5058
**Trap:** Chlorination is not free of risk; the by-product issue is the usual examiner point.
**Mains hook:** Use chlorination as the cheap default and UV or membranes as the alternative where by-products or turbidity matter.
**Back:** *"Cheap and lasting, but by-products remain."*

### Flashcard SNT-5032 — UV disinfection
**Front:** What are the strengths and limitations of UV disinfection?
**Definition (learn):** UV disinfection inactivates microbes with ultraviolet light without adding chemicals or residual (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- No chemicals and no residual protection
- Ineffective in turbid water because particles shield microbes
- Works best as a polishing step after filtration
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated limitation (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5031, SNT-5033
**Trap:** UV is not a chemical-free "complete" solution in all water; turbidity limits it.
**Mains hook:** Choose UV for chemical-free disinfection with clear water; pair with filtration where water is turbid.
**Back:** *"No chemical, no residual, clear water only."*

### Flashcard SNT-5033 — Reverse osmosis
**Front:** What does reverse osmosis remove, and what are its costs?
**Definition (learn):** Reverse osmosis forces water through a selective membrane against osmotic pressure, removing dissolved salts (⚠️ owner 22; advanced owner gives the pressure-across-membrane mechanism).
**Breakdown:**
- Removes dissolved salts
- Energy intensive
- Produces brine (reject water) and strips beneficial minerals
- Needs pretreatment
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated trade-offs (⚠️ owner 22; advanced owner, Section 4 Evidence units).
**Links:** SNT-5034, SNT-5035, SNT-5056, SNT-5058
**Trap:** "Reverse osmosis is the best water technology" is wrong; choose by contaminant, scale and energy (SNT-5056).
**Mains hook:** RO suits brackish or saline feedwater where energy and brine disposal are manageable.
**Back:** *"Pressure through membrane: salt out, brine and energy in."*

### Flashcard SNT-5034 — Thermal and multi-stage flash desalination
**Front:** How does thermal desalination work, and where is it suited?
**Definition (learn):** Thermal desalination uses phase change: water is evaporated and condensed, leaving salts behind; multi-stage flash (MSF) is a staged version (⚠️ owner 22; advanced owner, Section 4).
**Breakdown:**
- Robust for seawater
- Very energy intensive
- Phase change separates salts
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5033, SNT-5036, SNT-5058
**Trap:** Thermal distillation is not a membrane process; its energy use is the constraint, not its robustness.
**Mains hook:** Suits high-energy coastal sites with waste heat; otherwise energy cost limits it.
**Back:** *"Boil, condense, leave salt: robust, energy hungry."*

### Flashcard SNT-5035 — Electrodialysis
**Front:** What does electrodialysis remove, and in which water is it suited?
**Definition (learn):** Electrodialysis uses an electric field to move ions across selective membranes, removing ions from water (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Ion removal driven by electric field
- Suited to brackish water
- Ion-selective membranes separate concentrate
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5033, SNT-5034
**Trap:** It removes ions, not microbes; disinfection is a separate step.
**Mains hook:** Brackish-water schemes trade electric energy for ion removal; compare with RO.
**Back:** *"Electric field pulls ions; brackish water fits."*

### Flashcard SNT-5036 — Solar stills and atmospheric water generators
**Front:** What are solar stills and atmospheric water generators best suited for?
**Definition (learn):** Solar stills use solar heat to distil water; atmospheric water generators extract water vapour from air (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Off-grid applications
- Low output
- Useful at household or remote scale
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5034, SNT-5058
**Trap:** Low output means they are not a city-scale water supply.
**Mains hook:** Off-grid decentralised water supply is the right use case.
**Back:** *"Sun or air, off-grid, small output."*

### Flashcard SNT-5037 — From ore to metal: concentration, reduction, refining
**Front:** What are the steps from ore to a refined metal, and how does metal reactivity choose the route?
**Definition (learn):** Ore is concentrated to remove gangue; converted if needed; the compound is reduced chemically, electrolytically or thermally; the crude metal is refined (✅ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Concentration removes gangue
- Reduction route depends on reactivity
- Highly reactive metals: electrolysis
- Less reactive metals: carbon reduction
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 mechanism steps (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5027, SNT-5015
**Trap:** "All metals can be extracted by carbon reduction" is wrong (owner 22 trap list).
**Mains hook:** Critical-mineral strategy depends on the extraction route, not just ore availability.
**Back:** *"Concentrate, reduce, refine; reactivity picks the route."*

### Flashcard SNT-5038 — Cobalt-60 (Co-60)
**Front:** What is Co-60 used for?
**Definition (learn):** Co-60 is a radioisotope used in radiotherapy and food irradiation (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Radiotherapy
- Food irradiation
- Radiation dose, half-life and shielding govern use
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated applications (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5003, SNT-5039, SNT-5040
**Trap:** Co-60 is not a dating isotope; that role belongs to C-14.
**Mains hook:** Radiation safety and food-irradiation policy are linked to the same physics.
**Back:** *"Co-60 treats cancer and sterilises food."*

### Flashcard SNT-5039 — Iodine-131 (I-131)
**Front:** What is I-131 used for?
**Definition (learn):** I-131 is a radioisotope used in thyroid diagnosis and therapy (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Thyroid diagnosis and therapy
- Thyroid takes up iodine, so I-131 concentrates there
- Links to iodine deficiency goitre in SNT-5061
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5038, SNT-5061
**Trap:** I-131 is a radioactive iodine, not a dietary iodine supplement.
**Mains hook:** Diagnostic isotopes depend on half-life and dose control.
**Back:** *"I-131 finds the thyroid."*

### Flashcard SNT-5040 — Carbon-14 (C-14) dating
**Front:** What is C-14 used for?
**Definition (learn):** C-14 is a radioactive carbon isotope used in dating organic material (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Radiocarbon dating of organic remains
- Relies on isotope half-life
- Isotope of C-12 (same element, different A)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5003, SNT-5038
**Trap:** C-14 is an isotope of carbon; it is not a separate element.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item; supports nuclear-dating discussions.
**Back:** *"C-14 dates the once-living."*

### Flashcard SNT-5041 — Technetium-99m (Tc-99m) and phosphorus-32 (P-32)
**Front:** What are Tc-99m and P-32 used for?
**Definition (learn):** Tc-99m is used in medical imaging; P-32 is used in agricultural research (⚠️ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Tc-99m: imaging
- P-32: agricultural research tracer
- Both are radioisotope applications
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated uses (⚠️ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5038, SNT-5040
**Trap:** Tc-99m and P-32 are different uses; do not match P-32 to imaging.
**Mains hook:** Radioisotope applications span medicine and agriculture.
**Back:** *"Tc images; P tracks in fields."*

### Flashcard SNT-5042 — Biomolecule classes: four distinct families
**Front:** What are the four biomolecule classes, and why must they not be merged?
**Definition (learn):** Carbohydrates, proteins, lipids and nucleic acids are distinct biomolecule classes; DNA and RNA are nucleic acids and proteins are polymers of amino acids (✅ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Carbohydrates: sugars and starches (energy)
- Proteins: amino-acid polymers (structure, catalysis, signalling)
- Lipids: fats and oils
- Nucleic acids: DNA and RNA
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 Must-Know Facts (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5009, SNT-5043
**Trap:** "DNA, RNA and proteins are the same kind of biomolecule" is wrong (owner 22 trap list).
**Mains hook:** Biochemistry frames biotech and diagnostics; the class determines the tool.
**Back:** *"Sugars, proteins, fats, nucleic acids: four families."*

### Flashcard SNT-5043 — Biomolecule chemistry and the 2024 Nobel link
**Front:** Why is protein chemistry counted as chemistry as well as biology?
**Definition (learn):** Protein structure and design are chemistry questions; the 2024 Nobel Prize in Chemistry recognised computational protein design and protein-structure prediction (✅ owner 22; the names of the laureates are ⚠️ owner-unnamed, see SNT-5243).
**Breakdown:**
- Structure is determined by bonding and folding
- Design and prediction are computational chemistry
- Applications: medicine, sensors, enzymes
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 09 Oct 2024, Nobel Prize in Chemistry (📰 owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5042, SNT-5243
**Trap:** "Nobel Chemistry is medicine" is wrong; the category depends on the chemistry of the cited work.
**Mains hook:** Biomolecule chemistry shows that the boundary between chemistry and life science is porous.
**Back:** *"Protein design won Chemistry, not Medicine."*

### Flashcard SNT-5044 — Hydrogel (PYQ 2024 Prelims GS-I Q36)
**Front:** What is a hydrogel, and what are its uses? (PYQ: "Uses of hydrogels", 2024 Prelims GS-I Q36)
**Definition (learn):** A hydrogel is a cross-linked hydrophilic polymer network that absorbs and retains water (✅ owner 22 routed evidence).
**Breakdown:**
- Applications: dressings, contact lenses, controlled release, water management
- Not a liquid and not a generic water purifier
- Official key available locally; the option letter is not recorded here (owner rule)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 Prelims GS-I Q36 (✅ owner 22 PYQ ledger).
**Links:** SNT-5022, SNT-5046
**Trap:** A hydrogel retains water in a network; it is not simply a water filter.
**Mains hook:** Water-management use cases reveal polymer chemistry in agriculture.
**Back:** *"Cross-linked, water-holding polymer: dressings to crops."*

### Flashcard SNT-5045 — Coal gasification (PYQ 2025 Prelims GS-I Q45)
**Front:** What does coal gasification produce? (PYQ: 2025 Prelims GS-I Q45)
**Definition (learn):** Controlled conversion of coal with oxygen or steam produces synthesis gas chiefly containing carbon monoxide and hydrogen (✅ owner 22 routed evidence).
**Breakdown:**
- Not combustion; the process produces syngas
- Other gases and by-products depend on process
- Climate and air-pollution impact depend on capture, efficiency and downstream use
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 Prelims GS-I Q45; official key available locally, option not recorded here (owner rule).
**Links:** SNT-5044, SNT-5030
**Trap:** Coal gasification is not coal burning; the product is syngas.
**Mains hook:** Syngas can power or feed chemicals; the climate case depends on capture and efficiency.
**Back:** *"Coal plus oxygen or steam: syngas, not smoke."*

### Flashcard SNT-5046 — Water's polarity as universal solvent (PYQ 2021 Q71)
**Front:** Why is water a near-universal solvent, and what limit does the PYQ test? (PYQ: "Water dipolar nature as universal solvent property", 2021 Prelims GS-I Q71)
**Definition (learn):** Water's polarity (dipolar nature) dissolves many ionic and polar substances, but not every substance (✅ owner 22 routed evidence).
**Breakdown:**
- Polarity supports solvation of ions and polar molecules
- Non-polar substances dissolve poorly
- "Universal" is a qualification, not a fact
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 Prelims GS-I Q71; key unavailable locally (owner rule).
**Links:** SNT-5009, SNT-5047
**Trap:** Water does not dissolve everything; the word "universal" is the trap.
**Mains hook:** Water chemistry explains why contaminants move and why treatment targets specific solutes.
**Back:** *"Polar water dissolves polar things, not everything."*

### Flashcard SNT-5047 — Bisphenol A and polycarbonate (PYQ 2021 Q74)
**Front:** What is BPA and what is it used for? (PYQ: "Bisphenol A BPA polycarbonate plastics manufacturing", 2021 Q74)
**Definition (learn):** Bisphenol A is a monomer used in some polycarbonate and epoxy applications (✅ owner 22 routed evidence).
**Breakdown:**
- Monomer, not a finished plastic
- Associated with polycarbonate and epoxy uses
- Exposure and regulation are product- and context-specific
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 Prelims GS-I Q74; key unavailable locally (owner rule).
**Links:** SNT-5022, SNT-5048
**Trap:** Do not claim a universal product list or safety outcome without a source (owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Chemicals of concern in plastics are a treaty-level issue (SNT-5053).
**Back:** *"BPA: a monomer behind polycarbonate and epoxy."*

### Flashcard SNT-5048 — Triclosan in personal-care products (PYQ 2021 Q75)
**Front:** What is triclosan? (PYQ: "Triclosan presence in consumer personal care products", 2021 Q75)
**Definition (learn):** Triclosan is a synthetic antimicrobial used in some personal-care products (✅ owner 22 routed evidence).
**Breakdown:**
- Antimicrobial additive
- Presence depends on product and regulation
- Exposure is context specific
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 Prelims GS-I Q75; key unavailable locally (owner rule).
**Links:** SNT-5047, SNT-5024
**Trap:** Do not state that every personal-care product contains triclosan.
**Mains hook:** Antimicrobial additives link to antimicrobial-resistance concerns.
**Back:** *"Triclosan: synthetic antimicrobial in some personal-care products."*

### Flashcard SNT-5049 — CSIR-National Chemical Laboratory (NCL)
**Front:** What does CSIR-NCL do?
**Definition (learn):** CSIR-NCL is a major chemistry and materials research institution (✅ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Chemistry, catalysis, materials and molecular research
- Part of CSIR, an autonomous society under DSIR (see SNT-5139)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5050, SNT-5139
**Trap:** NCL is a research laboratory, not a funding council.
**Mains hook:** Institutions like NCL turn chemistry into industrial processes.
**Back:** *"NCL: chemistry and materials lab."*

### Flashcard SNT-5050 — CSIR-CECRI (Central Electrochemical Research Institute)
**Front:** What does CSIR-CECRI specialise in?
**Definition (learn):** CECRI works on batteries, corrosion, electrochemistry and related industrial chemistry (✅ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Batteries and electrochemistry
- Corrosion control
- Translational electrochemical technology
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5015, SNT-5017, SNT-5049
**Trap:** CECRI is an electrochemistry lab; do not confuse it with the electronics lab CEERI.
**Mains hook:** Battery and corrosion research link to EV and infrastructure goals.
**Back:** *"CECRI: batteries, corrosion, electrochemistry."*

### Flashcard SNT-5051 — CSIR-National Metallurgical Laboratory (NML) and IMMT ecosystem
**Front:** What does the NML/IMMT ecosystem do?
**Definition (learn):** NML and the IMMT ecosystem cover metallurgy and extraction-linked applied science (✅ owner 22).
**Breakdown:**
- Extraction and metallurgy
- Applied science for industry
- Links to critical-mineral strategy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-stated (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5037, SNT-5027
**Trap:** NML is a CSIR lab; the IMMT is a separate institute in the ecosystem, not a CSIR lab (⚠️ owner names it as an ecosystem).
**Mains hook:** Metallurgical capacity supports strategic manufacturing.
**Back:** *"NML: metals and extraction."*

### Flashcard SNT-5052 — National Green Hydrogen Mission (chemistry bridge)
**Front:** How does redox chemistry connect to the National Green Hydrogen Mission?
**Definition (learn):** The mission is a national policy bridge from electrolysis and electrochemistry to industrial decarbonisation (✅ owner 22; MNRE page as of 16 Jul 2026 per owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Electrolysis-linked policy support
- Standards, regulations and R&D (SHIP framework)
- Redox to decarbonisation at national scale
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** MNRE page as of 16 Jul 2026 (📰 owner 22; time-varying, verify later).
**Links:** SNT-5016, SNT-5057
**Trap:** Green hydrogen is not only an energy-policy topic; it is also electrochemistry, catalysis and standards.
**Mains hook:** Green hydrogen policy depends on electrolyser efficiency and standards.
**Back:** *"Electrolysis is the bridge to green hydrogen."*

### Flashcard SNT-5053 — UNEP plastics treaty process
**Front:** What are the UNEP plastics-treaty sessions named in the owner, and what do they cover?
**Definition (learn):** INC-5.1 (25 Nov to 1 Dec 2024) and INC-5.2 (5 to 14 Aug 2025) form the plastics-treaty process; the agenda covers polymer lifecycle, recyclability and chemicals of concern (📰 owner 22 and advanced owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Dates are owner-stated
- Focus: lifecycle, recyclability, chemicals of concern
- Connects polymer chemistry to governance
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 Nov to 1 Dec 2024 (INC-5.1); 5 to 14 Aug 2025 (INC-5.2) (📰 owner 22; status as of 16 Jul 2026 per owner).
**Links:** SNT-5022, SNT-5023, SNT-5024
**Trap:** Treaty negotiation sessions are not a ratified treaty; do not describe the treaty as adopted without a source.
**Mains hook:** Plastic governance needs chemistry of additives and recyclability, not only bans.
**Back:** *"INC-5.1 and 5.2: polymer lifecycle on the table."*

### Flashcard SNT-5054 — Trap pair: strong acid versus concentrated acid
**Front:** Does "strong acid" mean "concentrated acid"?
**Definition (learn):** No. Strength refers to the degree of ionisation; concentration refers to the amount of solute per volume (✅ owner 22 trap list).
**Breakdown:**
- Strong: nearly complete ionisation
- Concentrated: high solute per volume
- A dilute strong acid is still strong
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 trap (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5011, SNT-5012
**Trap:** Examiners use dilute strong acid versus concentrated weak acid to test this distinction.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Strong is ionisation; concentrated is amount."*

### Flashcard SNT-5055 — Trap pair: all bases are alkalis
**Front:** Are all bases alkalis?
**Definition (learn):** No. Only water-soluble bases are alkalis (✅ owner 22 trap list).
**Breakdown:**
- Alkali: soluble base
- Insoluble bases exist (e.g., some metal hydroxides)
- Base is the broader category
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 trap (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5011, SNT-5019
**Trap:** An insoluble base is still a base even though it is not an alkali.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Alkali is soluble base; base is the family."*

### Flashcard SNT-5056 — Trap pair: reverse osmosis is not simply the best technology
**Front:** Why is "reverse osmosis is the best water technology" wrong?
**Definition (learn):** RO removes dissolved salts but is energy intensive, wastes reject water, produces brine and strips beneficial minerals; the right technology depends on contaminant, scale and energy cost (✅ owner 22 trap list).
**Breakdown:**
- Contaminant: salts versus microbes versus fluoride
- Scale: household versus city
- Energy and brine disposal
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 22 trap and PYQ route (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** SNT-5033, SNT-5058
**Trap:** The 2024 freshwater question rewards a trade-off table, not a single "best" technology.
**Mains hook:** Freshwater answers must weigh energy, brine and cost.
**Back:** *"RO fits some waters, not all."*

### Flashcard SNT-5057 — Mains concept chain: redox to green hydrogen and fertiliser
**Front:** How should a 10-mark chemistry answer be structured?
**Definition (learn):** Identify the chemical principle; trace the process in two or three stages; give one application; name the material, energy or disposal limit (✅ owner 22 core architecture).
**Breakdown:**
- Principle: redox, acid-base, bonding
- Process: two or three stages
- Application: fertiliser, hydrogen, plastics
- Limit: energy, cost or waste
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10-mark spine and 15/20-mark spine in owner 22.
**Links:** SNT-5015, SNT-5052, SNT-5001
**Trap:** Do not balance equations unless directly useful; the answer is conceptual.
**Mains hook:** Use the spine: principle, process, application, limit, verdict.
**Back:** *"Principle, process, application, limit."*

### Flashcard SNT-5058 — Freshwater technology evaluation matrix (PYQ route)
**Front:** Which five criteria should you use to compare freshwater technologies?
**Definition (learn):** Every technology must be evaluated on energy, cost, capacity, brine or sludge disposal and operator skill (✅ owner 22 PYQ application; 2024 GS-III PYQ on freshwater technology).
**Breakdown:**
- Energy use
- Cost
- Capacity and scale
- Brine or sludge disposal
- Operator skill
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-III freshwater PYQ (✅ owner 22; the 250-word full answer is owned by Environment).
**Links:** SNT-5031 to SNT-5036, SNT-5056
**Trap:** Naming one technology and calling it "best" loses marks.
**Mains hook:** Compare chlorination, UV, RO, thermal, electrodialysis and solar stills on the same five criteria.
**Back:** *"Energy, cost, capacity, brine, operator."*

---

### GROUP — 23 General Science: Biology and Physiology (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard SNT-5059 — Biology chain: gene to physiology to health
**Front:** What is the logical chain linking genes, physiology and public health?
**Definition (learn):** DNA stores heritable information; RNA transcribes and regulates it; proteins perform functions; cells form tissues, organs and systems; the system outcome is health (✅ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Information: DNA → RNA → protein
- Regulation: hormones, nerves and RNAs decide what is expressed
- System: cell → tissue → organ → body → population health
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 chain and advanced owner regulation layer (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** SNT-5066, SNT-5078, SNT-5108, SNT-5112
**Trap:** Having DNA does not mean a cell expresses all genes; regulation creates cell types.
**Mains hook:** Biology supports health policy only when the mechanism is traced across levels, not a named organ alone.
**Back:** *"Gene, RNA, protein, cell, system, health."*

### Flashcard SNT-5060 — Prokaryotic versus eukaryotic cells
**Front:** How do prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells differ?
**Definition (learn):** Prokaryotes lack a true membrane-bound nucleus and membrane-bound organelles (bacteria); eukaryotes possess a nucleus (✅ owner 23; ⚠️ owner 23 structure table).
**Breakdown:**
- Prokaryote: bacteria, no true nucleus
- Eukaryote: plants, animals, fungi, protists
- Eukaryotes have organelles such as mitochondria and ER
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Prokaryotic cells lack a true membrane-bound nucleus (✅ owner 23, Must-Know Facts).
**Links:** SNT-5062, SNT-5063, SNT-5134
**Trap:** Fungi are eukaryotes; they are neither plants nor bacteria (✅ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** Antibiotics target prokaryotic structures, which is why they differ from antiviral action (SNT-5101).
**Back:** *"No nucleus, prokaryote; nucleus, eukaryote."*

### Flashcard SNT-5061 — Iodine deficiency: goitre and cretinism
**Front:** Which deficiency causes goitre, and which condition does it also link to?
**Definition (learn):** Iodine deficiency impairs thyroxine production; the thyroid enlarges as goitre; the owner also names cretinism (⚠️ owner 23 nutrition row).
**Breakdown:**
- Iodine → thyroxine (thyroid hormone)
- Low thyroxine → enlarged thyroid (goitre)
- Severe deficiency in children → cretinism
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Iodine deficiency → goitre and cretinism (⚠️ owner 23 table).
**Links:** SNT-5078, SNT-5039
**Trap:** Iodine deficiency is not the same as iron deficiency; each produces a different disorder.
**Mains hook:** Iodised salt programmes address a micronutrient deficiency that is preventable.
**Back:** *"No iodine, no thyroxine: goitre."*

### Flashcard SNT-5062 — Cell organelles and their functions
**Front:** Which organelle does what: nucleus, ribosome, rough and smooth ER, Golgi, lysosome?
**Definition (learn):** Nucleus: genetic control; ribosome: protein synthesis; rough and smooth ER: protein and lipid processing; Golgi: packaging and secretion; lysosome: hydrolytic enzymes, the "suicide bag" (⚠️ owner 23 structure table).
**Breakdown:**
- Nucleus: genetic control
- Ribosome: protein synthesis
- ER: processing
- Golgi: packaging and secretion
- Lysosome: digestion of worn-out parts
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Functions as listed in owner 23 (⚠️ owner-stated table).
**Links:** SNT-5060, SNT-5063
**Trap:** Ribosomes are present in prokaryotes too; they are not unique to eukaryotes.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Nucleus commands, ribosome builds, ER folds, Golgi ships, lysosome cleans."*

### Flashcard SNT-5063 — Mitochondria and chloroplasts
**Front:** Why are mitochondria linked to respiration and chloroplasts to photosynthesis?
**Definition (learn):** Mitochondria are the sites of aerobic respiration and ATP generation; chloroplasts host photosynthesis (✅ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Mitochondrion: ATP from food
- Chloroplast: light energy to chemical energy
- Chloroplasts are found in plants only
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mitochondria with ATP; chloroplasts with photosynthesis (✅ owner 23, Must-Know Facts).
**Links:** SNT-5062, SNT-5105
**Trap:** Plants respire too; mitochondria are present in plant cells as well (SNT-5134).
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Mitochondria burn for ATP; chloroplasts capture light."*

### Flashcard SNT-5064 — Membrane transport: diffusion, osmosis, active transport
**Front:** What is the difference between diffusion, osmosis and active transport?
**Definition (learn):** Diffusion moves substances down a gradient; osmosis moves water across a semipermeable membrane; active transport moves substances against a gradient using energy (⚠️ owner 23 structure table).
**Breakdown:**
- Diffusion: passive, down gradient
- Osmosis: water only
- Active transport: energy use, against gradient
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Transport modes as listed (⚠️ owner 23).
**Links:** SNT-5075, SNT-5062
**Trap:** Osmosis is a special case of diffusion for water, not a separate pump.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Diffuse down, osmose water, pump up."*

### Flashcard SNT-5065 — Mitosis versus meiosis
**Front:** How do mitosis and meiosis differ in outcome and purpose?
**Definition (learn):** Mitosis: one division producing two genetically identical diploid cells for growth and repair; meiosis: two divisions producing four genetically varied haploid gametes, with crossing over (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Mitosis: growth, repair; identical daughters
- Meiosis: gametes; variation through crossing over
- Haploid = half chromosome set
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 table (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5066, SNT-5068
**Trap:** Confusing the two is a standard error; meiosis is not for growth.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Mitosis copies; meiosis halves and shuffles."*

### Flashcard SNT-5066 — Central dogma: replication, transcription and translation
**Front:** What are replication, transcription and translation, and where does each happen?
**Definition (learn):** Replication copies DNA semi-conservatively; transcription makes mRNA from DNA; translation at the ribosome uses tRNA and the triplet genetic code to build protein (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Replication: DNA copies itself, semi-conservative
- Transcription: DNA → mRNA
- Translation: mRNA → protein at ribosome, using codons
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 table (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5059, SNT-5067, SNT-5130
**Trap:** Transcription is not translation; mRNA is the message, protein is the product.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Copy DNA, write mRNA, read codons to protein."*

### Flashcard SNT-5067 — Sickle-cell anaemia and thalassaemia
**Front:** What are sickle-cell anaemia and thalassaemia, and why are they Indian examples?
**Definition (learn):** Both are inherited blood disorders arising from gene mutations that alter haemoglobin production or structure (⚠️ owner 23 names them as standard Indian examples).
**Breakdown:**
- Mutation is the raw material of variation
- Sickle-cell: abnormal haemoglobin shape
- Thalassaemia: reduced haemoglobin production
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Named as standard examples in owner 23 (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5066, SNT-5070
**Trap:** Mutations are not always harmful; some are neutral or beneficial.
**Mains hook:** Genetic screening for blood disorders links basic genetics to public health.
**Back:** *"Mutated haemoglobin: sickle, thalassaemia."*

### Flashcard SNT-5068 — Mendelian laws and monohybrid and dihybrid ratios
**Front:** Which laws and ratios define Mendelian inheritance?
**Definition (learn):** Mendel's laws are dominance, segregation and independent assortment; monohybrid gives 3:1 and dihybrid gives 9:3:3:1 (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Dominance: one allele masks another
- Segregation: alleles separate in gametes
- Independent assortment: genes sort independently
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ratios as stated in owner 23 (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5065, SNT-5069
**Trap:** Independent assortment applies to genes on different chromosomes or far apart; linked genes do not assort independently.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Dominate, segregate, assort; 3:1 and 9:3:3:1."*

### Flashcard SNT-5069 — Sex determination and X-linked conditions
**Front:** How is human sex determined, and why are haemophilia and colour blindness commoner in males?
**Definition (learn):** Human sex is XX/XY: the sperm determines sex; haemophilia and red-green colour blindness are X-linked recessive conditions (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Female XX, male XY
- Sperm determines sex
- X-linked recessive: males need one copy only to show the trait
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 table (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5068
**Trap:** The mother does not determine the sex of the child.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Sperm decides; X-linked hits males harder."*

### Flashcard SNT-5070 — ABO blood groups and universal donor
**Front:** Which blood groups are universal donors and recipients, and why?
**Definition (learn):** AB is the universal recipient; O is the universal donor (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- A, B, AB and O groups
- O: no A or B antigen on RBCs, so compatible donor
- AB: no antibodies against A or B, so compatible recipient
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 table (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5071, SNT-5072
**Trap:** Universal donor and recipient are opposite roles; O is donor, AB is recipient.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"O gives, AB receives."*

### Flashcard SNT-5071 — Rh factor and erythroblastosis foetalis
**Front:** Why does an Rh-negative mother carrying an Rh-positive foetus face risk?
**Definition (learn):** Rh-negative mother exposed to Rh-positive foetal blood can form antibodies, which can attack the foetus's red cells in a later pregnancy, causing erythroblastosis foetalis (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Rh-negative mother, Rh-positive foetus
- Sensitisation at birth
- Risk rises in later pregnancies
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Named in owner 23 (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5070
**Trap:** The risk is not in the first pregnancy by default; sensitisation is the mechanism.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Rh-negative mother, Rh-positive child: antibody risk."*

### Flashcard SNT-5072 — Blood components
**Front:** What do plasma, red cells, white cells and platelets do?
**Definition (learn):** Plasma carries dissolved substances and proteins; RBCs carry oxygen by haemoglobin (no nucleus in humans); WBCs defend; platelets clot (✅ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Plasma: transport medium
- RBC: haemoglobin, no nucleus in humans
- WBC: immunity
- Platelet: clotting
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 Must-Know Facts (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** SNT-5070, SNT-5074
**Trap:** Haemoglobin carries oxygen; plasma carries dissolved substances.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Plasma carries, red carries O2, white defends, platelets plug."*

### Flashcard SNT-5073 — Circulatory system and double circulation
**Front:** What is double circulation and what is the pulmonary artery's exception?
**Definition (learn):** The four-chambered heart pumps blood through two circuits: pulmonary (to lungs) and systemic (to body); arteries carry blood away from the heart (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Four chambers
- Double circulation
- Pulmonary artery carries deoxygenated blood
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 table (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5072, SNT-5074
**Trap:** Arteries are defined by direction of flow away from the heart, not by oxygen content; the pulmonary artery is the deoxygenated exception.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Two circuits; arteries leave, pulmonary artery is the exception."*

### Flashcard SNT-5074 — Haemoglobin and carbon monoxide poisoning
**Front:** Why is carbon monoxide poisoning lethal?
**Definition (learn):** Haemoglobin binds carbon monoxide far more strongly than oxygen, blocking oxygen transport (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- O2 binding is weaker
- CO occupies binding sites
- Result: tissue hypoxia
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 table (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5072, SNT-5073
**Trap:** Carbon monoxide does not need to be "toxic" in the usual sense; the issue is haemoglobin affinity.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"CO grips haemoglobin harder than O2."*

### Flashcard SNT-5075 — Nephron and dialysis
**Front:** What does the nephron do, and what does dialysis substitute for?
**Definition (learn):** The nephron filters blood (glomerulus), reabsorbs useful substances and secretes waste; dialysis substitutes for failed kidneys (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Filtration at glomerulus
- Reabsorption and secretion along tubules
- Dialysis replaces filtration function
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 table (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5064
**Trap:** Dialysis is a substitute, not a cure for kidney failure.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Filter, reabsorb, secrete; dialysis stands in."*

### Flashcard SNT-5076 — Nervous system: neurons, medulla and reflex arcs
**Front:** How does the nervous system transmit signals, and what does the medulla control?
**Definition (learn):** Neurons transmit electrical impulses and use neurotransmitters at synapses; the medulla controls involuntary functions; reflex arcs bypass the brain (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Central and peripheral nervous system
- Medulla: involuntary control
- Reflex arc: rapid automatic response
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 23 table (⚠️ owner-stated).
**Links:** SNT-5112, SNT-5078
**Trap:** A reflex does not require the brain to decide; the arc works through the spinal cord.
**Mains hook:** Not a standalone Mains item.
**Back:** *"Impulse runs; medulla auto-pilots; reflex skips the brain."*

### Flashcard SNT-5077 — Digestive enzymes and pH
**Front:** At what pH do the main digestive enzymes act, and what does bile do?
**Definition (learn):** Salivary amylase acts in mouth; pepsin acts in acid stomach; pancreatic and intestinal enzymes act in alkaline medium; bile emulsifies fat (⚠️ owner 23).
**Breakdown:**
- Salivary amylase: carbohydrate, mouth
- Pepsin: protein, acid stomach
